# Corporate & Other Laws — HARD Reasoning-First Q&A (Q1–Q100)

*100 of the toughest CA-Intermediate questions — multi-step problems with twists, integrated cross-concept problems, and "analyse / advise / examine validity" case-style questions. Every answer carries a **"Why this way (the reasoning)"** block that explains the principle behind each step (and why the tempting wrong approach fails), so you learn to think, not memorise. Full chapter coverage, ICAI-depth working notes and statements.*

## Part C — HARD Reasoning-First Bank (Q1–Q100)

### Q1. Ch: Preliminary & Definitions — Small Company Test with Holding/Subsidiary Trap (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** ABC Pvt. Ltd. was incorporated on 1 April 2024. For the financial year ended 31 March 2025 its paid-up share capital was ₹3.20 crore and its turnover (as per the last profit and loss account) was ₹18 crore. ABC Pvt. Ltd. holds 80% of the equity of DEF Pvt. Ltd., whose paid-up capital is ₹40 lakh and turnover is ₹2 crore. The management of DEF Pvt. Ltd. claims that DEF is a "small company" and is therefore entitled to the relaxations available to small companies (fewer board meetings, no cash-flow statement, abridged annual return, lower penalties). Examine the validity of this claim. Would your answer change if DEF were a public company with the same figures?

**Answer:**
**Governing provision — Section 2(85), Companies Act, 2013 (read with Companies (Specification of Definitions Details) Rules).** A small company is a company, **other than a public company**, whose (i) paid-up capital does not exceed the prescribed limit (₹4 crore) **and** (ii) turnover does not exceed the prescribed limit (₹40 crore). Both limits are conjunctive — the "and" means *both* must be satisfied.

**The four disqualifying carve-outs in the proviso to Section 2(85):** A company can NEVER be a small company, regardless of its size, if it is —
(a) a **holding company or a subsidiary company**;
(b) a company registered under **Section 8** (charitable);
(c) a company or body corporate **governed by any special Act**.

**Application to DEF Pvt. Ltd.:** On its own numbers DEF is tiny — capital ₹40 lakh (< ₹4 crore) and turnover ₹2 crore (< ₹40 crore) — so it clears the *size* test comfortably. **But** ABC holds 80% of DEF's equity, which exceeds one-half of the total voting power; therefore under Section 2(87) DEF is a **subsidiary** of ABC. Carve-out (a) squarely applies: **a subsidiary can never be a small company** however small its figures. DEF's claim fails.

**Conclusion:** DEF Pvt. Ltd. is **not** a small company and cannot avail the small-company relaxations. The management's claim is invalid.

**If DEF were a public company:** It would fail at the very first gate — the definition opens with "a company, *other than a public company*." A public company is excluded outright; the size figures are then irrelevant. So the answer (not a small company) is the same, but for a *different and prior* reason.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Students lose marks by testing only the two numerical thresholds and stopping. The examiner is testing whether you know that Section 2(85) is a **two-stage filter**: first the categorical exclusions (public company at the threshold; holding/subsidiary/Section 8/special-Act in the proviso), and *only then* the size test. The mischief the proviso addresses is that a small subsidiary is not truly "small" in substance — it is an arm of a larger group and can access the group's resources, so Parliament denied it the compliance relaxations meant for genuinely stand-alone small businesses. The tempting wrong approach — "figures are within limits, so it's small" — ignores that being embedded in a corporate group is itself disqualifying.

*(Full-marks tip: The examiner rewards naming Section 2(85), stating both size limits AND the proviso carve-outs, and correctly linking 80% shareholding to subsidiary status under Section 2(87). Common deduction: concluding "small" purely on figures, or forgetting the "public company" exclusion in the alternative scenario.)*

---

### Q2. Ch: Preliminary & Definitions — OPC Eligibility, Conversion & Nominee Traps (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Mr. Rajan, an Indian citizen who stayed in India for 130 days during the immediately preceding financial year, wishes to form a One Person Company (OPC) to run a manufacturing business, and proposes to nominate his brother-in-law Mr. Suresh. Consider each of the following independently and comment on the legal position:
(i) Is Rajan eligible to form an OPC given his stay of 130 days?
(ii) Suresh is already the sole member of another OPC. Can he be Rajan's nominee?
(iii) The OPC's paid-up capital rises to ₹60 lakh and its average annual turnover for the relevant preceding period is ₹2.5 crore. Is conversion into a private/public company now mandatory?
(iv) Rajan wants the OPC to carry on Non-Banking Financial Investment activities. Permissible?
(v) Six months after incorporation, Rajan (member) dies. What happens?

**Answer:**
**Governing law — Section 2(62) and Section 3(1)(c) read with Rule 3 of the Companies (Incorporation) Rules, 2014.** An OPC is a company with only one person as a member. Only a **natural person who is an Indian citizen** — whether resident in India **or otherwise** — is eligible to form an OPC and to be its nominee.

**(i) Residence — 130 days:** After the 2021 amendment, the requirement of being "resident in India" was relaxed and the qualifying period reduced to **not less than 120 days** during the immediately preceding financial year (earlier 182 days). Rajan stayed **130 days ≥ 120 days**, so he qualifies. **He is eligible.**

**(ii) Suresh already a member of another OPC:** Rule 3 bars a person from being a member/nominee in **more than one OPC**. Since Suresh is *already the sole member* of another OPC, he *can* be a nominee here, **but** if by operation of the nomination he ever becomes a member of Rajan's OPC as well, he must, within **180 days**, meet the eligibility criterion by ceasing to be a member of one of the two OPCs. As a *nominee* he is not yet a member, so the nomination is initially valid — the one-OPC restriction bites only when membership actually overlaps.

**(iii) Threshold conversion — capital ₹60 lakh / turnover ₹2.5 crore:** The **mandatory** conversion thresholds (paid-up capital exceeding ₹50 lakh **and** average turnover exceeding ₹2 crore) that earlier compelled conversion were **omitted by the 2021 amendment**. An OPC may now grow without a size ceiling and convert **voluntarily** at any time. So conversion is **NOT mandatory** merely because capital is ₹60 lakh and turnover ₹2.5 crore.

**(iv) NBFC activity:** Rule 3 expressly prohibits an OPC from carrying on **Non-Banking Financial Investment activities including investment in securities of any body corporate**. Therefore the OPC **cannot** undertake NBFC investment activity. This object is impermissible.

**(v) Death of the sole member:** On Rajan's death, the **nominee (Suresh)** becomes the member of the OPC (Section 3, proviso mechanism). Suresh must then nominate a fresh person as his own nominee within the prescribed time, and the change is intimated to the Registrar. The OPC does **not** dissolve — perpetual succession is preserved through the nominee mechanism.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** OPC questions are examiner favourites because the 2021 amendment rewrote three numbers (182→120 days residence, and deletion of the ₹50 lakh/₹2 crore mandatory-conversion trigger). The concept being tested is that an OPC is designed to give a *single entrepreneur* limited liability while ensuring the entity never "dies" with the individual — hence the compulsory nominee (continuity) and the one-OPC-per-person rule (to prevent a web of shell OPCs used to fragment liability). The NBFC bar exists because an OPC's thin governance (one member, possibly one director) is unsuited to holding public financial trust. The tempting wrong answers — "130 days fails," or "conversion is mandatory at ₹60 lakh" — are exactly the pre-amendment law the examiner is checking you have updated.

*(Full-marks tip: State the *current* (post-2021) figures — 120 days and no mandatory-conversion threshold. Deductions fall for quoting 182 days or the deleted ₹50 lakh/₹2 crore trigger. Cite Rule 3 for the NBFC bar and the one-OPC rule.)*

### Q3. Ch: Preliminary & Definitions — Associate Company vs Subsidiary: "Significant Influence" (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** M Ltd. holds the following in three companies. Determine, with reasons, the relationship of each with M Ltd.:

| Investee | M Ltd.'s equity holding | Other facts |
|---|---|---|
| P Ltd. | 22% of total voting power | No board control agreement |
| Q Ltd. | 18% of total voting power | M Ltd. controls composition of Q's Board by agreement |
| R Ltd. | 45% of total voting power | Held jointly with N Ltd. under a documented joint-venture agreement; neither controls alone |

**Answer:**
**Governing provisions — Section 2(6) [associate company] and Section 2(87) [subsidiary].** An **associate company** is one in which another company has **significant influence** (control of **at least 20% of total voting power**, OR control of/participation in business decisions under an agreement), but which is **not a subsidiary** and **includes a joint venture** company. A **subsidiary** exists where the holding company **controls the composition of the Board** OR **exercises/controls more than one-half of total voting power**.

**Application:**
- **P Ltd. (22%):** 22% ≥ 20% of voting power ⇒ significant influence exists. It is not a subsidiary (no board control, ≤ 50% votes). **P Ltd. is an associate company of M Ltd.**
- **Q Ltd. (18% but board composition controlled):** Although the 18% holding is *below* the 20% numerical test, the *second limb* of "significant influence"/control is met — M Ltd. **controls the composition of Q's Board by agreement**. Control of Board composition is the *first test of a SUBSIDIARY* under Section 2(87). Therefore **Q Ltd. is a subsidiary**, not merely an associate. (The Explanation to 2(87): "controls the composition of the Board" means the power to appoint or remove all or a majority of directors.)
- **R Ltd. (45%, joint venture):** Section 2(6) *expressly includes a joint venture* within "associate company." A joint venture is a joint arrangement where the parties have joint control and rights to the *net assets*. Since it is held jointly under a documented JV agreement and neither party controls alone, **R Ltd. is an associate company (joint venture) of M Ltd.** — even though 45% exceeds 20%, it does not exceed 50%, so it is not a subsidiary.

**Conclusion:** P — associate; Q — subsidiary (board control overrides the 18% figure); R — associate (joint venture).

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The trap the examiner sets is the assumption that the relationship follows the *percentage alone*. The concept is that **control has two independent gateways — votes AND board composition** — and *either* is sufficient. Q Ltd. teaches the sharpest lesson: an 18% economic stake that carries the power to appoint the Board is a *subsidiary* (de facto control), because company law looks to *who governs*, not who owns the most shares. This mirrors the doctrine that legal form yields to control-in-substance. The mischief addressed is round-tripping and off-balance-sheet control: without the board-composition test, a parent could keep effective control while holding a thin equity stake to escape consolidation and subsidiary compliance.

*(Full-marks tip: Award-winning answers explain that "significant influence" has TWO limbs (20% votes OR agreement) and that subsidiary status has TWO limbs (board OR >50% votes), and that board-control trumps a sub-20% holding. Deduction: classifying Q as "associate" purely on 18%.)*

---

### Q4. Ch: Preliminary & Definitions — Deemed Public Company & "Body Corporate" Web (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]

**Question:** X Pvt. Ltd. has a paid-up capital of ₹5 crore. Its entire share capital is held by Y Ltd., a public listed company (99%) and Y's nominee (1%). X's Articles retain the three restrictions typical of a private company. The directors argue that since its Articles still contain the private-company restrictions and its name ends in "Private Limited," X continues to enjoy all private-company exemptions. Additionally, a Limited Liability Partnership (LLP) registered under the LLP Act, 2008 has subscribed to X's shares; X's board contends an LLP cannot be a member because it is a "firm," not a "body corporate." Examine both contentions.

**Answer:**
**Contention 1 — Subsidiary of a public company. Governing law: proviso to Section 2(71).** A private company which is a **subsidiary of a company that is not a private company** shall be **deemed to be a public company** for the purposes of the Act, **even where it continues as a private company in its articles**. Y Ltd. is a public listed company and holds 99% + 1% (nominee) = 100% of X's voting power; hence X is a subsidiary of a public company (Section 2(87)). Consequently X is a **deemed public company** and must comply with public-company provisions (e.g., minimum directors, no automatic exemptions), **notwithstanding that its Articles retain private restrictions and its name ends in "Private Limited."** The directors' first contention is **invalid** — the label and articles do not override the statutory deeming.

**Contention 2 — Can an LLP be a member? Governing law: Section 2(11) "body corporate."** "Body corporate" or "corporation" **includes a company incorporated outside India**, but **excludes** (i) a co-operative society and (ii) any other body corporate notified by the Central Government. Under the **LLP Act, 2008, an LLP is a body corporate** with perpetual succession. Nothing in the Companies Act excludes an LLP from holding shares. Therefore an **LLP can validly be a member** of X. The board's contention that an LLP is a mere "firm" and cannot be a member is **incorrect** — an LLP is a body corporate, not a partnership firm under the Indian Partnership Act, 1932.

**Conclusion:** X is a deemed public company; the LLP is a valid member.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Two distinct concepts are bundled. First, the deeming fiction in Section 2(71): the *substance* of ownership (being a subsidiary of a non-private company) controls status, not the *form* of the name or articles — Parliament closed the loophole of hiding a public-controlled entity behind a "Private Limited" label to escape the stricter public-company governance meant to protect wider stakeholders. Second, "body corporate" is a *wider* genus than "company": an LLP, though born of partnership economics, is statutorily incorporated and thus a body corporate capable of owning shares. Students err by treating "LLP = firm" (economic intuition) instead of reading Section 2(11) with the LLP Act (legal status).

*(Full-marks tip: Cite the proviso to Section 2(71) by name and stress "notwithstanding the articles." For the LLP, cite Section 2(11) and the LLP Act's "body corporate" status. Deduction: saying X keeps private exemptions because of its name, or that an LLP cannot hold shares.)*

---

### Q5. Ch: Incorporation of Company — Doctrine of Ultra Vires & Its Consequences (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Sunrise Ltd.'s Memorandum of Association authorises it "to manufacture and deal in ceramic tiles." The Board, seeing an opportunity, borrowed ₹50 lakh from a bank and used it to finance a completely unrelated venture — running a chain of restaurants — an activity nowhere mentioned or reasonably incidental to the objects clause. The restaurant venture collapsed. Analyse: (i) Is the restaurant activity valid? (ii) Can the bank recover the ₹50 lakh? (iii) Can the shareholders, by passing a special resolution, ratify the act? (iv) Distinguish this from an act that is merely beyond the powers of the directors.

**Answer:**
**Governing doctrine — Doctrine of Ultra Vires** (*Ashbury Railway Carriage & Iron Co. v. Riche*), read with **Section 4(1)(c)** (objects clause) and **Section 245**. An act outside the objects clause of the Memorandum is **ultra vires the company**, and is **void ab initio** — it binds neither the company nor the outsider, and is incapable of ratification even by the unanimous consent of all shareholders.

**(i) Validity of the restaurant activity:** Manufacturing ceramic tiles is the sole object; running restaurants is neither expressly authorised nor reasonably incidental to tile manufacture. The activity is **ultra vires the company and void.** It creates no legal rights or obligations qua the company.

**(ii) Bank's recovery of ₹50 lakh:** An ultra vires *borrowing* does not create a valid debt; the lender cannot sue on the contract. **However**, equity provides relief: (a) **Doctrine of subrogation** — if the ultra vires loan was used to pay off *intra vires* (lawful) debts of the company, the lender steps into the shoes of the paid-off creditors to that extent; and (b) **tracing** — the lender can recover the money or property so long as it can be *identified* in the company's hands. Here the money was **sunk into the failed restaurant venture** and is *not traceable* and was *not* used to discharge lawful debts, so the bank **cannot recover** under contract, subrogation, or tracing. The bank's remedy, if any, lies in a **personal action against the directors** for breach of warranty of authority / for the loss.

**(iii) Ratification:** An ultra vires act is a **nullity** and **cannot be ratified** — not even by a special resolution or by 100% of the members. The only cure is to **alter the objects clause** *prospectively* by special resolution under Section 13; but alteration cannot validate a past ultra vires act.

**(iv) Distinction from acts beyond directors' powers:** If an act is **within the company's objects** but merely beyond the *directors'* authority (ultra vires the directors, not the company), it is **NOT void** — the company **can ratify** it (usually by ordinary resolution), and an innocent outsider is protected by the doctrine of indoor management. Only acts ultra vires the *company itself* are incurably void.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The doctrine exists to protect two groups: **shareholders** (who invested on the faith that their money would be used only for the stated objects) and **creditors** (who assessed risk against a defined business). This is the "mischief": unlimited object-drift would defeat both. That is *why* ratification is impossible — the members cannot, by later vote, undo a protection the law confers on creditors and dissenting/future shareholders. The crucial exam distinction is the *level* at which "ultra vires" bites: **ultra vires the company = void, unratifiable**; **ultra vires the directors = voidable, ratifiable**. Students who blur these two lose the whole question. Note also that the doctrine has been *diluted* in practice because modern objects clauses are drafted very widely — but where the object genuinely does not cover the act, the doctrine still voids it.

*(Full-marks tip: Cite Ashbury, name the void ab initio + no-ratification rule, and score the extra marks by discussing subrogation/tracing for the lender and the directors' personal liability. Sharpest deduction: saying shareholders can ratify by special resolution — they cannot for an act ultra vires the company.)*

---

### Q6. Ch: Incorporation of Company — Doctrine of Indoor Management vs Constructive Notice (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Fortune Ltd.'s Articles empower the Board to borrow up to ₹10 crore, and provide that borrowings beyond that require a members' special resolution. The Managing Director, without any such resolution, executed a loan agreement for ₹15 crore with Trust Bank. Consider three variants and decide, in each, whether Trust Bank can enforce the loan:
(i) Trust Bank made no enquiry and relied on the MD's representation that all internal approvals were in place;
(ii) The MD is also a director of Trust Bank and the bank was aware no special resolution had been passed;
(iii) The loan document was signed by a person who was described as "MD" but had never actually been validly appointed as such, and the irregularity was discoverable from the company's registered filings.

**Answer:**
**Two competing doctrines. Constructive notice (against outsiders):** every person dealing with a company is *deemed to have read* its public documents (Memorandum & Articles) and to know their contents — **Section 399** makes them a public record. **Doctrine of indoor management / the rule in *Royal British Bank v. Turquand* (in favour of outsiders):** an outsider who has read the public documents, and finds the transaction *consistent with them*, is **entitled to assume that all internal/indoor procedures** (like passing the required resolution) have been duly complied with; he is not bound to verify indoor compliance.

**(i) No enquiry, relied on MD:** The Articles publicly show a *power to borrow up to ₹10 crore* and a *requirement of special resolution beyond that*. Borrowing ₹15 crore is beyond ₹10 crore; whether the special resolution was passed is an **indoor** matter. Under Turquand, Trust Bank — dealing in good faith and finding nothing on the face of the Articles to arouse suspicion about the *existence* of a resolution — may **assume the special resolution was duly passed**. **The loan is enforceable.** (The passing of a resolution is a classic indoor procedure the outsider need not police.)

**(ii) MD is a director of Trust Bank / bank knew no resolution existed — EXCEPTION 1 (Knowledge of irregularity):** The protection of Turquand is **lost** where the outsider **had actual knowledge** of the irregularity, or is *put on inquiry*. As a director of the borrower-company, the MD's knowledge is imputed to the bank; the bank **knew** no special resolution had been passed. An outsider with knowledge of the irregularity **cannot** invoke indoor management. **The loan is NOT enforceable.**

**(iii) "MD" never validly appointed, irregularity on public record — EXCEPTION 2 (Forgery / no title, and matters of public record):** Turquand protects assumptions about *internal procedure*, not about a person having *no authority at all*. Where the very appointment is a nullity, or where the defect is discoverable from **public documents** (constructive notice cuts the other way), the outsider cannot rely on indoor management. Since the invalid appointment was **discoverable from the registered filings**, constructive notice fixes the bank with knowledge of it; and an act by a person with **no valid title** (akin to forgery — *Ruben v. Great Fingall*) confers no authority. **The loan is NOT enforceable** on the strength of that signatory's authority.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The two doctrines are a matched pair balancing certainty and fairness. **Constructive notice protects the company** — outsiders must read the public documents, so a borrowing limit *stated in the Articles* binds them. **Indoor management protects the outsider** — because he *cannot* see inside the boardroom, he need not verify that internally-required steps (resolutions, quorums, sanctions) actually happened. The dividing line is *visibility*: what is on the **public record** the outsider is deemed to know (constructive notice); what happens **behind closed doors** he may assume was done properly (Turquand). The three exceptions — (1) knowledge of the irregularity, (2) suspicion/put on inquiry, (3) forgery/no authority, plus acts void under public documents — all share one theme: the outsider **cannot** claim the benefit of an assumption he knew (or the public record told him) was false. This is why (i) succeeds but (ii) and (iii) fail.

*(Full-marks tip: Name *Turquand's* case, *Ruben v. Great Fingall* for forgery, and Section 399 for constructive notice. Full marks require correctly mapping each variant to the *right* doctrine and exception. Deduction: applying indoor management to variant (ii)/(iii) despite knowledge/no-authority.)*

---

### Q7. Ch: Incorporation of Company — Lifting the Corporate Veil (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Mr. Kapoor, a former employee of Zenith Ltd., had signed a valid non-compete covenant barring him for three years from soliciting Zenith's customers. To circumvent it, Kapoor formed "Kapoor Trading Pvt. Ltd.," of which he and his wife are the only shareholders and directors, and used it to solicit Zenith's customers. Separately, the same company was found to have been used to divert ₹40 lakh of business profits to evade income tax, and to hold, as a mere front, assets that in reality belong to an enemy-controlled foreign entity during wartime. Advise Zenith and the authorities on whether the separate legal personality of Kapoor Trading Pvt. Ltd. can be disregarded in each situation.

**Answer:**
**Governing principle — Separate legal personality (*Salomon v. Salomon & Co. Ltd.*), and its exception, the doctrine of "lifting/piercing the corporate veil."** A company is, in law, a person distinct from its members; ordinarily members are not liable for its acts. **But** where the corporate form is used as a **façade to evade legal obligations, perpetrate fraud, or defeat public interest**, courts and statute will **lift the veil** and look at the real persons behind it.

**(i) Evasion of the non-compete covenant — veil LIFTED (fraud / sham):** This is on all fours with ***Gilford Motor Co. v. Horne*** and ***Jones v. Lipman*** — a person under a personal legal obligation cannot escape it by interposing a company that is a "mere cloak or sham." Kapoor Trading Pvt. Ltd. was formed **precisely to evade** Kapoor's non-compete covenant; the company is his alter ego. The court will **lift the veil**, treat the company's solicitation as Kapoor's own, and grant Zenith an **injunction against both** Kapoor and the company. Advice to Zenith: it can enforce the covenant against the company too.

**(ii) Diversion of profits to evade tax — veil LIFTED (evasion of taxation):** Where the corporate device is used to **evade tax** (as opposed to legitimate tax planning), courts lift the veil to reach the real income and the real person (*CIT v. Meenakshi Mills* line of reasoning). The ₹40 lakh diverted to evade tax can be brought to tax in the hands of the true earner; the veil is **lifted**.

**(iii) Front for an enemy-controlled entity in wartime — veil LIFTED (enemy character / public interest):** Following ***Daimler Co. Ltd. v. Continental Tyre & Rubber Co.***, a company registered in a friendly country may still be treated as an **"enemy"** if it is *controlled* by enemy nationals; the court looks behind the veil to the nationality/control of the persons in de facto control. The veil is **lifted** to determine the company's real (enemy) character and to protect national interest.

**Conclusion:** In all three situations the separate personality of Kapoor Trading Pvt. Ltd. is **disregarded** — for fraud/evasion of obligation, for tax evasion, and for enemy-character/public interest respectively.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** *Salomon* is the bedrock — incorporation creates a real, separate person, and that separateness must be respected to make limited liability meaningful (otherwise no one would invest). The veil is *not* lifted merely because a company is small, family-owned, or a one-man company — that alone is legitimate (*Salomon* itself was a near one-man company). It is lifted only for a **recognised justifying ground**: fraud/sham to defeat a legal obligation, evasion of tax, enemy character, or where a statute itself pierces (e.g., reduction of members below minimum, fraudulent conduct of business under Section 339). The unifying idea is that the corporate form is a **privilege granted for legitimate business**, and the law will not let it become "an engine of fraud" or a shield against public interest. The exam trap is to lift the veil too readily — you must anchor each lifting to a *specific recognised category*, not a vague "unfairness."

*(Full-marks tip: Cite the correct case per ground — Gilford/Jones (fraud), Daimler (enemy), and the tax-evasion line. Start with Salomon to show you know the *default* is separateness. Deduction: lifting the veil just because it is a two-person family company, with no justifying category.)*

---

### Q8. Ch: Incorporation of Company — Memorandum Alteration: Change of Registered Office Across States (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Meridian Ltd., registered in Maharashtra, wishes to shift its registered office to Karnataka to be nearer to its principal customers. A group of debenture-holders and one creditor object, fearing the shift will make it harder to enforce their claims. A rival group of shareholders alleges the real motive is to escape an ongoing investigation by the Maharashtra Registrar. Advise the company on (i) the procedure and the authority whose approval is required, and (ii) the grounds on which the approval may be refused.

**Answer:**
**Governing law — Section 13(4) and 13(7), read with Section 12.** A change of registered office **from one State to another** requires **alteration of the "situation clause" of the Memorandum**, which needs:
1. A **special resolution** of the members;
2. **Approval of the Central Government** (power delegated to the **Regional Director, RD**);
3. Filing of the RD's confirmation with **both** Registrars (the State it is leaving and the State it is entering), and the Registrar of the new State issues a fresh certificate.

**(i) Procedure and authority:** Meridian must (a) pass a special resolution; (b) give **individual notice** to every creditor, debenture-holder, and to the Registrar and other regulators concerned, and advertise the application so that objectors may be heard; (c) apply to the **Regional Director** for confirmation. The RD, after hearing objectors, may confirm the alteration on such terms as it thinks fit.

**(ii) Grounds for refusal / conditions:** The confirming authority is concerned to protect **creditors and revenue and public interest**. It may **refuse or impose conditions** where:
- the interests of **creditors/debenture-holders are not adequately safeguarded** — typically the RD requires the company to **secure or discharge** their claims before confirming;
- the change is sought for an **improper or mala fide purpose**, e.g., to **evade an ongoing investigation, inquiry, or inspection** or to defeat regulatory jurisdiction — this is a recognised ground to refuse;
- proper notice has not been given to affected persons or a regulator objects on legitimate grounds.

**Advice:** Meridian may shift its office to Karnataka *provided* it demonstrates a bona fide commercial reason (proximity to customers is legitimate), and *secures the claims* of the objecting creditor and debenture-holders to the RD's satisfaction. If the shareholders' allegation that the true motive is to **escape the Registrar's investigation** is substantiated, the RD is entitled to **refuse confirmation**, because the alteration would defeat the very object of the investigation.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The situation clause is part of the Memorandum — the company's "charter" — so altering it is not a purely internal matter; it affects *third parties* (creditors who chose to deal with a Maharashtra company knowing where to sue) and *the State's revenue and regulators*. That is why an inter-State shift needs an **external, quasi-judicial confirmation** (the RD) on top of the members' special resolution, unlike a shift *within* the same city or (with only Registrar involvement) within the same State/ROC jurisdiction. The safeguard exists to prevent a company from using a change of address as a device to **outrun creditors or regulators** — hence the twin grounds of "creditor protection" and "no mala fide escape from investigation." The tempting wrong answer — "special resolution alone suffices" — confuses an inter-State shift with an intra-city one.

*(Full-marks tip: Distinguish the four levels of office change (within city; within State but different ROC; State to State; and note the Registrar's role). Marks for citing Section 13(4)/(7) and the RD as delegate of Central Government, plus the mala fide/creditor grounds for refusal. Deduction: forgetting RD approval, or that both Registrars are involved.)*

---

### Q9. Ch: Prospectus & Allotment of Securities — Private Placement Compliance & Penalty (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Nova Ltd. proposes to raise ₹4 crore by a private placement of equity shares in a financial year. Examine the validity of each of the following and state the consequences:
(i) The offer (private placement offer-cum-application letter, Form PAS-4) is made to **220 persons** (excluding qualified institutional buyers and employees under a stock option scheme) in the year.
(ii) An applicant, Mr. Aditya, who received the offer, forwarded it to his friend Mr. Bharat, who applied and was allotted shares.
(iii) The application money of ₹80 lakh was received partly in **cash** and was **not kept in a separate bank account**, and shares were allotted **75 days** after receipt of the application money.
(iv) The company advertised the private placement in a leading newspaper to attract subscribers.

**Answer:**
**Governing law — Section 42 read with Rule 14 of the Companies (Prospectus and Allotment of Securities) Rules, 2014.** A private placement is an offer of securities to a **select group of identified persons** (not the public) through a **private placement offer-cum-application (PAS-4)**, subject to strict conditions.

**(i) Offer to 220 persons — LIMIT BREACHED:** The offer in a financial year must not be made to **more than 200 persons in the aggregate** (excluding QIBs and employees under ESOP), counted **per kind of security**. An offer to 220 identified persons **exceeds 200** and therefore **loses the character of a private placement** and is **deemed to be a public offer** — triggering the entire prospectus regime (Section 23 onwards). This is a **contravention**. The tempting relief — "just ignore the extra 20" — is wrong; crossing 200 converts the *whole* issue into a public offer.

**(ii) Aditya forwarding to Bharat — RENUNCIATION BARRED:** A private placement offer is **personal to the identified addressee** and carries **no right of renunciation**. Bharat is not an identified offeree; an application by a non-invitee is invalid, and treating such applications as valid is a contravention. Allotment to Bharat is **improper**.

**(iii) Cash / no separate account / 75-day allotment — MULTIPLE CONTRAVENTIONS:**
- Application money must be paid by **cheque, demand draft or other banking channel — NOT in cash.** Receipt in cash violates Section 42.
- The money must be kept in a **separate bank account** in a scheduled bank and **not utilised** except for allotment or refund. Non-maintenance of the separate account is a contravention.
- Allotment must be completed **within 60 days** of receipt of application money; if not, the money must be **repaid within 15 days** thereafter, failing which it is repayable with **interest at 12% p.a.** from the end of the 60th day. Allotment on the **75th day** breaches the 60-day rule; the company was obliged to refund by day 75 with interest.

**(iv) Newspaper advertisement — PROHIBITED:** A private placement offer shall **not** carry any **public advertisement** or use any media/marketing to inform the public at large. Advertising in a newspaper **violates** Section 42 and betrays the "select identified persons" nature of the issue.

**Consequence / Penalty:** For contravention of Section 42, the company, its promoters and directors are liable to a **penalty which may extend to the amount raised through the private placement or ₹2 crore, whichever is lower**, and the company must **refund all monies to subscribers within 30 days** of the order imposing the penalty.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Private placement is a *deliberate carve-out* from the elaborate, investor-protective public-issue machinery — so the law hedges it with tight conditions to ensure it does **not become a public issue in disguise**. Each rule guards that boundary: the **200-person cap** (beyond which the offer is *too wide* to be "private"), the **no-renunciation / no-advertisement** rules (which stop the offer from *leaking to the public*), and the **separate-account, banking-channel, 60-day** rules (which protect subscribers' money and prevent its diversion before allotment). The unifying mischief is companies raising money from the public while dodging prospectus liability. That is *why* breaching the 200 cap does not merely penalise the excess — it re-characterises the *entire* issue as a public offer, because the very justification for the lighter regime (narrow, identified investors) has failed.

*(Full-marks tip: Cite Section 42 + Rule 14; nail the four numbers — 200 persons, banking channel (no cash), separate account, 60-day allotment/15-day refund/12% interest — and the penalty (amount raised or ₹2 crore, lower). Deduction: saying only the extra 20 offers are void, or permitting renunciation.)*

---

### Q10. Ch: Prospectus & Allotment of Securities — Mis-statement Liability: Civil & Criminal (Marks: 10) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Horizon Ltd. issued a prospectus that overstated its expected profits by relying on a valuation report of an expert engineer (which the Board honestly believed) and by including a statement — later found materially false — that a major government contract had been "awarded" when in fact only a tender had been submitted. Mr. Deepak subscribed for shares on the faith of the prospectus and suffered loss when the truth emerged and the share price crashed. Advise:
(i) Deepak's civil remedies and against whom;
(ii) The defences available to (a) an ordinary director and (b) the expert engineer;
(iii) The criminal liability for the mis-statement;
(iv) Whether Deepak, who bought the shares **in the secondary market from an existing shareholder** (not on original allotment), can sue for mis-statement.

**Answer:**
**Governing law — Sections 34 (criminal liability), 35 (civil liability), and 36 (fraudulently inducing investment); plus Section 26 (contents of prospectus).**

**(i) Civil remedies (Section 35) and against whom:** Where a person subscribes for securities **on the faith of a prospectus containing a misleading statement**, and sustains loss, he may recover **compensation** from — (a) every **director** at the time of issue; (b) every **promoter**; (c) every person who **authorised the issue** of the prospectus; and (d) every **expert** (to the extent of his statement). Deepak may claim compensation from the directors, promoters and those who authorised the prospectus for the loss caused by the false "contract awarded" statement. He may **additionally rescind** the contract of allotment (return the shares and recover his money) against the company for misrepresentation, provided he acts promptly and before the company goes into liquidation.

**(ii) Defences:**
*(a) An ordinary director (Section 35(2)):* escapes liability if he proves — (1) he **withdrew consent** before issue and the prospectus was issued without his authority; or (2) the prospectus was issued without his knowledge/consent and on becoming aware he gave **reasonable public notice**; or (3) as regards an **expert's statement**, he had **reasonable ground to believe** the expert was competent, that the expert consented, and had not withdrawn consent — i.e., a director may rely honestly on a competent expert's report. So on the **profit projection based on the engineer's report**, an honestly-believing director has a good defence; but on the **"contract awarded" falsehood** — a statement of *fact within the Board's own knowledge* — the "honest expert reliance" defence does **not** help.
*(b) The expert engineer:* is liable only for **his own statement**, and has a defence if he proves he was **competent** and **honestly believed** the statement true up to allotment, or that he **withdrew his consent** in writing before delivery of the prospectus. He is not liable for the "contract awarded" statement, which was not his.

**(iii) Criminal liability (Section 34):** Where a prospectus **includes any statement which is untrue or misleading in a material respect**, or omits a material matter likely to mislead, **every person who authorised the issue** is criminally liable under Section 34, which attracts the punishment for **fraud under Section 447** (imprisonment 6 months to 10 years and fine up to 3× the amount involved). **Defence:** the person proves the statement/omission was **immaterial**, or that he had **reasonable grounds to believe, and did believe up to issue, that it was true**. Since the "contract awarded" statement is material and demonstrably false and within the Board's knowledge, criminal liability under Section 34/447 is attracted for those who authorised it. Section 36 additionally penalises fraudulently inducing persons to invest.

**(iv) Secondary-market purchaser:** Section 35 civil liability protects a person who subscribes **on the faith of the prospectus** — i.e., one to whom the prospectus was *addressed for subscription/allotment*. A person who buys **in the secondary market** from an existing shareholder did **not** subscribe on the faith of the prospectus in the primary issue, and therefore **generally cannot** sue under Section 35 for prospectus mis-statement. (His remedy, if any, lies in general law against his seller, or under securities-market/fraud provisions if the statement was intended to induce market purchases.) So Deepak, if he bought in the secondary market, **loses the Section 35 remedy**.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** A prospectus is an *invitation issued to the public to part with money*, so the law imposes a **near-fiduciary standard of truthfulness** and spreads liability across everyone who lends the document credibility — directors, promoters, authorisers and experts — because investors cannot independently verify the issuer. The **defence structure mirrors fault**: those who *honestly and reasonably* believed the truth (or reasonably relied on a competent expert) are excused, because liability is meant to punish *culpable* misrepresentation, not honest error — this is why the same director wins on the expert-based projection but loses on the fact he himself misstated. The **secondary-market cut-off** flows from the words "on the faith of the prospectus": the prospectus is a *primary-issue* document, and its statutory liability attaches to the *subscription* it invited, not to later trading — a crucial trap the examiner plants to test whether you grasp *who* the provision protects.

*(Full-marks tip: Separate the two statements (expert-based projection vs. the Board's own false fact) and show the defence works for one but not the other — that nuance earns the top band. Cite Sections 34, 35, 36 and link Section 34 to 447 punishment. Deduction: giving a secondary-market buyer a Section 35 remedy, or ignoring rescission as a separate remedy.)*

---

### Q11. Ch: Prospectus & Allotment of Securities — Minimum Subscription, Application Money & Refund (Marks: 6) [Problem]

**Question:** Galaxy Ltd. issued a prospectus offering 10,00,000 equity shares of ₹10 each to the public. The prospectus stated the minimum subscription. By the closing date, the company had received valid applications for the number of shares shown below. Application money was ₹2 per share (i.e., not less than 5% of nominal value). Examine the legal position and compute refunds where applicable, for each scenario:

| Scenario | Shares applied for | Shares offered |
|---|---|---|
| A | 6,80,000 | 10,00,000 |
| B | 9,10,000 | 10,00,000 |

Assume SEBI's requirement of 90% minimum subscription of the "issue" applies.

**Answer:**
**Governing law — Section 39, read with SEBI (ICDR) requirement.** No allotment of securities offered to the public shall be made unless the **minimum subscription** stated in the prospectus has been received, and the **application money** (not less than **5% of nominal value**, or SEBI's prescribed amount) has been received by cheque/other instrument. For a public issue, SEBI mandates minimum subscription of **90% of the issue**. If minimum subscription is **not received within 30 days** of the date of the prospectus (or such period as SEBI prescribes), the **entire application money must be refunded within the prescribed time**, failing which it is repayable with **interest**.

**WN-1 — Minimum subscription threshold (90% of issue):**
90% × 10,00,000 shares = **9,00,000 shares** must be subscribed.

**WN-2 — Test each scenario against 9,00,000:**
- **Scenario A:** Applications = 6,80,000 shares < 9,00,000 ⇒ **minimum subscription NOT met.**
- **Scenario B:** Applications = 9,10,000 shares ≥ 9,00,000 ⇒ **minimum subscription MET.**

**WN-3 — Application money involved (₹2 per share):**
- Scenario A money received = 6,80,000 × ₹2 = **₹13,60,000** (must be fully refunded).

| Statement Showing Legal Position and Refund |  |  |
|---|---|---|
| Scenario | Position | Amount to refund |
| A (6,80,000 applied) | Minimum subscription NOT received; **no allotment** permissible | Refund entire ₹13,60,000 within prescribed time; interest if delayed beyond 15 days after the 30-day period |
| B (9,10,000 applied) | Minimum subscription received; **allotment can proceed** | Nil (subject to allotment/basis of allotment) |

**Answer:** In **Scenario A**, the company **cannot allot** any shares; it must **refund ₹13,60,000** (6,80,000 × ₹2) to all applicants within the prescribed period, with interest at the prescribed rate if delayed. In **Scenario B**, minimum subscription (9,00,000 shares) is satisfied, so **allotment may proceed.**

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Minimum subscription protects investors and the company's own viability: if the public does not subscribe to at least the threshold, the company would be *undercapitalised* to carry out the very objects for which it raised money — allotting on a thin subscription would leave the venture underfunded and expose early subscribers to disproportionate risk. That is *why* the law makes allotment **conditional** on reaching the threshold and mandates a *full and prompt refund* (with penal interest) if it is not — the applicants' money must not be locked up in a failed issue. The SEBI 90% figure operationalises this for public issues. The trap is comparing applications to the *full offer* (10,00,000) and concluding "under-subscribed, so scale down"; the correct test is against the **90% minimum subscription**, below which *no allotment at all* is permitted.

*(Full-marks tip: State the 90%-of-issue rule and the 5%-of-nominal-value application-money floor, compute the exact refund (₹13,60,000), and mention the interest-on-delay consequence. Deduction: allowing partial allotment in Scenario A, or comparing to 100% of the offer instead of the 90% threshold.)*

---

### Q12. Ch: Prospectus & Allotment of Securities — Deemed Prospectus & "Offer for Sale" (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Pioneer Ltd. allotted its entire fresh issue of 5,00,000 shares to an issue house, Wealth Securities Ltd., under an arrangement that Wealth Securities would, within a fortnight, offer these shares to the public. Wealth Securities then issued a document to the public offering the shares, but that document did not contain the disclosures required of a prospectus, on the plea that "it is not a prospectus issued by the company." A member of the public who subscribed suffered loss due to omission of material particulars. Examine whether the offer document is a prospectus, who is liable, and the additional particulars such a document must state.

**Answer:**
**Governing law — Section 25 (document containing offer of securities for sale to be deemed prospectus).** Where a company **allots or agrees to allot** securities **with a view to those securities being offered for sale to the public**, any **document by which the offer for sale to the public is made** shall, for all purposes, be **deemed to be a prospectus** issued by the company; and all the liabilities and requirements of a prospectus apply to it.

**Deeming of intention:** Section 25 further provides that it shall be **evidence that the allotment was made with a view to the securities being offered for sale to the public** if it is shown that (a) the offer to the public was made **within six months** after the allotment/agreement to allot; **or** (b) at the date of the offer, the company had **not received the whole consideration** for the shares.

**Application:** Pioneer Ltd. allotted the whole issue to Wealth Securities under an arrangement that they be **offered to the public within a fortnight** — well within six months. The transaction is a classic **"offer for sale"** and the arrangement shows the allotment was made *with a view to* a public offer. Therefore the document issued by Wealth Securities is a **deemed prospectus**, and the plea that "it is not a prospectus issued by the company" **fails**.

**Who is liable:** Because it is a deemed prospectus, the **company (Pioneer) and its directors** attract prospectus liability, and in addition, **the persons making the offer for sale — the directors/officers of Wealth Securities (the issuing house) — are deemed to be "directors" for this purpose** and are liable for the mis-statement/omission. The subscriber may pursue civil liability under Section 35 and, for material untruths, criminal liability under Section 34.

**Additional particulars required (Section 26 read with Section 25):** The deemed prospectus must state, over and above the ordinary prospectus contents — (i) the **net amount of consideration** received or to be received by the company for the shares; and (ii) the **place and time** at which the **contract** under which the securities were allotted may be inspected.

**Conclusion:** The offer document **is a prospectus** (deemed); Wealth Securities and Pioneer, and the persons offering the shares, are **liable** for the omission of material particulars; and the document was legally required to contain full prospectus disclosures plus the two additional particulars.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Section 25 closes an obvious evasion route. A company could otherwise dump its whole issue on a friendly intermediary (who *is* an identified subscriber, so no public-issue rules apply), and let the intermediary re-sell to the public through a bare offer document carrying **none** of the investor protections of a prospectus. The law sees through this **two-step device** and, by a deeming fiction, treats the *intermediary's public offer* as the company's own prospectus — so the disclosure duties and liabilities cannot be shed by routing the issue through an issue house. The **six-months / unpaid-consideration** presumptions are evidentiary shortcuts that stop the parties from denying the obvious purpose. The mischief is identical to the private-placement provisions: prevent a *public* fund-raising from masquerading as a *private* transaction to escape prospectus liability.

*(Full-marks tip: Name Section 25, state BOTH presumptions (6 months / consideration not fully received), and list the TWO additional particulars. Marks for identifying that the issue-house persons are treated as directors. Deduction: accepting the "not the company's prospectus" plea, or omitting the extra particulars.)*

---

### Q13. Ch: Prospectus & Allotment of Securities — Variation of Objects & Utilisation of Issue Proceeds (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Vertex Ltd. raised ₹50 crore from the public through a prospectus that stated the money would be used to build a new plant (₹35 crore) and repay borrowings (₹15 crore). Eighteen months later, having spent only ₹10 crore, the Board — citing a market downturn — wishes to divert ₹25 crore to acquire a stake in an unrelated startup. A section of dissenting shareholders opposes the change. Advise on (i) whether and how the objects for which money was raised can be varied, and (ii) the protection available to dissenting shareholders.

**Answer:**
**Governing law — Section 27 (variation in terms of contract or objects in prospectus).** A company which has **raised money from the public through a prospectus** and **still has any unutilised amount** of the money so raised, shall **not vary the terms of the contract referred to in the prospectus or the objects** for which the prospectus was issued, **except** by way of a **special resolution** passed in general meeting, AND subject to the disclosure and exit conditions below.

**(i) Procedure to vary the objects:**
1. The Board must publish a **notice of the special resolution** in the manner prescribed (including newspaper advertisement and on the company's website), **clearly indicating the justification** for the variation.
2. A **special resolution** must be passed by the members.
3. The **dissenting shareholders** must be given an **exit opportunity**.

**Application:** Vertex has ₹40 crore (₹50cr − ₹10cr spent) of *unutilised* public-issue money. Diverting ₹25 crore to an unrelated startup acquisition is a **variation of the objects** stated in the prospectus. It **cannot** be done by a mere Board decision; it requires the Section 27 machinery — publication of the justification and a **special resolution**. A downturn may be a legitimate justification, but the *process* is mandatory.

**(ii) Protection of dissenting shareholders (Section 27(2)):** Shareholders who **did not agree** to the variation must be given an **exit offer** by the **promoters/controlling shareholders** at a price and in the manner **specified by SEBI**. This is the key safeguard: those who invested on the faith of the *original* objects are entitled to **exit at a fair price** rather than be dragged into a different business.

**Conclusion:** Vertex may vary the objects **only** by special resolution with full disclosure of justification, and **must** offer the dissenting shareholders an exit at the SEBI-specified price. A unilateral Board diversion is **invalid**.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Investors subscribe to a public issue **relying on the stated use of funds** — the objects clause of the prospectus is, in effect, a promise about *what their money will do*. Allowing the Board to redirect unutilised proceeds to a wholly different (and riskier) venture would betray that reliance. Section 27 therefore imposes a **double protection**: (a) a *supermajority* (special resolution) with *transparent justification*, so the change reflects the collective will and is publicly reasoned; and (b) an *exit* for the minority who object, so no investor is *forced* to fund a business he never signed up for. This is the same investor-protection philosophy that runs through the whole prospectus chapter — the difference here is that it protects investors *after* the money is raised, at the point of *repurposing*. The trap is to think a "good commercial reason" lets the Board act alone; the reason may justify the *resolution*, but never *replaces* it.

*(Full-marks tip: Cite Section 27, insist on the special resolution + published justification + SEBI-priced exit for dissenters. Deduction: allowing the Board to divert funds without a special resolution, or omitting the dissenting-shareholders' exit right.)*

---

### Q14. Ch: Prospectus & Allotment of Securities — Allotment Irregularities & Return of Allotment (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Crest Ltd., a public company, made a public issue. Consider the following independently and state the legal consequence in each case:
(i) The company received the minimum subscription but **failed to file the return of allotment** in Form PAS-3 within the prescribed period.
(ii) The company **allotted shares before the beginning of the fifth day** after the issue of the prospectus.
(iii) The shares are dealt in on a stock exchange; the company applied for listing but **listing permission was refused**. What happens to the allotment?

**Answer:**
**Governing law — Sections 39 and 40, and the allotment rules under the Companies Act, 2013.**

**(i) Failure to file return of allotment (PAS-3):** Under **Section 39(4)**, whenever a company makes an allotment, it must file a **return of allotment (Form PAS-3)** with the Registrar within **thirty days** of allotment. Failure to file attracts a **penalty** on the company and every officer in default (a per-day penalty subject to a maximum). The allotment itself is **not automatically void**, but the default is a punishable contravention and must be remedied by filing (with additional fees). 

**(ii) Allotment before the fifth day / opening of subscription list:** A company must not allot securities until the **beginning of the fifth day after the date of issue of the prospectus** (or such later time specified in the prospectus) — this is the earliest date the **subscription list opens**. An allotment made **before** that time is **irregular**; the purpose of the waiting period is to give the public a fair, minimum window to consider and apply. The contravention exposes the company/officers to penalty.

**(iii) Refusal of listing permission — Section 40:** Where a prospectus states that application has been/will be made for the securities to be **listed** on a stock exchange, it is a **condition** that such permission be obtained. If **listing permission is refused** (or not applied for/not granted before the prescribed time), the **allotment becomes VOID**, and the company must **forthwith repay all money received** from applicants. If the money is not repaid within the prescribed period (e.g., within 8 days after the company becomes liable to repay, plus a further grace, failing which **with interest**), the **directors are jointly and severally liable** to repay with interest at the prescribed rate. Money received must be kept in a **separate bank account** until listing permission is obtained.

**Conclusion:** (i) punishable default, allotment valid but must file PAS-3; (ii) irregular allotment, penal consequences; (iii) allotment **void**, money repayable forthwith with directors personally liable on default.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Each rule protects a different facet of the investor's position. **Filing PAS-3** creates a *public record* of who holds the shares — transparency for creditors and regulators — so its breach is penal but does not undo the allotment (voiding it would harm the very allottees the record is meant to serve). The **fifth-day/subscription-list** rule guarantees the offer is genuinely *open to the public for a minimum period*, preventing insiders from grabbing the issue before ordinary investors can apply. The **listing-refusal** rule (Section 40) is the strongest: investors subscribe *expecting liquidity* through listing; if that fails, the foundation of their investment decision collapses, so the law **voids the allotment and returns their money**, backed by *personal director liability* to ensure the refund actually happens. The severity scales with how fundamentally the breach defeats the investor's expectation.

*(Full-marks tip: Distinguish "punishable but valid" (PAS-3, early allotment) from "void with mandatory refund" (listing refusal, Section 40), and mention directors' joint-and-several liability plus the separate bank account. Deduction: treating listing refusal as merely penal instead of voiding the allotment.)*

---

### Q15. Ch: Preliminary & Definitions — Government Company, Foreign Company & Control Nuance (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Determine, with reasons, the correct classification in each case:
(i) Alpha Ltd. — 45% of paid-up share capital held by the Central Government, 8% by a State Government, and the balance by the public.
(ii) Beta Ltd. — a company incorporated in Singapore that has no place of business in India but conducts online business with a significant number of Indian customers through electronic mode.
(iii) Gamma Ltd. — 60% held by "Delta Ltd.," which is itself a Government Company. Is Gamma a Government Company?

**Answer:**
**(i) Government Company — Section 2(45):** A "Government company" means a company in which **not less than 51%** of the **paid-up share capital** is held by the **Central Government, or by any State Government(s), or partly by the Central Government and partly by one or more State Governments**, and includes a subsidiary of such a company. **Application:** Central 45% + State 8% = **53% ≥ 51%** in aggregate. Since the definition permits *combining* Central and State holdings, Alpha Ltd. **is a Government Company.** (The trap is to look only at the 45% Central holding and conclude "no.")

**(ii) Foreign Company — Section 2(42):** A "foreign company" means any company or body corporate **incorporated outside India** which (a) has a **place of business in India** — whether by itself or through an agent, physically **or through electronic mode** — and (b) **conducts any business activity in India in any other manner.** After the clarifying rules, **business through electronic mode** (online transactions with Indian customers, data servers, etc.) is expressly covered even without a physical office. **Application:** Beta Ltd., incorporated in Singapore, conducting online business with Indian customers through electronic mode, **is a foreign company** and must comply with the foreign-company provisions (Sections 379–393), despite having no physical place of business in India.

**(iii) Subsidiary of a Government Company — Section 2(45):** The definition **expressly includes a subsidiary of a Government company**. Gamma Ltd. is 60% held by Delta Ltd., a Government Company; hence Gamma is a **subsidiary of a Government Company** and is therefore **itself a Government Company** within Section 2(45).

**Conclusion:** (i) Government Company (aggregate 53%); (ii) Foreign Company (electronic mode business); (iii) Government Company (subsidiary limb).

**Why this way (the reasoning):** These definitions each turn on a *substance* test that students under-read. For a **Government Company**, the law counts **combined** government holdings (Central + State) because the policy concern is *government control of at least a majority*, irrespective of which tier of government holds it — and it *extends down the chain* to subsidiaries so that public accountability (CAG audit, etc.) is not lost by interposing a holding structure. For a **Foreign Company**, the 2013 Act deliberately widened the net to **electronic mode**, recognising that a company can carry on a substantial Indian business purely online without a physical office — the old "place of business" test would otherwise let digital businesses escape regulation. Both illustrate the chapter's theme: classification follows *economic and control substance*, not superficial form.

*(Full-marks tip: For (i) stress aggregation of Central + State to cross 51%; for (ii) cite "electronic mode"; for (iii) cite the "includes a subsidiary" limb. Deduction: concluding Alpha is not a Government Company by looking only at the 45% Central stake.)*

---

### Q16. Ch: Incorporation of Company — Pre-Incorporation Contracts & Promoter Liability (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Before Skyline Ltd. was incorporated, its promoter Mr. Verma entered into a contract, purportedly on behalf of "Skyline Ltd. (under incorporation)," to purchase land for the company's factory. The land-owner delivered possession. After incorporation, Skyline Ltd. used the land but later refused to pay, contending: (a) the company was not in existence when the contract was made and so is not bound; (b) even if it wished, it cannot "ratify" a contract made before it existed. The land-owner sues both the company and Mr. Verma. Advise on the enforceability of the contract and the promoter's personal liability.

**Answer:**
**Governing principle — Pre-incorporation (preliminary) contracts; the company's non-existence at the time of contracting; and Sections 15 & 19 of the Specific Relief Act, 1963.**

**Common-law position (the starting point):** A company, before incorporation, is **not a legal person**; it cannot be a party to a contract, cannot appoint an agent, and cannot **ratify** a pre-incorporation contract after birth (there was no principal in existence when the contract was made). Consequently, at common law the company is **not automatically bound**, and the **promoter who contracted "on behalf of" the company is personally liable** on the contract (*Kelner v. Baxter*).

**Statutory modification — Specific Relief Act, 1963:**
- **Section 15(h):** Where the promoters of a company have, before its incorporation, entered into a contract **for the purposes of the company**, and such contract is **warranted by the terms of its incorporation**, the **company may enforce** the contract — *provided* the company has **accepted the contract** and **communicated such acceptance** to the other party.
- **Section 19(e):** Correspondingly, such a contract may be **enforced against the company** (by the other party) if the company has **adopted/accepted** it after incorporation and it was warranted by the terms of incorporation.

**Application:** The land purchase was for the company's factory — clearly *for the purposes of* and *warranted by* Skyline's incorporation. After incorporation, **Skyline used the land**, which evidences **adoption/acceptance** of the contract. Under Sections 15(h)/19(e), the contract is therefore **enforceable BY and AGAINST Skyline Ltd.**, notwithstanding that the company did not exist when it was made. The company's plea that "it cannot ratify" is technically true at common law but is **displaced** by the Specific Relief Act's *adoption* mechanism — and by *conduct* (using the land), the company has adopted it. Contention (a) and (b) therefore **fail**.

**Promoter's liability:** If the company had **not** adopted the contract, Mr. Verma would remain **personally liable** (he purported to contract for a non-existent principal). Since the company has adopted it, the land-owner can enforce against the **company**; the promoter is discharged to the extent the company is substituted, subject to the terms of adoption.

**Conclusion:** The contract is enforceable against Skyline Ltd. by virtue of its post-incorporation adoption (use of the land) under Section 19(e) of the Specific Relief Act; the land-owner will succeed against the company. Absent adoption, Mr. Verma would have been personally liable.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The puzzle is that agency requires a *principal in existence* at the time of the act — so, logically, a not-yet-born company can neither authorise the promoter beforehand nor ratify afterward (ratification relates back to a moment when there was no principal). Common law resolved the unfairness by making the **promoter personally liable** so the outsider is not left remediless. Indian law went further through the **Specific Relief Act's "adoption" device**: instead of the impossible "ratification," the incorporated company may *adopt* the contract *afresh* — a new acceptance by a now-existing person — which can be shown by express communication *or by conduct* (here, taking the benefit by using the land). The mischief being cured is that a company should not be allowed to **enjoy the benefit** of a promoter's pre-incorporation contract while **disclaiming the burden**. That is why using the land estops Skyline from denying the contract.

*(Full-marks tip: Cite Kelner v. Baxter for promoter liability AND Sections 15(h)/19(e) of the Specific Relief Act for enforceability by/against the company on adoption. Marks for spotting that "use of the land" = adoption by conduct. Deduction: stopping at "a company cannot ratify" without the Specific Relief Act cure.)*

---

### Q17. Ch: Incorporation of Company — Effect of Registration, MOA vs AOA & Doctrine of Constructive Notice (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Titan Ltd.'s Articles of Association contain a clause requiring that **all cheques above ₹5 lakh be signed by two directors jointly**, and its Memorandum states an objects clause limited to "textile manufacturing and trading." Consider:
(i) A supplier accepted a ₹7 lakh cheque signed by only one director, which later bounced; the supplier claims the company is bound because he did not know of the two-signature requirement. 
(ii) The company entered a contract to trade in textiles through an agent whose appointment breached an internal Board procedure in the Articles.
(iii) The company seeks to enter the pharmaceuticals business relying on a clause in its **Articles** (not the Memorandum) permitting "any lawful business." Advise on each.

**Answer:**
**Governing framework — Section 10 (effect of memorandum and articles: they bind the company and members as a contract), Section 399 (public inspection ⇒ constructive notice), the hierarchy MOA > AOA, and the doctrines of constructive notice and indoor management.**

**(i) Two-signature clause — constructive notice binds the supplier:** The two-director signing requirement is contained in the **Articles**, a **public document** open to inspection under Section 399. Under the **doctrine of constructive notice**, every person dealing with the company is **deemed to have read and understood** the Articles. The supplier is therefore **deemed to know** that a ₹7 lakh cheque needs **two** signatures. Accepting a cheque signed by only one director is at his own peril; he **cannot** plead ignorance of a public document. The company is **not bound** to honour the improperly-signed instrument on the strength of that single signature. The supplier's claim **fails**.

**(ii) Agent's appointment breaching an internal Board procedure — indoor management protects the outsider:** Trading in textiles is **within the objects** (intra vires). Whether the *internal Board procedure* for appointing the agent was followed is an **indoor** matter. Under the **rule in Turquand's case (doctrine of indoor management)**, an outsider dealing in good faith is entitled to **assume that the company's internal procedures have been duly complied with**, and need not verify them. Provided the outsider had no knowledge of the irregularity, the **contract is binding** on the company. (Constructive notice fixes him only with the *contents* of the public documents, not with whether an *internal step* actually occurred.)

**(iii) Pharmaceuticals via an Articles clause — MOA prevails over AOA:** The **Memorandum** limits the objects to **textile manufacturing and trading**; the **Articles** cannot enlarge the objects, because in the **hierarchy of constitutional documents the Memorandum is supreme** and the Articles are **subordinate** to it (and to the Act). A clause in the Articles permitting "any lawful business" is **inoperative to the extent it conflicts with the Memorandum**. Entering the pharmaceuticals business would be **ultra vires the company** (outside the objects clause) and **void** — the Articles cannot cure it. To do so lawfully, Titan must first **alter the objects clause of the Memorandum** by special resolution under Section 13. The reliance on the Articles clause **fails**.

**Conclusion:** (i) supplier bound by constructive notice — company not liable; (ii) contract binding under indoor management; (iii) pharmaceuticals is ultra vires — the Articles cannot override the Memorandum; alter the MOA first.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** This question threads together the whole architecture of a company's constitution. **Section 10** makes the MOA and AOA a binding contract; **Section 399** makes them public, which is *why* constructive notice attaches — the price of that public availability is that outsiders are charged with their contents (part (i)). **Indoor management is the counter-balance** — outsiders are charged only with what is *visible* (the documents), not with the *invisible* internal workings, so they may assume procedural regularity (part (ii)). Finally, the **MOA-over-AOA hierarchy** exists because the Memorandum is the company's **fundamental charter defining its capacity and relationship with the outside world**, whereas the Articles are the **internal rulebook**; a subordinate rulebook cannot expand the very capacity the charter confines — allowing it would resurrect the ultra vires mischief the objects clause is meant to prevent (part (iii)). Recognising *which* document and *which* doctrine governs each limb is exactly what separates a top-band answer from a muddled one.

*(Full-marks tip: Correctly route each limb — constructive notice (public document), indoor management (internal procedure), and MOA-supremacy/ultra vires (Articles cannot override Memorandum). Cite Sections 10 & 399 and Turquand. Deduction: letting the Articles' "any lawful business" clause validate the pharmaceuticals venture, or applying indoor management to the two-signature clause in (i).)*

### Q18. Ch: Share Capital & Debentures — Further Issue of Capital (Sec 62), Rights & Renunciation (Marks: 10) [Problem]
**Question:** Vistara Manufacturing Ltd. (a public company) has a paid-up equity share capital of ₹50,00,000 divided into 5,00,000 equity shares of ₹10 each. To fund expansion the Board proposes to raise ₹40,00,000 of fresh equity. The Board's plan is set out below. Examine the validity of each element and compute the entitlement, treating the rights issue at par.

| Element of the Board's plan | Particulars |
|---|---|
| (a) Rights offer to existing shareholders | 3,00,000 new shares offered pro-rata; letter of offer gives **10 days** to accept |
| (b) Renunciation | Board's offer letter states the right is **NOT renounceable** |
| (c) Unsubscribed portion | 40,000 shares not taken up; Board wishes to allot these to a friendly investor at par |
| (d) Balance 1,00,000 shares | Board wishes to allot preferentially to a strategic PE investor by an **ordinary resolution**, price to be fixed by the Board itself |

Mr. Rao holds 20,000 equity shares. State his rights-entitlement. Advise the Board on the legality of (a)–(d).

**Solution:**

**WN-1 — Rao's rights entitlement (Sec 62(1)(a)):** Rights must be offered *in proportion to existing paid-up capital*. Ratio of rights = 3,00,000 new : 5,00,000 existing = 3 : 5. Rao holds 20,000 shares → entitlement = 20,000 × 3/5 = **12,000 shares**.

**WN-2 — Notice period:** Sec 62(1)(a)(i) requires the offer to remain open **not less than 15 days and not more than 30 days**; a lesser period is allowed only if 90% of members agree in writing. The Board's 10-day window is therefore **invalid**.

**WN-3 — Renunciation:** Sec 62(1)(a)(ii) mandates that the offer *shall* include a right of renunciation in favour of any other person, unless the articles otherwise provide. A blanket "non-renounceable" clause in the offer letter (with no enabling article) is **invalid**.

**WN-4 — Unsubscribed 40,000 shares:** Sec 62(1)(a)(iii) permits the Board, after the offer lapses/is declined, to dispose of the shares "in such manner which is **not disadvantageous** to the shareholders and the company." Allotment to a friendly investor **at par** is permissible only if par is not disadvantageous; if the market/fair value exceeds par, the Board must justify the price. Route is legal in principle; **pricing must not prejudice** members.

**WN-5 — Preferential 1,00,000 shares:** Allotment to persons other than existing holders under Sec 62(1)(c) requires a **special resolution** and a price determined by the report of a **registered valuer** (Rule 13, Companies (Share Capital & Debentures) Rules). An ordinary resolution with Board-fixed price is **invalid**.

| Element | Verdict | Governing provision |
|---|---|---|
| (a) 10-day window | Invalid — need 15–30 days | Sec 62(1)(a)(i) |
| (b) Non-renounceable | Invalid — renunciation is a statutory right | Sec 62(1)(a)(ii) |
| (c) Unsubscribed disposal | Valid if price not disadvantageous | Sec 62(1)(a)(iii) |
| (d) Preferential allotment | Invalid — needs SR + registered valuer | Sec 62(1)(c) + Rule 13 |

**Answer:** Rao is entitled to **12,000 shares**. Elements (a), (b) and (d) are illegal as framed; (c) is permissible only at a non-disadvantageous price.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Sec 62 exists to protect the *proportionate ownership and voting power* of existing shareholders — the "pre-emptive right." Every safeguard maps to that mischief: pro-rata offer preserves each holder's percentage; the 15–30 day window gives a real chance to fund the call; renunciation lets a holder who cannot pay still monetise the value of the right instead of forfeiting it to the company cheaply. The tempting shortcut — a quick preferential allotment by ordinary resolution at a Board-set price — is exactly what the section forbids for *outsiders*, because it dilutes existing holders and can transfer value at an under-priced rate; hence the higher bar of a special resolution plus an independent registered-valuer price. Confusing route (a) [rights] with route (c) [preferential] is the classic error: outsider allotment can never be done merely because the rights portion was under-subscribed unless the "not disadvantageous" test is met.

*(Full-marks tip: examiners reward citing the exact sub-clause for each defect and the 90%-consent escape for the notice period. Marks are lost for giving only "Sec 62" without distinguishing (1)(a) rights from (1)(c) preferential, and for forgetting the registered-valuer requirement.)*

---

### Q19. Ch: Share Capital & Debentures — Buy-back of Shares (Sec 68): Maximum Computation (Marks: 8) [Problem]
**Question:** The Board of Meridian Alloys Ltd. wishes to buy back its equity shares. The audited figures are below. Compute (i) the maximum buy-back the **Board can approve on its own**, (ii) the maximum buy-back with a **special resolution**, and (iii) test the **post-buy-back debt-equity** condition assuming a buy-back of ₹7,00,000.

| Particulars | Amount (₹) |
|---|---|
| Paid-up equity share capital | 30,00,000 |
| Free reserves (general reserve) | 15,00,000 |
| Securities premium account | 10,00,000 |
| Secured + unsecured borrowings (existing) | 90,00,000 |

**Solution:**

**WN-1 — "Free reserves" base for buy-back:** For Sec 68, free reserves *include the securities premium account*. Aggregate of paid-up capital + free reserves = 30,00,000 + 15,00,000 + 10,00,000 = **₹55,00,000**.

**WN-2 — Board (no-SR) route [proviso to Sec 68(2)]:** Board alone may buy back up to **10%** of (paid-up equity capital + free reserves) = 10% × 55,00,000 = **₹5,50,000**.

**WN-3 — Special-resolution route [Sec 68(2)(c)]:** Up to **25%** of aggregate paid-up capital and free reserves = 25% × 55,00,000 = **₹13,75,000**.

**WN-4 — Equity-specific ceiling [proviso to Sec 68(2)(c)]:** In one financial year the buy-back of *equity* shares shall not exceed **25% of total paid-up equity capital** = 25% × 30,00,000 = **₹7,50,000**. This is *lower* than WN-3, so it **binds**. Maximum with SR = **₹7,50,000**.

**WN-5 — Debt-equity test [Sec 68(2)(d)] for a ₹7,00,000 buy-back:** After buy-back, borrowings must be ≤ 2 × (paid-up capital + free reserves) *post buy-back*.
