A buy-back of ₹7,00,000 reduces owned funds by ₹7,00,000 → post-buy-back base = 55,00,000 − 7,00,000 = 48,00,000. Permissible debt = 2 × 48,00,000 = 96,00,000. Existing debt 90,00,000 ≤ 96,00,000 → **condition satisfied**.

| Route | Ceiling formula | Amount (₹) |
|---|---|---|
| Board only | 10% of (55 lakh) | 5,50,000 |
| Special resolution — 25% aggregate | 25% of (55 lakh) | 13,75,000 |
| Equity cap (binding with SR) | 25% of paid-up equity (30 lakh) | 7,50,000 |
| Debt-equity (post ₹7 lakh buy-back) | 2 : 1 satisfied | OK |

**Answer:** (i) Board-only maximum = **₹5,50,000**; (ii) with special resolution = **₹7,50,000** (the 25%-of-equity cap bites before the 25%-of-aggregate cap); (iii) a ₹7,00,000 buy-back satisfies the 2:1 debt-equity test.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Buy-back returns capital to shareholders, so the law caps it to protect creditors and prevent hollowing-out of the capital base. There are *two independent size tests* and a student must apply both and pick the lower — the "25% of aggregate funds" test guards the total, while the "25% of paid-up equity in a year" test specifically throttles how fast equity can be shrunk. Here the equity test (₹7.5 lakh) is stricter than the aggregate test (₹13.75 lakh); taking the larger figure is the classic trap. The debt-equity condition is tested *after* the buy-back because paying cash out reduces owned funds and mechanically worsens gearing — so it must be checked on the diminished base, not the pre-buy-back base. Including securities premium inside "free reserves" is deliberate: premium is a realised, non-distributable-as-dividend reserve that the Act nonetheless permits as a buy-back source under Sec 68(1).

*(Full-marks tip: the examiner awards the mark for identifying that the equity-specific 25% cap over-rides the aggregate 25% cap, and for testing debt-equity on the reduced base. Losing marks: forgetting securities premium in the base, or omitting the Board-vs-SR distinction.)*

---

### Q20. Ch: Share Capital & Debentures — Reduction of Capital (Sec 66) (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Sterling Textiles Ltd. has accumulated losses and a large block of non-productive cash. Its Board proposes three simultaneous steps and claims none needs Tribunal approval because "the shareholders unanimously agree." Examine the validity of each.

| Step | Proposal |
|---|---|
| (a) | Extinguish ₹4 per share of unpaid liability on partly-paid shares (paid-up ₹6, face ₹10) |
| (b) | Pay off ₹3 per share of surplus paid-up capital to shareholders in cash |
| (c) | Cancel ₹2 per share of paid-up capital representing losses not represented by assets |
| (d) | Board says: since a company also buys back under Sec 68, and redeems preference shares under Sec 55, "these are all the same thing, so we can use whichever is convenient" |

**Answer:** *Governing law — Sec 66.* A reduction of share capital requires (i) authority in the **articles**, (ii) a **special resolution**, and (iii) **confirmation by the Tribunal (NCLT)**. Shareholder unanimity does **not** dispense with Tribunal confirmation, because reduction affects **creditors**, whose interests only the Tribunal can safeguard (via the objection/settlement process under Sec 66(2)).

- **(a) Extinguishing unpaid liability** — a recognised mode under Sec 66(1)(a): reducing/extinguishing liability on shares not paid up. Valid *only with* NCLT confirmation. This mode chiefly touches shareholders (relieves them of a future call), yet still needs confirmation.
- **(b) Paying off surplus capital** — mode under Sec 66(1)(c): returning capital in excess of the company's wants. This directly reduces the creditors' cushion, so the creditor-objection machinery of Sec 66(2) squarely applies. Valid only with NCLT confirmation.
- **(c) Cancelling lost capital** — mode under Sec 66(1)(b): cancelling paid-up capital lost/unrepresented by available assets. This is an accounting realignment; here creditor consent is generally not called for because no assets leave the company. Still requires SR + NCLT confirmation.
- **(d)** Wrong. Sec 66 excludes buy-back: reduction under Sec 66 **does not apply to buy-back under Sec 68**, and redemption of redeemable preference shares under Sec 55 is a *separate* mechanism that (being out of profits or fresh issue with a Capital Redemption Reserve) is **not** treated as a reduction of capital. They are not interchangeable.

**Conclusion:** All three real steps (a)–(c) are permissible *modes* but every one needs articles + special resolution + **NCLT confirmation**; the "unanimity removes Tribunal" argument fails. Step (d)'s "pick any route" premise is legally wrong.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Capital is the creditors' guarantee fund — the doctrine of "maintenance of capital." Any transaction that gives capital back to members, or that could, is policed so creditors are not silently stripped of security. That is why Sec 66 is *court-supervised*: the Tribunal alone can require the company to secure/settle creditor claims before confirming. Members cannot waive that protection by unanimity because it is not *their* protection to waive. The reason buy-back (Sec 68) and preference-redemption (Sec 55) sit outside Sec 66 is that each has its *own* built-in creditor safeguards — CRR creation, source restrictions, and quantitative caps — so Parliament provided self-contained regimes rather than routing them through the Tribunal. Treating them as fungible ignores those distinct guardrails.

*(Full-marks tip: state that NCLT confirmation is non-negotiable even on unanimity, and correctly map each of (a)/(b)/(c) to the three modes in Sec 66(1). Weak answers describe reduction generally without distinguishing the creditor impact of "paying off" vs "cancelling lost" capital.)*

---

### Q21. Ch: Share Capital & Debentures — Variation of Class Rights (Sec 48) (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Kaveri Power Ltd. has two classes of shares: 8% preference shares (₹20,00,000) and equity shares (₹80,00,000). The company wishes to (a) reduce the preference dividend rate from 8% to 6%, and (b) do so by a resolution passed only in the **general meeting of equity holders**, arguing preference holders "are a minority." One preference shareholder holding 15% of the preference class objects. Advise on validity and remedies.

**Answer:** *Governing law — Sec 48.* Where the share capital is divided into different classes, the rights attached to any class may be varied only (i) with the **consent in writing of holders of not less than three-fourths of that class**, or (ii) by a **special resolution passed at a separate meeting of the holders of that class** — and only if variation is permitted by the terms of issue or the articles.

- Reducing the preference dividend from 8% to 6% is unquestionably a **variation of the rights** of the preference class (dividend rate is a core class right).
- It therefore requires the **separate class meeting** of preference shareholders with a 3/4 special resolution — a resolution of *equity* holders cannot bind the preference class. The Board's "minority" argument is irrelevant; class rights are protected *precisely because* the class is numerically small.
- **Remedy for the objector (Sec 48(2)):** even if the variation *were* duly passed by 3/4 of the class, dissentients holding **not less than 10%** of that class who did not consent may apply to the **Tribunal to cancel the variation within 21 days** of the consent/resolution. The objector holds 15% > 10%, so he has *locus standi*. The Tribunal's decision binds all. Pending disposal, the variation does not take effect.

**Conclusion:** Route (b) is invalid — the variation needs the preference class's own 3/4 consent/SR, not an equity-holders' resolution. The 15% objector can move the Tribunal under Sec 48(2) within 21 days.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** A "class right" is a bargain struck when that class subscribed — preference holders accepted lower/limited upside in exchange for a fixed, prior dividend. Letting another class (equity), whose interests are *opposed*, vote away that bargain would destroy the very idea of class protection. Hence Sec 48 insists the *affected* class consents, by a super-majority, in its *own* meeting. The additional 10%-dissentient Tribunal remedy exists because even a 3/4 in-class majority can be captured or coerced; it gives a real minority a judicial safety-valve. The trap here is thinking numerical minority weakens their rights — it is the reverse: the smaller and more vulnerable the class, the more the section shields it.

*(Full-marks tip: examiner wants the two alternative modes of variation, the 3/4 threshold, and the 10%/21-day Tribunal remedy with the correct time limit. Common deduction: quoting Sec 47 (voting rights) instead of Sec 48, or omitting the 21-day window.)*

---

### Q22. Ch: Share Capital & Debentures — Bonus Shares (Sec 63): Permissible Sources (Marks: 5) [Theory]
**Question:** The Board of Nilgiri Foods Ltd. resolves to issue fully-paid bonus shares in the ratio 1:2. It proposes to capitalise, in this order: (i) revaluation reserve created on upward revaluation of land; (ii) securities premium; (iii) capital redemption reserve; and (iv) general reserve. There are partly-paid shares outstanding and the company has defaulted on a term-loan interest instalment. Examine which sources and conditions are valid.

**Answer:** *Governing law — Sec 63.*

**Permissible sources (Sec 63(1)):** bonus shares may be issued out of (a) **free reserves**, (b) the **securities premium account**, and (c) the **capital redemption reserve account**. Therefore items (ii), (iii) and (iv) are valid sources.

**Prohibited source (proviso to Sec 63(1)):** bonus shares shall **not** be issued by capitalising reserves created by the **revaluation of assets**. Item (i) — revaluation reserve on land — is an **unrealised, notional** gain and is expressly barred.

**Conditions (Sec 63(2)) that must all be satisfied:**
1. Authorised by the **articles**;
2. Recommended by the Board and **authorised in general meeting**;
3. The company has **not defaulted** in payment of interest/principal on fixed deposits or debt securities;
4. The company has **not defaulted** in payment of statutory dues to employees (PF, gratuity, bonus);
5. **Partly-paid shares are made fully paid-up** before the bonus issue;
6. The bonus, once recommended, **cannot be withdrawn**.

**Conclusion:** Sources (ii)–(iv) are valid; source (i) revaluation reserve is illegal. Additionally, the term-loan interest **default must be cured** (condition 3) and the **partly-paid shares must first be made fully paid** (condition 5); until then the bonus issue cannot proceed.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** A bonus issue merely reclassifies reserves into share capital — no cash comes in, no assets change. So the law only allows *genuine, realised* reserves to be capitalised. Revaluation reserve is barred because it represents an *unrealised* book-up of asset values; converting it into share capital would create "watered stock" — shares backed by a paper gain that may never crystallise, misleading creditors and investors about the real capital cushion. The default and partly-paid conditions embody a fairness principle: a company must honour existing money obligations and equalise existing shareholders (no one should ride a bonus while still owing calls) before distributing fresh paid-up capital gratis.

*(Full-marks tip: the single most-tested point is the revaluation-reserve bar — state it explicitly and explain "unrealised." Listing the no-default and partly-paid conditions earns the remaining marks; forgetting the "bonus cannot be withdrawn" rule is a common small loss.)*

---

### Q23. Ch: Acceptance of Deposits — Deposits from Members: Private Company Limit (Sec 73 + Rules) (Marks: 8) [Problem]
**Question:** Ganga Traders Pvt. Ltd. (an ordinary private company, not a start-up, not exempt) wants to accept money from its members. Its latest audited balance sheet shows the figures below. It already holds ₹40,00,000 of deposits from members accepted last year and still outstanding. Compute the **maximum fresh deposits** it may now accept from members.

| Particulars | Amount (₹) |
|---|---|
| Paid-up share capital | 40,00,000 |
| Free reserves | 20,00,000 |
| Securities premium account | 10,00,000 |
| Revaluation reserve | 8,00,000 |
| Amount received from a director (out of her own funds, with written declaration) | 12,00,000 |

**Solution:**

**WN-1 — Aggregate base [Rule 3(3), Companies (Acceptance of Deposits) Rules, 2014]:** the members-deposit ceiling for a private company is a percentage of the aggregate of **paid-up share capital + free reserves + securities premium account**. Revaluation reserve is *not* a free reserve and is **excluded**. Base = 40,00,000 + 20,00,000 + 10,00,000 = **₹70,00,000**.

**WN-2 — Applicable percentage:** an ordinary private company may accept from members up to **100%** of the aggregate base = 100% × 70,00,000 = **₹70,00,000** (overall ceiling on outstanding member-deposits).

**WN-3 — Director's money is not a deposit:** money received from a director who furnishes a written declaration that it is her own funds and not borrowed is an **exempt deposit** [Rule 2(1)(c)(viii)]. The ₹12,00,000 does **not** count against the ceiling.

**WN-4 — Head-room:** Ceiling 70,00,000 − existing member-deposits 40,00,000 = **₹30,00,000**.

| Step | Amount (₹) |
|---|---|
| Aggregate base (excl. revaluation reserve) | 70,00,000 |
| Ceiling @ 100% | 70,00,000 |
| Less: existing outstanding member-deposits | (40,00,000) |
| **Maximum fresh deposits from members** | **30,00,000** |

**Answer:** Ganga Traders Pvt. Ltd. may accept a **maximum of ₹30,00,000** in fresh deposits from members. The director's ₹12,00,000 is an exempt deposit and does not consume any limit.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The deposit rules ring-fence how much a company can borrow from its "insiders" (members) so that a private company cannot become a disguised, unregulated finance house. The ceiling is pegged to *own funds* — paid-up capital plus genuinely distributable/realised reserves plus securities premium — because those represent the real net-worth cushion available to repay depositors. Revaluation reserve is stripped out for the same reason it is barred for bonus: it is an unrealised book entry, not repayment capacity. Director's money is exempt *only* with the "own funds, not borrowed" declaration, because the mischief the rules target is public money routed through directors; if it is the director's genuine capital, there is no such risk. The classic error is to (i) include revaluation reserve in the base, and (ii) forget that existing outstanding deposits eat into the *same* 100% ceiling — the limit is on the *outstanding total*, not on each fresh tranche.

*(Full-marks tip: show the base build-up line-by-line, exclude revaluation reserve with a reason, and net off existing deposits. Marks lost for treating the director's money as a deposit or applying 35% (the non-eligible-company rate) instead of 100%.)*

---

### Q24. Ch: Acceptance of Deposits — Eligible Company: Members + Public Limits (Sec 76 + Rules) (Marks: 10) [Problem]
**Question:** Indus Infra Ltd. is a public company with a net worth of ₹150 crore, thus an **eligible company** under Sec 76. From its balance sheet: paid-up share capital ₹120 crore, free reserves ₹60 crore, securities premium ₹20 crore. It has NOT yet accepted any deposits. Compute the maximum deposits it may accept (i) from **members** and (ii) from the **public**. Also decide whether the following receipts are "deposits" that consume these limits.

| Receipt | Amount |
|---|---|
| (p) Advance received against supply of goods, to be adjusted within 300 days | ₹5 crore |
| (q) Amount raised by issue of secured, listed non-convertible debentures | ₹30 crore |
| (r) Share application money pending allotment, allotment to be made in 75 days | ₹4 crore |

**Solution:**

**WN-1 — Aggregate base:** paid-up capital + free reserves + securities premium = 120 + 60 + 20 = **₹200 crore**.

**WN-2 — Members limit [Rule 3(4)]:** an eligible company may accept deposits from **members** up to **10%** of the aggregate base = 10% × 200 = **₹20 crore**.

**WN-3 — Public limit [Rule 3(5)]:** an eligible company may accept deposits from the **public** up to **25%** of the aggregate base = 25% × 200 = **₹50 crore**.

**WN-4 — Classifying the receipts (Rule 2(1)(c) exclusions):**
- **(p) Advance for goods — 300 days:** an advance for supply of goods is exempt *only if* appropriated against supply within **365 days**. 300 ≤ 365 → **not a deposit** (exempt). Does not consume the limit.
- **(q) Secured listed NCDs:** amounts raised by issue of debentures **secured** by charge / listed and compliant are **not deposits** (secured/convertible/listed debentures are excluded). Does **not** consume the limit.
- **(r) Share application money — 75 days:** exempt only if shares are allotted within **60 days**; if allotment is beyond 60 days and money not refunded within a further 15 days, it becomes a **deposit**. 75 days > 60 → this ₹4 crore is a **deposit** and consumes the limit.

| Particulars | Amount (₹ cr) |
|---|---|
| Aggregate base | 200 |
| Max from members @ 10% | 20 |
| Max from public @ 25% | 50 |
| (p) Advance 300 days — exempt | Nil impact |
| (q) Secured/listed NCD — exempt | Nil impact |
| (r) Share-application money 75 days — is a deposit | 4 |

**Answer:** Indus Infra Ltd. may accept up to **₹20 crore from members** and **₹50 crore from the public**. Items (p) and (q) are not deposits; item (r) of **₹4 crore is a deposit** that must be counted within the applicable limit.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Only an "eligible company" (net worth ≥ ₹100 cr or turnover ≥ ₹500 cr, with a special resolution) may tap the *public* at all — because public depositors are the most vulnerable and only large, well-capitalised companies are trusted with their money; that is why the section splits the limit into a smaller members' bucket (10%) and a larger public bucket (25%). The exclusion tests all turn on *substance and time*: a genuine trade advance is commercial credit, not borrowing — but only if it is actually consumed against supply within 365 days, else it is a disguised loan. Share-application money is meant to become shares; the 60-day allotment / 15-day refund window prevents companies from parking public money indefinitely under the "pending allotment" label. Secured/listed debentures are already policed by charge-creation and listing rules, so they sit outside the deposit regime. The traps are the day-count thresholds — 365 for advances, 60(+15) for application money — mixing them up flips a receipt in or out of the deposit net.

*(Full-marks tip: state both limits with the 10%/25% split *and* correctly time-test each receipt with the right threshold. Biggest deductions: applying 35% (non-eligible rate) instead of the 10%+25% eligible split, and treating the 300-day advance as a deposit.)*

---

### Q25. Ch: Acceptance of Deposits — Exempt Deposits: Director vs Relative, Advances (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Deccan Springs Ltd. (a non-eligible public company) receives the four amounts below in one quarter. Its officer treats all four as "exempt" and outside the deposit rules. Examine the correctness of each treatment.

| Receipt | Facts |
|---|---|
| (a) ₹10,00,000 from Mr. X, a **director**, who gives a written declaration the money is from his own funds and not borrowed | |
| (b) ₹8,00,000 from Mrs. Y, **wife (relative) of a director** — the company is a *public* company — with a similar declaration | |
| (c) ₹6,00,000 advance towards a long-term supply contract; supply will begin only after 20 months | |
| (d) ₹3,00,000 as a **non-interest-bearing security deposit** from an employee, equal to 4 months' salary | |

**Answer:** *Governing law — Rule 2(1)(c) exemptions.*

- **(a) Director's own funds — EXEMPT.** Money from a director accompanied by a written "own funds, not borrowed" declaration is expressly excluded [Rule 2(1)(c)(viii)]. The officer is correct. *(The company must disclose it in the Board's report.)*
- **(b) Relative of director — depends on company type.** The exemption for a **relative of a director** applies to a **private company**, not a public company. Deccan Springs is a *public* company, so Mrs. Y's ₹8,00,000 is **NOT exempt** on the "director's relative" ground — it is a **deposit**. The officer is wrong.
- **(c) Advance for supply — beyond 365 days — NOT exempt.** An advance for supply of goods/provision of services is exempt only if appropriated within **365 days** of acceptance. Supply here starts after 20 months (>365 days), so the advance is treated as a **deposit** to the extent not so appropriated. The officer is wrong.
- **(d) Employee security deposit — conditionally exempt.** A non-interest-bearing security deposit from an employee is exempt only up to **the employee's annual salary** under a contract of employment. ₹3,00,000 = 4 months' salary, which is **within** annual salary → **EXEMPT**. The officer is correct.

**Conclusion:** (a) and (d) are correctly treated as exempt; (b) and (c) are **deposits** and must be brought within the deposit limits and compliance (DPT-3, etc.).

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Each exemption is drafted around a specific risk. Director's own money carries no "public money" risk, so it is exempt with a self-certifying declaration. But a director's *relative* in a *public* company is one step removed — public companies deal with dispersed outside stakeholders, so the law refuses to let money flow in through relatives without deposit discipline; it grants that relaxation only to private companies where ownership and management overlap. Trade advances are exempt only while they remain *genuinely commercial*: a 365-day cap stops companies parking borrowings as "advances" for years. Employee security deposits are exempt only up to annual salary and only if interest-free, because beyond that they start to look like a return-seeking loan rather than a bona-fide employment security. The distinction that decides (b) — private vs public company — is the single most missed point.

*(Full-marks tip: examiner rewards spotting that the "relative of director" exemption is **private-company-only**, and applying the 365-day and "annual salary" caps. Common error: exempting the relative's money in a public company.)*

---

### Q26. Ch: Share Capital & Debentures — Buy-back Prohibitions & Financial Assistance (Sec 68/70/67) (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Examine the validity of each action of Orbit Chemicals Ltd. under the buy-back and capital-integrity provisions.

| Action | Facts |
|---|---|
| (a) | The company buys back shares and, within **8 months**, proposes a **second** buy-back |
| (b) | The company buys back shares out of the **proceeds of a fresh issue of the same kind of shares** made two months earlier |
| (c) | The company defaulted in repayment of a **matured deposit**; three months after curing the default it proceeds to buy back |
| (d) | The company gives a **loan to Mr. Z** to enable him to **purchase its own shares** in the market |

**Answer:** *Governing law — Sec 68, 70 and 67.*

- **(a) Second buy-back within 8 months — INVALID.** Sec 68(2) proviso: no offer of buy-back shall be made within **one year** from the closure of the preceding buy-back. Eight months violates the mandatory cooling-off; the second buy-back is impermissible.
- **(b) Buy-back from proceeds of fresh issue of same kind — INVALID.** Sec 68(1) proviso: no buy-back shall be made **out of the proceeds of an earlier issue of the same kind of shares**. Funding an equity buy-back from a recent equity issue is barred.
- **(c) Buy-back after curing deposit default — conditionally VALID.** Sec 70(1) prohibits buy-back if the company has defaulted in repayment of deposits/interest, redemption of debentures/preference shares, or payment of dividend/term-loan. **However**, the disqualification **ceases** once the default is **remedied and three years have elapsed** after such default ceased. Here only *three months* have passed, so the buy-back is still **prohibited** — the officer's assumption that curing alone suffices is wrong.
- **(d) Loan to buy its own shares — INVALID.** Sec 67 prohibits a company (other than as specifically permitted) from giving, directly or indirectly, **financial assistance** — loan, guarantee, security — to any person to purchase its own shares. The loan to Mr. Z is illegal financial assistance.

**Conclusion:** All four actions are invalid: (a) breaches the 1-year gap, (b) breaches the same-kind-proceeds bar, (c) is caught by the 3-year cooling period after a deposit default under Sec 70, and (d) is prohibited financial assistance under Sec 67.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Buy-back and the financial-assistance ban are two faces of "capital maintenance." The 1-year gap and the same-kind-proceeds bar (a) and (b) exist to stop a company **churning** its capital — issuing shares, buying them back, re-issuing — which would let insiders manipulate share price and EPS while giving creditors no stable capital base. (c) reflects a *priority-of-creditors* logic: a company that has failed to pay its depositors/lenders may not hand cash to shareholders instead; the law even imposes a three-year quarantine after the default is cured, because recent default signals fragility. (d) targets the oldest abuse — a company propping up demand for its *own* shares with its *own* money, which both waters the capital and can rig the market; hence the flat prohibition on financial assistance. The trap in (c) is assuming "default cured = eligible"; the three-year clock is the point.

*(Full-marks tip: cite the *1-year* gap, the *same-kind-proceeds* bar, the *3-year* post-default cooling under Sec 70, and Sec 67 for financial assistance. Losing marks: confusing the 1-year *gap between buy-backs* with the 1-year period to *complete* a buy-back, and missing the 3-year rule in (c).)*

---

### Q27. Ch: Acceptance of Deposits — Deposit Repayment Reserve & Compliance Conditions (Marks: 5) [Theory]
**Question:** Sarayu Cements Ltd. accepts deposits from its members. Explain the safeguards it must maintain regarding (i) the **Deposit Repayment Reserve Account**, (ii) the **tenure** of deposits, and (iii) **credit rating**. State what the Deposit Repayment Reserve may and may not be used for.

**Answer:** *Governing law — Sec 73(2)/76 and the Companies (Acceptance of Deposits) Rules, 2014.*

**(i) Deposit Repayment Reserve Account (DRRA):** on or before **30th April** each year, the company must deposit into a **scheduled bank**, in a separate DRRA, a sum **not less than 20%** of the amount of its deposits **maturing during the following financial year**. This sum **cannot be used for any purpose other than repayment of deposits**. It is a ring-fenced liquidity buffer, not general working capital.

**(ii) Tenure:** deposits may be accepted for a term of **not less than 6 months and not more than 36 months**. To meet **short-term** requirements a company may accept deposits for a shorter term, but not less than **3 months**, and such short-term deposits must **not exceed 10%** of the aggregate of paid-up capital, free reserves and securities premium.

**(iii) Credit rating (eligible companies):** an eligible company accepting public deposits must obtain, at the time of inviting deposits and **every year during the tenure**, a **credit rating** from a recognised agency, disclosed in the circular; the rating must not be below the minimum prescribed investment grade.

**Additional standing conditions:** issue of a **circular/advertisement** (Form DPT-1), filing with the Registrar, appointment of a **deposit trustee** (for secured deposits), and a **general/special resolution** as applicable.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The deposit regime is fundamentally about **repayment certainty** for a vulnerable creditor — the depositor. The DRRA is a *pre-funded escrow*: by parking 20% of next year's maturities in a bank account that legally cannot be touched for anything else, the law guarantees that at least a slice of cash physically exists when deposits fall due, instead of relying on the company's future solvency. The 6–36 month tenure prevents both fly-by-night ultra-short borrowing and indefinite locking-in of depositors' money, while the narrow 3-month/10% short-term window is a controlled exception for genuine liquidity needs. Annual credit rating forces continuous, independent disclosure of the company's repayment capacity so depositors are warned if health deteriorates *during* the deposit's life, not just at inception.

*(Full-marks tip: the precise figures earn the marks — 20% of *next year's* maturities, by 30 April, in a scheduled bank, usable only for repayment; 6–36 months tenure. Common losses: saying the DRRA can fund general operations, or misstating the reserve as a percentage of *total* deposits rather than *next-year maturities*.)*

---

### Q28. Ch: Share Capital & Debentures — Shares at Discount (Sec 53) vs Sweat Equity (Sec 54) (Marks: 6) [Problem]
**Question:** Examine the validity of each of the following proposals of Zenith Software Ltd. (face value ₹10 per share).

| Proposal | Facts |
|---|---|
| (a) | Issue 1,00,000 equity shares to the public at ₹8 (a ₹2 discount) to make the issue attractive |
| (b) | Issue **sweat equity shares** to a key engineer at a 40% discount to fair value for her past IP contribution; the company is **3 years old** |
| (c) | Sweat equity proposed in this year = 30% of existing paid-up equity capital; total sweat equity so far (including this) would be 20% of paid-up capital |
| (d) | Issue shares at a discount to a **bank under a statutory debt-restructuring** scheme, converting its loan into equity |

**Answer:** *Governing law — Sec 53 and 54.*

- **(a) Public issue at a discount — VOID.** Sec 53 flatly prohibits issue of shares at a discount; any share issued at a discount is **void**, and the company/officers are penalised. Proposal (a) is illegal.
- **(b) Sweat equity — permissible in principle.** Sec 54 *carves out* sweat equity as a **lawful exception** to Sec 53: shares may be issued to employees/directors at a discount, or for non-cash consideration (know-how, IP, value additions). A company may issue sweat equity even early in life (the "one year since commencement of business" restriction was **removed**), so being 3 years old is no bar. Requires a **special resolution** specifying number, price, consideration and class, and compliance with Rule 8. Valid subject to those conditions.
- **(c) Quantum limit — the 30% breaches the yearly cap.** Sweat equity in a year shall not exceed **15% of existing paid-up equity capital or shares of value ₹5 crore, whichever is higher**, and the **aggregate** shall not exceed **25%** of paid-up equity capital at any time (higher overall ceiling for eligible start-ups). The proposed **30% in one year exceeds the 15% yearly cap** and is invalid to that extent, even though the cumulative 20% is within the 25% aggregate cap.
- **(d) Discounted issue to a bank on debt-restructuring — VALID.** Sec 53(2A)/proviso permits issue of shares at a discount to creditors when debt is converted into equity **pursuant to a statutory resolution/RBI-directed restructuring scheme**. Proposal (d) falls in this exception and is valid.

**Conclusion:** (a) void; (b) valid subject to SR and Rule 8; (c) invalid — breaches the 15% *annual* sweat-equity cap; (d) valid under the debt-restructuring exception.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Sec 53's blanket ban protects the "no watered capital" principle — shares issued below par mean assets received are less than the capital shown, misleading creditors about the cushion. But the law recognises two *legitimate* reasons to go below par: rewarding genuine value-creators (sweat equity, Sec 54) and rescuing distressed companies by converting debt to equity under a supervised scheme. Both are hedged: sweat equity needs a special resolution, valuation and *quantum caps* so it cannot be abused to enrich insiders or dilute outsiders wholesale; the yearly 15% cap and the 25% aggregate cap are *independent* — passing one does not cure a breach of the other, which is exactly the trap in (c). The removal of the "one year old" condition reflects a policy shift to let young, IP-heavy firms reward talent early.

*(Full-marks tip: state that Sec 54 is an *exception* to Sec 53, and apply the *two separate* caps (15% yearly / 25% aggregate) — the answer must flag that the annual cap is breached even though the aggregate is fine. Deduction: treating sweat equity as simply "shares at discount" or forgetting the special-resolution requirement.)*

---

### Q29. Ch: Share Capital & Debentures — Preferential Allotment: Pricing & Valuation (Sec 62(1)(c)) (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Aravalli Steel Ltd. (unlisted public company) proposes to allot 5,00,000 equity shares preferentially to a strategic investor. The Board proposes: (i) price fixed by the Board's own internal estimate of ₹45 per share; (ii) approval by ordinary resolution; (iii) allotment to be completed **14 months** after the resolution; (iv) part of the consideration to be a plant (non-cash) with no valuation report. Examine the validity of the proposal and advise on the correct procedure.

**Answer:** *Governing law — Sec 62(1)(c) read with Rule 13 of the Companies (Share Capital & Debentures) Rules, 2014.*

A preferential offer of shares to **persons other than existing shareholders/employees** requires:

1. **Authorisation by a special resolution** — an *ordinary* resolution is insufficient. Proposal (ii) is **invalid**.
2. **Price determined by the valuation report of a registered valuer** — the Board's own internal estimate of ₹45 cannot substitute for an independent registered-valuer valuation. Proposal (i) is **invalid** as framed; the price must be justified by, and not less than, the valuer's price.
3. **Allotment within 12 months** of the special resolution — Rule 13 requires that allotment be completed within **twelve months**; if not, a fresh special resolution is needed. Proposal (iii)'s **14-month** timeline **breaches** the rule.
4. **Non-cash consideration valued by a registered valuer** — where consideration is other than cash (here, a plant), the assets must be **valued by a registered valuer** and the price/consideration justified; carrying non-cash consideration without a valuation report is **invalid**. Proposal (iv) is **invalid**.

**Correct procedure/advice:** obtain a **special resolution**; obtain a **registered-valuer report** fixing the minimum price and separately valuing the plant; ensure the explanatory statement to the notice discloses the basis of pricing, the identity of the allottee and the intended use; **complete allotment within 12 months**.

**Conclusion:** As framed, every material element (i)–(iv) is defective. The allotment is valid only if restructured with an SR, registered-valuer pricing and non-cash valuation, and completion within 12 months.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** A preferential allotment to an *outsider* is the transaction most prone to value-leakage: insiders can hand a favoured party shares cheaply, diluting and disadvantaging existing shareholders. Sec 62(1)(c) counters this with a *higher democratic bar* (special resolution — 75%) and, crucially, *independent pricing* by a registered valuer so the Board cannot self-serve with a convenient internal number. The 12-month allotment window stops companies keeping an open-ended licence to issue at a stale price while conditions change. Non-cash consideration is valued independently for the same reason — a plant "worth" whatever the Board says could be a channel to over-issue shares against an over-stated asset. The recurring exam trap is treating a preferential *outsider* allotment like a *rights* issue (ordinary resolution, Board pricing); the two are governed by different sub-clauses precisely because the conflict-of-interest is different.

*(Full-marks tip: nail all four defects with the specific requirement (SR, registered valuer, 12-month window, non-cash valuation). Marks are lost for saying "special resolution" but omitting the registered-valuer pricing, which is the pricing-integrity heart of the section.)*

---

### Q30. Ch: Acceptance of Deposits — Share Application Money & Advances as Deemed Deposits (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Kosi Retail Ltd. holds the following balances at year-end. Classify each as a deposit or an exempt receipt, giving the governing time-test.

| Item | Facts |
|---|---|
| (a) | Share application money of ₹6,00,000; the company **has not allotted** shares and it is now **80 days** since receipt, no refund made |
| (b) | Advance of ₹2,00,000 received for supply of goods; supply completed and advance adjusted within **200 days** |
| (c) | Advance of ₹4,00,000 towards providing a service; the underlying agreement was declared **void** by a court, and the amount is retained for **50 days** thereafter |
| (d) | ₹1,50,000 received as an **advance under a long-term project**, refundable, but the necessary statutory **approval lapsed** and money held **9 months** since lapse |

**Answer:** *Governing law — Rule 2(1)(c) provisos.*

- **(a) Share application money, 80 days, no allotment/refund — DEPOSIT.** Application money is exempt only if shares are allotted within **60 days**; if not allotted and not refunded within a further **15 days** (i.e., by day 75), it becomes a **deposit** from day 76. At 80 days with no refund, the ₹6,00,000 is a **deposit**.
- **(b) Trade advance adjusted in 200 days — NOT a deposit.** An advance for supply of goods is exempt if appropriated against supply within **365 days**. 200 ≤ 365 → **exempt**.
- **(c) Advance under a void agreement, retained 50 days — DEPOSIT.** An advance for goods/services is exempt only if it becomes refundable and, once the underlying arrangement/regulatory approval fails, is **refunded within 15 days**. Held 50 days after the contract became void → the ₹4,00,000 is a **deposit**.
- **(d) Project advance, approval lapsed, held 9 months — DEPOSIT.** Where an advance was received for a purpose requiring approval and that approval **lapses/is refused**, the amount is exempt only if refunded within **15 days**; retention for 9 months makes the ₹1,50,000 a **deposit**.

**Conclusion:** (a), (c) and (d) are **deposits**; only (b) is exempt.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The deposit rules chase *substance over label*. Money received for a bona-fide purpose — shares to be allotted, goods/services to be supplied — is legitimately outside the borrowing regime *only while that purpose is being genuinely pursued within a reasonable time*. Once the purpose fails (no allotment, void contract, lapsed approval) and the company nonetheless **keeps the money**, the receipt has, in economic reality, become an interest-free (or convenient) loan from the payer — precisely the "deposit" the rules mean to regulate. Hence each exemption carries a *sunset*: 60(+15) days for application money, 365 days for trade advances, and a 15-day refund clock once an approval-linked advance fails. The examiner's trap is remembering that different receipts have *different* clocks — mixing the 365-day advance test with the 60-day application-money test flips the answer.

*(Full-marks tip: give the exact clock for each item — 60+15 days, 365 days, and the 15-day refund-on-failure rule. The single most common error is applying "365 days" to share application money.)*

---

### Q31. Ch: Share Capital & Debentures — Buy-back Timing, CRR & Cancellation (Sec 68/69) (Marks: 8) [Problem]
**Question:** Solar Ceramics Ltd. completed a buy-back on 1 May 2025. The relevant data appear below. Answer (i)–(iv).

| Particulars | Amount (₹) |
|---|---|
| Face value of equity shares bought back | 5,00,000 |
| Buy-back price paid (total) | 8,00,000 |
| Source: free reserves used | 6,00,000 |
| Source: securities premium used | 2,00,000 |

(i) Within what time must the shares bought back be **physically extinguished**? (ii) What is the **Capital Redemption Reserve (CRR)** to be created and from where? (iii) When may the company make its **next** buy-back? (iv) Can the company **re-issue** the same shares later, and within what period is it barred from making a **fresh issue of the same kind**?

**Solution:**

**WN-1 — Extinguishment of shares [Sec 68(7)]:** shares bought back must be **physically destroyed/extinguished within 7 days** of the last date of completion of the buy-back. Deadline = **8 May 2025**.

**WN-2 — CRR requirement [Sec 69(1)]:** where buy-back is out of **free reserves or securities premium**, a sum equal to the **nominal (face) value** of the shares bought back must be transferred to the **Capital Redemption Reserve Account**. CRR = **₹5,00,000** (the face value, not the price). This preserves the capital base.

**WN-3 — Utilisation of CRR [Sec 69(2)]:** CRR may be used only for **paying up unissued shares to be issued as fully-paid bonus shares**.

**WN-4 — Next buy-back timing [Sec 68(2) proviso]:** no further buy-back offer within **one year** of closure of this buy-back → not before **1 May 2026**.

**WN-5 — Re-issue / fresh issue bar [Sec 68(8)]:** once shares are bought back, the company **cannot make a further issue of the same kind of shares (including allotment of new shares) within 6 months**, except by way of a **bonus issue** or discharge of subsisting obligations (conversion of warrants, ESOPs, sweat equity, preference/debenture conversion). Fresh same-kind issue barred until **1 November 2025**.

| Question | Answer |
|---|---|
| (i) Extinguish shares | Within 7 days → by 8 May 2025 |
| (ii) CRR | ₹5,00,000 (= face value) from free reserves/securities premium |
| (iii) Next buy-back | Not before 1 May 2026 (1-year gap) |
| (iv) Fresh same-kind issue | Barred for 6 months → until 1 Nov 2025 (except bonus/obligations) |

**Answer:** Extinguish by **8 May 2025**; create **CRR of ₹5,00,000** (face value); next buy-back only after **1 May 2026**; no fresh same-kind issue for **6 months** (until 1 Nov 2025) except bonus/subsisting-obligation allotments.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Buy-back returns capital, so Sec 69 forces the company to **quarantine an equal amount** of distributable reserves into CRR — a non-distributable reserve — so that the *total* capital+CRR figure the creditors rely on is unchanged. Critically the transfer equals the **face value**, not the price paid: the premium over face is a distribution to shareholders (funded from reserves), while the face value is the "capital" that must be notionally preserved via CRR. The 7-day extinguishment rule prevents bought-back shares from being quietly re-issued or used to manipulate holdings — they must cease to exist. The 6-month fresh-issue bar and the 1-year gap stop capital churning and price manipulation. The classic error is transferring the *buy-back price* (₹8,00,000) to CRR instead of the *face value* (₹5,00,000).

*(Full-marks tip: the marks turn on CRR = **face value** and the 7-day / 6-month / 1-year clocks. Deduction: using the buy-back price for CRR, or confusing the 6-month fresh-issue bar with the 1-year gap between buy-backs.)*

---

### Q32. Ch: Share Capital & Debentures — Debentures: DRR, Voting & Conversion (Sec 71) (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Examine the validity of the following in relation to Chenab Motors Ltd.'s debentures.

| Item | Facts |
|---|---|
| (a) | The company issues debentures carrying **voting rights** on general resolutions, to make them attractive |
| (b) | The company issues **convertible debentures**, the conversion into shares approved by an **ordinary resolution** in general meeting |
| (c) | The company (a listed company covered by the rules) plans to redeem debentures **without creating any Debenture Redemption Reserve** |
| (d) | A debenture-holder complains the company has failed to redeem on the due date; advise the remedy |

**Answer:** *Governing law — Sec 71 and Rule 18 of the Companies (Share Capital & Debentures) Rules.*

- **(a) Voting rights on debentures — INVALID.** Sec 71(2) expressly provides that **no company shall issue debentures carrying any voting rights**. A debenture is a *creditor* instrument, not a *membership* instrument; conferring voting rights is prohibited.
- **(b) Convertible debentures by ordinary resolution — INVALID.** Where debentures are to be **converted into shares** wholly or partly, the issue must be approved by a **special resolution** in general meeting [Sec 71(1)]. An ordinary resolution is insufficient.
- **(c) Redemption without DRR — depends/broadly INVALID for applicable companies.** Sec 71(4) and Rule 18(7) require creation of a **Debenture Redemption Reserve** out of profits available for dividend (for the companies still required to maintain it), plus the annual **DRR investment/deposit** of a prescribed percentage of debentures maturing in the next year, on or before **30 April**, kept until redemption. A covered company cannot dispense with these safeguards.
- **(d) Failure to redeem — remedy.** On failure to redeem debentures or pay interest when due, the debenture-holder (or the debenture trustee) may apply to the **Tribunal (NCLT)** under **Sec 71(10)**, which may direct the company to **redeem forthwith and pay principal and interest**. The **debenture trustee** (Sec 71(5)) is also duty-bound to protect the holders' interests.

**Conclusion:** (a) and (b) are invalid; (c) cannot skip the DRR/DRR-investment safeguards where applicable; (d) the holder's remedy is a Sec 71(10) application to the NCLT, backed by the debenture trustee.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The debenture provisions rest on one idea: a debenture-holder is a **lender, not an owner**. That is why voting rights are forbidden (owners vote; lenders get paid) and why redemption is fortified with a Debenture Redemption Reserve — a slice of profits set aside so the company does not distribute away the cash needed to repay debt. The special-resolution requirement for *convertible* debentures exists because conversion changes the very nature of the instrument (creditor → shareholder) and dilutes existing members, so it demands the higher 75% consent. The trustee-plus-Tribunal remedy gives holders a collective, enforceable route rather than each fighting alone. The trap in (b) is treating convertible-debenture approval as a routine ordinary resolution.

*(Full-marks tip: state the flat bar on voting rights, the special-resolution requirement for convertibles, and the Sec 71(10) NCLT remedy. Losing marks: forgetting that conversion needs a special resolution, or omitting the debenture trustee's protective role.)*

---

### Q33. Ch: Acceptance of Deposits — Comprehensive Limit Computation with Exempt Stripping (Marks: 10) [Problem]
**Question:** Yamuna Engineering Ltd. is a **non-eligible public company** (net worth below the Sec 76 threshold). It wishes to accept deposits from its members. From its records, compute the **maximum deposits it may accept from members**, after correctly excluding non-deposit receipts.

| Particulars | Amount (₹) |
|---|---|
| Paid-up equity share capital | 60,00,000 |
| Paid-up preference share capital | 10,00,000 |
| Free reserves | 25,00,000 |
| Securities premium account | 15,00,000 |
| Capital reserve (arising on revaluation) | 6,00,000 |
| Existing outstanding deposits from members | 20,00,000 |
| Amount from a director (own funds, declaration filed) | 5,00,000 |
| Secured loan from a scheduled bank | 30,00,000 |

**Solution:**

**WN-1 — Build the aggregate base:** the base for the members-deposit limit = **paid-up share capital + free reserves + securities premium account**. Paid-up share capital includes **both equity and preference** = 60,00,000 + 10,00,000 = 70,00,000.
- Add free reserves 25,00,000 and securities premium 15,00,000.
- **Exclude** the ₹6,00,000 capital reserve arising on **revaluation** (not a free reserve).
- Base = 70,00,000 + 25,00,000 + 15,00,000 = **₹1,10,00,000**.

**WN-2 — Strip out non-deposits (not part of, and not counted against, the limit):**
- Director's ₹5,00,000 (own funds, declaration) → **exempt deposit**, does not consume the limit.
- Secured bank loan ₹30,00,000 → borrowing **from a bank is excluded** from the definition of deposit; irrelevant to the limit.

**WN-3 — Applicable percentage [Rule 3(3)]:** a **non-eligible** company (public or private, other than the 100%-eligible private companies) may accept deposits from members up to **35%** of the aggregate base = 35% × 1,10,00,000 = **₹38,50,000** (ceiling on total outstanding member-deposits).

**WN-4 — Head-room:** Ceiling 38,50,000 − existing outstanding member-deposits 20,00,000 = **₹18,50,000**.

| Step | Amount (₹) |
|---|---|
| Paid-up capital (equity + preference) | 70,00,000 |
| Free reserves | 25,00,000 |
| Securities premium | 15,00,000 |
| Less: revaluation capital reserve (excluded) | — |
| **Aggregate base** | **1,10,00,000** |
| Ceiling @ 35% | 38,50,000 |
| Less: existing member-deposits | (20,00,000) |
| **Maximum fresh deposits from members** | **18,50,000** |

**Answer:** Yamuna Engineering Ltd. may accept a **maximum of ₹18,50,000** in fresh deposits from members. The director's ₹5,00,000 and the bank loan of ₹30,00,000 are not deposits and do not consume the limit.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Three judgment calls decide this problem. First, "paid-up share capital" in the base means **all** paid-up capital, equity *and* preference — both are permanent owned funds cushioning depositors. Second, the base counts only *genuine* reserves: revaluation-based capital reserve is an **unrealised** book-up and is stripped out (same logic as bonus and buy-back). Third, the **35%** rate applies because Yamuna is a *non-eligible* company — only Sec 76 "eligible" companies use the 10%(members)+25%(public) split, and only certain private companies enjoy the 100% rate. Bank borrowings and director's own money are *definitionally outside* "deposit," so they never enter the calculation. The ceiling caps the **total outstanding**, so existing deposits must be netted before computing head-room. Missing any one of these — including preference capital, deducting revaluation reserve, choosing the right percentage, or netting existing deposits — changes the answer.

*(Full-marks tip: examiners specifically test whether you (a) include *preference* paid-up capital, (b) exclude the *revaluation* reserve, (c) pick the *35%* non-eligible rate, and (d) net off existing deposits. Each is a distinct mark; the bank loan and director money are deliberate distractors.)*

---

### Q34. Ch: Acceptance of Deposits — Default in Repayment & Consequences (Sec 73/74) (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Godavari Papers Ltd. accepted deposits from members before the current provisions and some fall due now; it also faces a fresh maturity it cannot meet. Advise on (i) the company's obligation for **existing/pre-existing deposits**, (ii) the **remedy of a depositor** on default, and (iii) the **consequences** for the company and officers on failure to repay.

**Answer:** *Governing law — Sec 73 and 74.*

**(i) Existing deposits (Sec 74):** where any deposit accepted **before** the commencement of the Act (or amounts remaining unpaid) is outstanding, the company must (a) **file a statement** of all deposits and amounts remaining unpaid with the Registrar, and (b) **repay** such deposits within the time agreed or as directed — the scheme requires the company to honour and clear the legacy deposits rather than roll them over indefinitely. The company cannot simply ignore pre-existing dues.

**(ii) Depositor's remedy (Sec 73(4) / Sec 74):** on the company's **failure to repay** the deposit or interest when due, the **depositor may apply to the Tribunal (NCLT)** for an order directing repayment of the amount with interest and any loss/damage suffered. The depositor need not sue individually in a civil court; the NCLT provides a focused remedy.

**(iii) Consequences of default (Sec 74/76A):**
- The company is liable to **repay the deposit with interest**, and is punishable with a **fine** (as prescribed under Sec 76A), which can be substantial.
- **Every officer in default** is liable to **imprisonment and/or fine**; where it is proved the default was with **intent to defraud** depositors, the officer is personally liable, without limitation of liability, for **fraud under Sec 447**.
- The Tribunal may also, on application, allow **further time** or direct immediate repayment as the facts warrant.

**Conclusion:** Godavari Papers Ltd. must file the statement of and repay legacy deposits (Sec 74); a defaulted depositor's remedy is an **application to the NCLT**; and default exposes the company to fine plus officers to imprisonment/fine, escalating to Sec 447 fraud where intent to defraud is shown.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The deposit chapter treats the depositor as a *protected creditor whose money is at systemic risk*, so on default the law provides both a **swift forum** (the NCLT, not slow civil litigation) and **deterrent personal liability** for officers. Personal, and potentially unlimited, liability under Sec 447 exists because the greatest danger with public deposits is *deliberate diversion* by management; making officers personally answerable removes the corporate veil as a shield for fraud. The Sec 74 obligation to disclose and clear pre-existing deposits prevents companies from carrying stale, unfunded liabilities off the radar — transparency is the first line of depositor protection.

*(Full-marks tip: the marks lie in naming the **NCLT** as the depositor's forum and flagging **officer liability including Sec 447 fraud** on intent to defraud. Weak answers stop at "company must repay" without the remedy and the personal-liability consequences.)*

### Q35. Ch: Registration of Charges — Time-limit for registration, condonation & effect of non-registration (Sec 77, 78, 87) (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Vaibhav Metals Ltd. created a charge on its plant and machinery in favour of Sunrise Bank on **10th April 2025** to secure a term loan of ₹5 crore. Owing to a dispute over stamping, the particulars of charge were NOT filed within 30 days. The following sequence of events took place:

| Event | Date |
|---|---|
| Charge created | 10-Apr-2025 |
| First 30-day window ends | 10-May-2025 |
| Company files nothing | — |
| Bank pressurises; company applies to Registrar for registration | 15-Jun-2025 |
| Winding-up petition admitted against company (unrelated creditor) | 20-Jul-2025 |

The company secretary argues: "We can still register within 300 days by paying additional fees, so the bank is fully secured." Examine the validity of this statement and advise Sunrise Bank on (a) whether registration is still possible and by when, (b) the consequences if the charge remains unregistered, and (c) whether the bank's underlying debt is affected.

**Answer:**
**Governing provisions:** Sec 77(1) requires a company creating a charge to register particulars with the Registrar within **30 days** of creation. The **first proviso** to Sec 77(1) (read with Rule 4 of the Companies (Registration of Charges) Rules, 2014, as amended by the 2019 Amendment Act) creates two regimes based on the **date of creation of the charge**:

- For a charge created **on or after 02-11-2018** (which this charge is), the Registrar may allow registration within a **further period of 60 days** (i.e., total 90 days from creation) on payment of **additional fees plus ad valorem fees**. There is **no 300-day route** for such charges.

**WN — Time limits (charge created 10-Apr-2025, post-2018 regime):**
- Base 30 days: expires **10-May-2025**.
- Extended window (further 60 days beyond 30): expires **09-Jul-2025** (90 days from creation).

**(a) Is registration still possible?** The company applied on **15-Jun-2025** — this is beyond 30 days but **within the further 60-day window (before 09-Jul-2025)**. Therefore the Registrar **can** allow registration on payment of additional + ad valorem fees, provided the application is made on Form CHG-1 with the required declaration. The secretary's claim of "300 days" is **wrong** for a post-2018 charge — that route was abolished by the 2019 amendment. Fortunately the actual application date (15-Jun) is still valid, but not because of a 300-day rule.

**(b) Consequence if it remains unregistered:** Under **Sec 77(3)**, no charge shall be **taken into account by the liquidator or any other creditor** unless it is duly registered and a certificate of registration issued. The charge is **void against the liquidator and other creditors** — the security becomes worthless in insolvency. Note the winding-up petition was admitted on 20-Jul-2025; if registration had not been completed by 09-Jul, the bank would rank as an **unsecured creditor** in the winding up.

**(c) Effect on the debt:** Sec 77(4) makes clear that non-registration renders the **charge** void against liquidator/creditors, but **does NOT affect the underlying contract or obligation to repay** the money. The debt of ₹5 crore remains fully payable; only the **security** (priority over assets) is lost. The bank can still sue on the loan as an unsecured creditor.

**Conclusion/Advice:** Sunrise Bank must ensure Form CHG-1 registration is completed **before 09-Jul-2025** (the 90-day outer limit). If it is, the bank is a secured creditor despite the winding-up petition of 20-Jul. If it misses that date, the charge is void against the liquidator (Sec 77(3)) and the bank becomes unsecured — though the ₹5 crore debt itself survives (Sec 77(4)).

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The whole architecture of Sec 77 rests on the mischief of **secret charges** — a lender who does not put the charge on public record leaves other creditors and a future liquidator ignorant of the encumbrance, so the law's sanction is to make the *unregistered charge* ineffective against exactly those parties who were kept in the dark, while leaving the *personal debt* untouched (the borrower never denied owing the money). The tempting wrong answer is to apply the old "300 days" rule; students must internalise that the 2019 amendment **bifurcated** the timeline by creation date — pre-02-11-2018 charges still get 300 days, but charges created after get only a further 60 days (90 total). Getting the regime wrong changes the entire advice, because here a "300-day" belief would have lulled the bank into filing after the true 90-day deadline and losing its security.

*(Full-marks tip: The examiner rewards (i) correctly identifying the post-2018 regime and the exact 90-day outer date, (ii) the Sec 77(3) void-against-liquidator point, and (iii) the Sec 77(4) "debt survives" distinction. Common deduction: mechanically writing "300 days" without checking the creation date.)*

---

### Q36. Ch: Registration of Charges — Duty to register, company vs charge-holder, and Sec 78 fallback (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Orion Textiles Ltd. borrowed ₹80 lakh from Mr. Deshmukh (an individual lender) against a charge on its factory land created on **1st March 2025**. The company, facing internal disputes, deliberately refused to file the particulars of charge, hoping to keep the encumbrance secret so it could raise a further loan. Mr. Deshmukh is worried he will lose his security through no fault of his own. Advise Mr. Deshmukh on the remedy available to him under the Companies Act, 2013, the time he has, and whether he can recover the registration cost from the company.

**Answer:**
**Governing provision:** The **primary duty** to register a charge is on the **company** (Sec 77(1)). However, **Sec 78** provides a crucial safeguard for the charge-holder: where a company **fails to register** the charge within the period of 30 days, the **person in whose favour the charge is created** (here, Mr. Deshmukh) **may himself apply** to the Registrar for registration.

**Application to facts:**
1. The charge was created on 01-Mar-2025; the company's 30-day window expired on **31-Mar-2025**. Since the company has not registered, Mr. Deshmukh's right under Sec 78 is now triggered.
2. Sec 78 requires the Registrar, on such application by the charge-holder, to give **14 days' notice to the company** to show cause why the charge should not be registered. If the company does not object (or fails to show sufficient cause), the Registrar registers the charge.
3. **Recovery of cost:** Sec 78 expressly allows the charge-holder who registers the charge to **recover from the company the amount of any fees or additional fees** paid to the Registrar for such registration.
4. **Time:** Mr. Deshmukh should act within the overall outer limit applicable to the charge (further 60 days beyond the initial 30 for a post-2018 charge, i.e., before 30-May-2025), because Sec 78 does not create an independent longer period — it lets the charge-holder step into the registration process within the permitted window.

**Conclusion/Advice:** Mr. Deshmukh is **not helpless**. He should immediately file the particulars himself under Sec 78 (Form CHG-1), let the Registrar issue the 14-day show-cause notice to Orion Textiles, complete registration before the outer time limit, and then **recover the fees from the company**. This protects his security against the company's bad-faith attempt to keep the charge secret.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The law places the *duty* on the company because it controls the filing, but it would be grossly unfair to let a defaulting company destroy an innocent lender's security simply by sitting on its hands — the very party the registration protects would be punished for the company's inaction. Sec 78 fixes this asymmetry by giving the lender a **self-help remedy** and shifting the cost back to the company that shirked its duty. The 14-day notice exists so the company can raise a genuine objection (e.g., the charge was never actually created), preserving natural justice while stopping obstruction. The trap is to advise "only the company can register, so the lender must sue" — that misses Sec 78 entirely and gives worse, slower advice.

*(Full-marks tip: Cite Sec 78 by number, mention the 14-day show-cause notice AND the right to recover fees from the company. Deduction if you only say "company must register" and miss the charge-holder's self-help remedy.)*

---

### Q37. Ch: Registration of Charges — Modification of charge & priority (Sec 79) (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Zenith Foods Ltd. had a registered charge of ₹2 crore on its entire stock-in-trade in favour of Alpha Bank (registered 12-Jan-2025). On **5th May 2025**, the company and Alpha Bank agreed to **enhance the secured amount to ₹3.5 crore** and to **extend the charge to the company's book debts** as well. The finance head believes that because a charge is "already registered," no fresh filing is needed for this change. Meanwhile, Beta Finance advanced ₹1 crore on 20th May 2025 against the same book debts, registering its charge on 22nd May 2025. Examine whether Zenith must file anything for the 5th May change, and determine the priority between Alpha Bank and Beta Finance over the book debts.

**Answer:**
**Governing provision:** Under **Sec 79**, the provisions of Sec 77 relating to registration of charges apply **mutatis mutandis** to a **modification** of an existing charge. A "modification" includes a change in the **terms, extent, or conditions** of the charge — such as an increase in the amount secured or an extension to new assets. Therefore a modification **must be registered** (Form CHG-1) within the **same time limits** as a creation of charge.

**Application to facts:**
1. The 05-May-2025 change (amount raised ₹2 cr → ₹3.5 cr **and** extension to book debts) is a clear **modification** under Sec 79. The finance head is **wrong** — registration is compulsory. Zenith must file within 30 days (by 04-Jun-2025), extendable by a further 60 days.
2. **Priority over book debts:** Priority among charge-holders on the same asset is generally governed by the **date of registration** (the public-notice principle), not merely the date of the agreement.
   - Alpha Bank's extension to book debts is effective as security **only from the date its modification is registered**.
   - Beta Finance created and **registered** its charge on the book debts on **22-May-2025**.
   - If Alpha Bank registers its modification **after** 22-May-2025, Beta Finance — having registered first on the book debts — will rank **ahead of Alpha** on the book debts, even though Alpha's original charge (on stock) is older.

**Conclusion/Advice:** Zenith must register the modification. On the **book debts specifically**, Alpha Bank's priority depends on registering its modification before Beta's 22-May registration. If Alpha delays, Beta Finance gets first priority over the book debts. Alpha should register the modification **immediately** to protect its enlarged security.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Registration is a system of **public notice**; a later lender is entitled to rely on the register and to assume that assets not shown as charged are free. When Alpha *extended* its charge to book debts, that new encumbrance did not exist on the public record until registered — so Beta, searching the register on 20-May, would legitimately see the book debts as unencumbered and lend against them. To protect Beta's reasonable reliance, priority on the newly-charged asset runs from **registration of the modification**, not from the private agreement of 5-May. The seductive error is to treat Alpha's *original* early registration as automatically covering the *new* asset; but priority is asset-specific and notice-specific — the register only warns third parties about what it actually discloses.

*(Full-marks tip: Award-worthy points are (i) Sec 79 makes modification registrable like a fresh charge, (ii) the specific 30+60 day limit, and (iii) the asset-specific priority-from-registration logic. Deduction: assuming the original registration date protects the newly-added asset.)*

---

### Q38. Ch: Registration of Charges — Satisfaction of charge, condonation of delay & Registrar's notice (Sec 82, 83, 87) (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Meridian Ceramics Ltd. fully repaid a term loan to Pinnacle Bank on **2nd February 2025**, and the charge on its kiln was thereby satisfied. Due to staff turnover, the company forgot to intimate the Registrar. The sequence was:

| Event | Date |
|---|---|
| Charge fully satisfied (loan repaid) | 02-Feb-2025 |
| Statutory 30-day intimation window ends | 04-Mar-2025 |
| Company realises omission; wants to file satisfaction | 10-Jun-2025 |

A prospective investor's due-diligence report flags: "The public register still shows an open charge in favour of Pinnacle Bank — this reduces the value of the company's assets." Advise Meridian on (a) the time-limit and consequence of the missed satisfaction filing, (b) the remedy now available in June, and (c) what the Registrar can do independently to record satisfaction.

**Answer:**
**Governing provisions:** 
- **Sec 82** requires a company to give **intimation of satisfaction** of a charge (payment/discharge in full) to the Registrar within **30 days** from the date of satisfaction. On such intimation, the Registrar issues notice to the charge-holder (14 days to object) and, if no cause is shown, records the satisfaction and issues a **memorandum of satisfaction (Form CHG-4 / CHG-5)**.
- **Sec 87** empowers the **Central Government** (power delegated to the Regional Director) to order **rectification of the register** — including on **condonation of delay** in filing satisfaction — where the omission was **accidental, or due to inadvertence, or does not prejudice creditors/shareholders**, or where it is **just and equitable** to grant relief.
- **Sec 83** empowers the **Registrar himself**, on being satisfied that a charge has been satisfied or that the property has ceased to be part of the company's undertaking, to enter a memorandum of satisfaction **even without an application by the company**, after issuing notice to the parties.

**Application to facts:**
1. **(a) Time-limit & consequence:** The 30-day window under Sec 82 ended on **04-Mar-2025**. The company filed nothing by then, so it missed the statutory window. The consequence is **not** that the debt revives — the loan is genuinely repaid — but that the **public register wrongly shows a subsisting charge**, misleading investors and depressing the perceived value of assets (exactly the due-diligence concern). *Note:* the earlier 300-day tolerance for satisfaction under the old rules is no longer a simple right; delay beyond 30 days now requires the condonation route.
2. **(b) Remedy in June:** Since the 30-day window is gone, Meridian must apply to the **Regional Director under Sec 87** for **condonation of the delay**, showing the omission was accidental/inadvertent (staff turnover) and that no creditor is prejudiced (the charge-holder itself confirms repayment). On condonation, the Registrar records the satisfaction.
3. **(c) Registrar's independent power:** Under **Sec 83**, even without the company's application, if the Registrar has evidence (e.g., the bank's no-dues confirmation) that the charge is satisfied, he may **suo motu** enter a memorandum of satisfaction after giving notice.

**Conclusion/Advice:** Meridian should (i) obtain a **no-dues / satisfaction letter** from Pinnacle Bank, (ii) file for **condonation of delay under Sec 87** with the Regional Director since the 30-day Sec 82 window has lapsed, and (iii) note that the Registrar independently has power under Sec 83 to record satisfaction. Once recorded, the register will show the charge as satisfied, curing the investor's objection.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The register must mirror reality both when a charge is *created* and when it is *discharged* — a stale entry showing a paid-off charge is just as misleading to the market as an unregistered live charge, because a purchaser or investor over-estimates the company's encumbrances. That is why the law provides *three* mechanisms: routine intimation (Sec 82), the Registrar's own corrective power (Sec 83), and equitable condonation for honest delay (Sec 87). Students often wrongly think a missed satisfaction filing "revives the debt" or is fatal — it is neither; the debt is dead, and the law is generous about *clerical* delay because no one is defrauded by belatedly recording a genuine repayment. The reasoning to master is *whose interest each section protects*: Sec 82/83 protect market transparency, Sec 87 protects the honest company from disproportionate consequence of an innocent slip.

*(Full-marks tip: Distinguish Sec 82 (company's 30-day intimation), Sec 83 (Registrar's suo motu power) and Sec 87 (RD condonation). Deduction if you say the debt revives, or if you omit that post-30-days the only route is Sec 87 condonation.)*

---

### Q39. Ch: Registration of Charges — Charges NOT requiring registration & Sec 77(3) effect (Marks: 5) [Theory]

**Question:** "Every charge created by a company must be registered, failing which the security is void." A student states this as an absolute rule. Examine the correctness of this statement, identifying (a) any charges that are exempt from registration, and (b) precisely against whom an unregistered registrable charge is void, and against whom it remains valid.

**Answer:**
**The statement is only partly correct** and is stated too absolutely.

**(a) Exemptions — not everything needs registration:** The obligation under Sec 77 applies to charges "on its property or assets or any of its undertaking." However, the Central Government is empowered (Sec 77(1) proviso / rules) to prescribe charges that need not be registered, and by settled principle certain security interests fall outside the registration net, notably:
- A **pledge** where possession of goods passes to the pledgee (the pledgee's possession is itself notice), as against a registrable *hypothecation/mortgage*.
- **Charges prescribed as exempt** under the rules (e.g., certain charges governed by other specific enactments, or a lien on subscribed shares for unpaid calls).
- A charge created by **operation of law** (e.g., a statutory/unpaid vendor's lien) as opposed to one *created by* the company — Sec 77 targets charges the company *creates*.

So it is **not** true that literally every security interest requires registration.

**(b) Against whom void, against whom valid:** Under **Sec 77(3)**, an unregistered (but registrable) charge is **not void against the whole world** — it is void only as against:
- the **liquidator** appointed in winding up, and
- **any other creditor** of the company.

Crucially, **as between the company and the charge-holder themselves**, the charge **remains valid and enforceable** — the company cannot plead its own default in registration to defeat the lender while it is a going concern. Furthermore, **Sec 77(4)** preserves the **underlying debt**: non-registration voids the *security's priority*, not the *obligation to repay*.

**Conclusion:** The absolute statement is **incorrect**. (i) Some charges (pledges, prescribed/exempt charges, charges by operation of law) need not be registered; and (ii) even for a registrable charge, non-registration makes it void **only against the liquidator and other creditors**, not against the company itself, and the debt survives.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The registration regime is a **notice-to-third-parties** mechanism, so its sanctions are aimed at third parties who could be misled — the liquidator (standing in the shoes of all creditors) and competing creditors — not at the two parties to the charge who obviously already know about it. That is why the charge stays good *between* company and lender: neither was deceived. Exemptions exist where the *purpose* of registration is already met by other means: a pledgee's physical possession is open notice, and a charge arising by operation of law was not a secret bargain the company could have disclosed. Understanding registration as "curing secrecy" lets you predict, rather than memorise, both the exemptions and the against-whom-void answer.

*(Full-marks tip: The examiner wants the precise Sec 77(3) list — "liquidator and any other creditor" — plus the Sec 77(4) survival of debt and at least one genuine exemption (pledge / operation of law). Deduction for saying "void against everyone".)*

---

### Q40. Ch: Registration of Charges — Register of charges, inspection & Sec 86 penalty (Marks: 5) [Theory]

**Question:** Distinguish between the **register of charges maintained by the Registrar** and the **register of charges maintained by the company** under Sec 85. State who may inspect the company's register and the copies of instruments, and outline the consequence under Sec 86 where a company wilfully furnishes false particulars for registration of a charge.

**Answer:**
**Two distinct registers:**

| Basis | Registrar's register (Sec 81) | Company's own register (Sec 85) |
|---|---|---|
| Maintained by | The Registrar of Companies, for each company | The company itself, at its registered office (Form CHG-7) |
| Contents | Particulars of all charges registered, from CHG-1/CHG-4 filings | Every charge and floating charge, with details of property, amount, and charge-holder |
| Nature | Public record for the whole registry | Internal statutory register + copies of every instrument creating a charge |
| Inspection | Open to inspection by **any person** on payment of fee | See below |

**Inspection of the company's register (Sec 85(2)):**
- The **register of charges and the instruments** kept at the registered office are open to inspection **without fee by any member or creditor** of the company, and
- **by any other person on payment of the prescribed fee**,
during business hours (subject to reasonable restrictions in the articles).

**Sec 86 — Punishment for contravention / false statements:**
- A company contravening the charge provisions is **liable to penalty**, and every officer in default is **liable to penalty**.
- Critically, **Sec 86(2)** provides that where a person **wilfully furnishes false or incorrect information, or knowingly suppresses material information**, required to be registered under Sec 77, he shall be liable for **fraud under Sec 447** — a serious offence carrying imprisonment and fine.

**Conclusion:** The Registrar's register is the **public** record; the company's own Sec 85 register is an **internal** statutory register open to members/creditors free and to others on fee. Wilful falsity in charge particulars is not a mere fee-default — it attracts **Sec 447 fraud** liability via Sec 86(2).

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The law keeps *two* registers because they serve different audiences — the Registrar's register gives the outside market a searchable public record, while the company's own register (with copies of the actual instruments) lets its own members and creditors verify the terms without going to the registry. The escalation in Sec 86(2) to **Sec 447 fraud** for *wilful* false particulars reflects that the entire registration system is only as trustworthy as the accuracy of what is filed; a lie on the register defeats the very purpose of public notice and can defraud later creditors, so the sanction is criminal, not merely monetary. The trap is to treat all charge defaults as small penalties — deliberate falsification is treated as fraud.

*(Full-marks tip: Draw the two-register distinction clearly, state the free-for-members-and-creditors inspection rule, and specifically flag Sec 86(2) → Sec 447. Deduction if you reduce a wilful false filing to a mere penalty.)*

---

### Q41. Ch: Registration of Charges — Integrated: creation, non-registration, and rights in winding up (Marks: 10) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Consider the following independent facts concerning **Cobalt Engineering Ltd.**:

| # | Charge / event | Date | Registered? |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Charge to Bank P on machinery, ₹4 cr | 05-Jan-2025 | Registered 20-Jan-2025 |
| 2 | Charge to Bank Q on same machinery, ₹2 cr | 01-Mar-2025 | Registered 05-Mar-2025 |
| 3 | Charge to Lender R on machinery, ₹1.5 cr | 20-Jan-2025 | **Never registered** |
| 4 | Winding-up order passed; liquidator appointed | 15-Aug-2025 | — |

Machinery realises only **₹5 crore** in liquidation. Determine how the ₹5 crore is distributed among Bank P, Bank Q and Lender R, and explain the treatment of Lender R's claim. Assume all three charges were validly created and rank purely by the registration regime.

**Solution:**
**WN-1 — Validity of each charge against the liquidator (Sec 77(3)):**
- Charge 1 (Bank P): registered → valid & enforceable against liquidator.
- Charge 2 (Bank Q): registered → valid & enforceable against liquidator.
- Charge 3 (Lender R): **never registered** → under Sec 77(3), **void against the liquidator and other creditors**. R cannot claim as a secured creditor.

**WN-2 — Priority between the two registered charges (P and Q):**
Both charge the *same* machinery. Priority follows the **date of registration** (public-notice principle):
- Bank P registered **20-Jan-2025** — first.
