- Bank Q registered **05-Mar-2025** — second.
So **Bank P ranks ahead of Bank Q**.

**WN-3 — Distribution of ₹5 crore realisation:**

| Claimant | Secured claim (₹ cr) | Rank | Amount received (₹ cr) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Bank P | 4.00 | 1st (registered first) | 4.00 |
| Bank Q | 2.00 | 2nd | 1.00 (balance available) |
| Lender R | 1.50 | Void as secured (unregistered) | 0.00 as secured |
| **Total distributed** | | | **5.00** |

- Bank P is paid in full: ₹4 cr.
- Remaining ₹1 cr goes to Bank Q, who receives only **₹1 cr of its ₹2 cr** (shortfall ₹1 cr).
- Bank Q's unpaid **₹1 cr** becomes an **unsecured claim** in the general pool.
- **Lender R** gets **nothing as a secured creditor**; its charge is void against the liquidator. However, under **Sec 77(4)**, the **debt of ₹1.5 cr survives** — R ranks as an **unsecured creditor** for ₹1.5 cr in the general pool (pari passu with Bank Q's ₹1 cr shortfall and other unsecured creditors).

**Answer:** From the ₹5 cr machinery realisation — **Bank P: ₹4 cr (full); Bank Q: ₹1 cr (secured), plus ₹1 cr as unsecured; Lender R: ₹0 as secured, but ranks unsecured for ₹1.5 cr.**

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Two principles drive the whole answer. First, **Sec 77(3) is the gatekeeper**: an unregistered charge simply *does not exist* in the eyes of the liquidator, so Lender R — despite creating its charge on the very same day P registered — is thrown into the unsecured pool because it never gave public notice. This is the mischief rule in action: R kept its security secret, so it cannot spring it on the general creditors in winding up. Second, **as between validly registered charges on the same asset, registration date fixes priority**, not the date of the loan or the charge deed — because a later lender searches the register and is entitled to rely on what is recorded and when. The common error is to rank R by its early *creation* date (20-Jan) or to think R loses the debt itself; neither is right — R loses *priority*, not the *debt* (Sec 77(4)), and creation date is irrelevant once registration governs priority.

*(Full-marks tip: Show the Sec 77(3) knock-out of R, the registration-date priority between P and Q, the exact ₹4 cr / ₹1 cr split, and R's survival as an unsecured creditor under Sec 77(4). Deduction: ranking R by creation date or wiping out R's debt entirely.)*

---

### Q42. Ch: Management & Administration — Notice of general meeting, shorter notice & consent (Sec 101) (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Nimbus Realty Ltd. (a company having a share capital, with 500 members) proposes to hold its Annual General Meeting. The company dispatched notice on **8th September 2025** by post for a meeting on **20th September 2025**. Two items are on the agenda: (i) adoption of accounts (ordinary business) and (ii) a special resolution to alter the objects clause. A group of members objects that the notice is short. The company argues that 95% of members (by number) have consented to shorter notice. Examine (a) whether the notice period is valid, (b) the correct rule for shorter notice for an AGM, and (c) whether the special resolution can be validly passed at this meeting.

**Answer:**
**Governing provision — Sec 101:** A general meeting may be called by giving **not less than clear 21 days' notice** in writing or electronic mode. "**Clear 21 days**" means the day of **service of notice** and the day of the **meeting** are both **excluded**. Where notice is sent by post, service is deemed to be effected on the **expiry of 48 hours** after posting (Sec 20 / Secretarial Standards).

**WN — Counting clear days:**
- Notice posted 08-Sep-2025 → deemed served after 48 hours → **10-Sep-2025**.
- Exclude day of service (10-Sep) and day of meeting (20-Sep).
- Clear days available = 11th to 19th September = **9 clear days only.**
- Required = 21 clear days. **The notice is grossly short.**

**(b) Shorter notice rule (proviso to Sec 101(1)):** A meeting may be called at **shorter notice** if consent is given —
- For an **AGM**: by **not less than 95% of the members entitled to vote** at the meeting.
- For **any other general meeting**: by members holding not less than **95% of the paid-up share capital** carrying voting rights (or 95% of voting power where no share capital).

The company's claim of "95% of members by number" is the correct **basis for an AGM**. However, the consent must be of members **entitled to vote**, and — critically — it must be validly obtained (in writing or electronically) **for this specific shorter-notice meeting**.

**(c) Special resolution:** There is **no bar** on transacting special-business/special-resolution items at a validly convened AGM. Provided (i) the explanatory statement under Sec 102 accompanies the special-business item (object clause alteration), and (ii) valid 95% shorter-notice consent is obtained, the **special resolution to alter the objects clause can be passed** — subject additionally to the requirement (Sec 13) that alteration of objects also needs filing with the Registrar and, where money was raised from the public with unutilised amount, a special resolution and other safeguards.

**Conclusion/Advice:** The 9-clear-day notice is **invalid on its face** (needs 21 clear days). But because it is an **AGM**, the meeting can still be validly held at shorter notice **if genuine consent of ≥95% of members entitled to vote is obtained** — the company's 95%-by-number basis is correct for an AGM. If that consent is validly in place and the Sec 102 explanatory statement is annexed, the special resolution altering the objects clause is valid.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The 21-clear-day rule exists to give **every member enough time to study the agenda, arrange proxies, and attend** — it is a protection *for the members*. That is exactly why the law lets the members themselves *waive* it, but only by an overwhelming **95%** consent, because a right that protects the minority should not be surrendered by a bare majority. Note the deliberate design: for an **AGM** the threshold is **95% of members**, but for **other meetings** it is **95% of paid-up voting capital** — the AGM uses a head-count because AGMs concern all members' routine oversight, whereas EGMs often concern capital-weighted decisions. The classic trap is to (i) forget the 48-hour deemed-service and mis-count clear days, or (ii) mix up the two 95% bases. The special-resolution item is a red herring — nothing stops special business at an AGM; the real issue is the *notice validity*.

*(Full-marks tip: Correctly compute the 9 clear days using 48-hour deemed service, state BOTH shorter-notice thresholds and pick the right one for an AGM (95% of members), and confirm special business is permissible with a Sec 102 statement. Deduction: mis-counting clear days or citing 95% of capital for an AGM.)*

---

### Q43. Ch: Management & Administration — Quorum for a general meeting (Sec 103) (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Stellar Pharma Ltd., a **public company**, has **2,700 members**. An Extraordinary General Meeting is called for 4:00 p.m. on 12th October 2025. At 4:00 p.m., only **12 members** are personally present; several others attend only through proxies. Determine (a) the quorum required, (b) whether proxies count towards quorum, (c) the consequence if quorum is not present within half an hour, and (d) how the position differs if the meeting had been **requisitioned by members** under Sec 100.

**Answer:**
**Governing provision — Sec 103 (quorum for general meeting):**
For a **public company**, quorum (unless the articles provide a larger number) is:
- **5 members** personally present if members ≤ 1,000;
- **15 members** personally present if members are **more than 1,000 but up to 5,000**;
- **30 members** personally present if members exceed 5,000.

For a **private company**, quorum is **2 members** personally present.

**WN — Applying to Stellar Pharma (2,700 members):**
2,700 is more than 1,000 and up to 5,000 → quorum = **15 members personally present.**

**(a)** Quorum required = **15 members personally present.**

**(b) Do proxies count?** **No.** Sec 103 requires members **personally present**. A proxy may vote (Sec 105) but a proxy is **not counted for quorum** at a general meeting. Hence only the **12 members personally present** count — quorum of 15 is **not met**.

**(c) Consequence if quorum absent within half an hour (Sec 103(2)):**
- If within **half an hour** from the appointed time quorum is not present, the meeting shall stand **adjourned** to the **same day in the next week, at the same time and place**, or to such other date/time/place as the Board may determine.
- At the **adjourned meeting**, if quorum is again not present within half an hour, the **members present shall be the quorum** (i.e., the quorum requirement is relaxed) — but this applies only to a normally-called meeting.

**(d) If the meeting was requisitioned by members (Sec 100):**
Under **Sec 103(2)(b)**, if the meeting had been **called by requisitionists** and quorum is not present within half an hour, the meeting shall stand **cancelled** (dissolved), **not** carried to an adjourned meeting with relaxed quorum. This prevents requisitionists from forcing decisions with a handful of members.

**Conclusion:** Quorum needed is **15 members personally present**; only 12 are present and **proxies do not count**, so quorum fails. For a Board-called EGM, the meeting adjourns to the same day next week (and at that adjourned meeting the members present form the quorum). Had it been a **requisitioned** meeting, it would instead **stand cancelled**.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Quorum is the law's guarantee that a meeting genuinely represents the membership before it binds the company — hence it insists on members **personally present**, because a proxy is a mere voting instrument and cannot supply the deliberative presence quorum is meant to ensure. The graduated slabs (5/15/30) scale the safeguard to company size. The **different treatment of requisitioned meetings** is deliberate policy: an ordinary meeting adjourns and then relaxes quorum so the company's business is not paralysed by apathy, but a *requisitioned* meeting — convened by a dissenting minority — is simply **cancelled** if that minority cannot even muster a quorum, so a tiny group cannot ram through resolutions. The common error is to count proxies toward quorum, or to apply the "members present = quorum" relaxation to a requisitioned meeting.

*(Full-marks tip: Nail the 2,700-member slab → 15 personally present, state that proxies don't count, and give BOTH the adjournment rule and the special Sec 100 requisitioned-meeting cancellation. Deduction: counting proxies or missing the requisitioned-meeting distinction.)*

---

### Q44. Ch: Management & Administration — Proxies: rights, restrictions & 48-hour rule (Sec 105) (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** At the AGM of Vertex Chemicals Ltd. (a public company) scheduled for 11:00 a.m. on 25th November 2025, the following situations arise. Examine the validity of each with reasons:

| # | Situation |
|---|---|
| (i) | Mr. A, a member, appoints Mr. X as proxy. Mr. X, at the meeting, insists on **speaking** on the resolution and demands the right to **vote on a show of hands**. |
| (ii) | The proxy instrument executed in favour of Mr. X was **deposited at the registered office at 10:00 a.m. on 24th November 2025** (25 hours before the meeting). The company rejects it as late. |
| (iii) | Mr. X holds proxies for **60 members holding 12% of the total share capital** and additionally for members holding 4% — he wants to act for all. |
| (iv) | A clause in the company's **articles requires proxy forms to be lodged 60 hours before** the meeting. |

**Answer:**
**Governing provision — Sec 105 and its provisos:**

**(i) Right to speak / vote on show of hands:** Under Sec 105, a proxy —
- has the **right to attend and vote** but **NOT the right to speak** at the meeting; and
- (in the case of a company **having a share capital**) a proxy **cannot vote on a show of hands** — a proxy may vote **only on a poll**.
So Mr. X's demand to **speak** is **invalid**, and his demand to **vote on a show of hands** is **invalid**. He may vote only when a **poll** is taken. *(Result: both demands rejected.)*

**(ii) 48-hour deposit rule:** A proviso to Sec 105 provides that any provision requiring the instrument of proxy to be deposited **more than 48 hours before** the meeting shall have effect as if **48 hours** had been specified. The instrument was deposited at 10:00 a.m. on 24-Nov for an 11:00 a.m. meeting on 25-Nov — that is **25 hours before**, which is **within (less than) 48 hours before? No — 25 hours is less than 48 hours before the meeting, i.e., it is deposited AFTER the 48-hour cut-off point**. 

Let me apply the rule precisely: proxies must be deposited **not later than 48 hours before** the meeting. 48 hours before 11:00 a.m. on 25-Nov is **11:00 a.m. on 23-Nov**. The instrument was lodged at 10:00 a.m. on **24-Nov**, i.e., only **25 hours** before — this is **after** the 11:00 a.m. 23-Nov deadline. Therefore, if the articles require the maximum permissible 48 hours' deposit, the proxy is **validly rejected as late**. (The 48-hour proviso caps how *early* a company can demand deposit; it does not help a proxy lodged too *late*.)

**(iii) Aggregate limit on number of members / capital:** Under the rules to Sec 105, a person **cannot act as proxy for more than 50 members** and for members holding in aggregate **not more than 10% of the total share capital carrying voting rights**. However, a member holding **more than 10%** may appoint a **single person** as proxy who then **cannot act for any other member**. Here Mr. X holds proxies for **60 members** (exceeds 50) and **16% aggregate** (exceeds 10%). Therefore Mr. X **cannot validly act for all** — he can act for a **maximum of 50 members holding up to 10%** of voting capital; the excess appointments are invalid.

**(iv) 60-hour deposit clause in articles:** By the proviso to Sec 105, any article demanding deposit **more than 48 hours** in advance is **read down to 48 hours**. So the **60-hour clause is invalid to the extent of the excess** and is treated as a 48-hour requirement.

**Conclusion:** (i) Mr. X may neither speak nor vote on a show of hands — only on a poll; (ii) his proxy (lodged only 25 hours before) is validly **rejected as late** against the maximum 48-hour requirement; (iii) he may act for at most **50 members / 10% capital**, not 60 members / 16%; (iv) the 60-hour article is **read down to 48 hours**.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Each proviso answers a specific abuse. A proxy is a *voting delegate*, not a member — so the law lets him **vote (on a poll) but not speak**, because open debate is reserved for members themselves, and the show-of-hands bar prevents a single proxy-holder distorting a headcount vote. The **48-hour cap** protects members from articles that demand proxies so early they cannot react to late developments — but it is a *ceiling on the company's demand*, so it never rescues a genuinely late proxy. The **50-member / 10% aggregate** limit stops proxy-farming, where one person amasses control disproportionate to his own stake; yet a **single large (>10%) member** can still concentrate his own votes in one proxy, because that reflects his real economic interest. The frequent trap is to think the 48-hour proviso makes *any* deposit within 48 hours valid — it does the opposite: it caps the demand at 48 hours, and a proxy lodged inside the deadline window (too late) still fails.

*(Full-marks tip: Address all four; the discriminating points are (a) proxy cannot speak / cannot vote on show of hands, poll only, (b) the 48-hour proviso is a *ceiling*, (c) 50-members-and-10% limit with the single-large-member exception. Deduction: treating the 48-hour rule as validating a late proxy or missing the speak/show-of-hands bar.)*

---

### Q45. Ch: Management & Administration — Ordinary vs special resolution & demand for poll (Sec 114, 109) (Marks: 6) [Theory]

**Question:** "The distinction between an ordinary resolution and a special resolution lies only in the majority required." Critically examine this statement. Also state who may **demand a poll** under Sec 109 and the effect of a validly demanded poll on a decision already declared on a show of hands.

**Answer:**
**The statement is incomplete** — the distinction goes beyond the bare majority.

**Sec 114 — the two resolutions:**
- **Ordinary resolution:** passed by a **simple majority** — votes cast in favour **exceed** votes cast against (whether on show of hands or poll), by members entitled to vote.
- **Special resolution:** requires (i) the **intention to propose it as a special resolution** to be **specified in the notice**; (ii) the votes cast **in favour** to be **not less than three times** the votes cast **against** (i.e., a **≥75% majority** of votes cast).

So the differences are **three-fold**, not one:
1. **Majority** — simple majority vs ≥75% of votes cast.
2. **Notice** — a special resolution must be **expressly flagged** as such in the notice; the item cannot be upgraded at the meeting. An ordinary resolution has no such express-intimation requirement.
3. **Filing / matters reserved** — many special-resolution matters (alteration of MOA/AOA, reduction of capital, etc.) require **filing of the resolution (MGT-14)** and are statutorily reserved, reflecting their fundamental nature.

Thus the statement is wrong to reduce the distinction to majority alone; the **notice requirement** and the **class of matters** reserved for special resolutions are equally essential.

**Sec 109 — Demand for poll:** A poll may be **ordered by the Chairman** on his own motion, and **shall be ordered on a demand** made:
- In the case of a company **having a share capital**, by members present in person or by proxy holding **at least 1/10th of the total voting power** OR holding shares on which an **aggregate sum of not less than ₹5,00,000** (or such prescribed amount) has been paid up; and
- In the case of any other company, by any member(s) present having **not less than 1/10th of the total voting power**.

**Effect of a valid poll:** Once a poll is validly demanded and taken, the **result of the poll is the decision** of the meeting **on that resolution** — it **supersedes** the earlier declaration on a show of hands. This is because a show of hands is one-member-one-hand, whereas a poll counts **votes in proportion to shareholding**, giving the true weighted decision.

**Conclusion:** The distinction between ordinary and special resolutions rests on **majority, mandatory notice of intention, and the reserved subject-matter/filing** — not majority alone. A poll may be demanded by members holding 1/10th voting power or ₹5 lakh paid-up value, and a valid poll's result **overrides** any show-of-hands outcome.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** A special resolution is reserved for decisions that **alter the fundamental bargain** among shareholders (the constitution, capital, class rights), so the law demands not just a supermajority but **advance, explicit warning in the notice** — a member must know *before* the meeting that a fundamental change is proposed, so he can attend or arm a proxy. Reducing the difference to "majority only" misses this protective notice function. The poll mechanism exists because a show of hands ignores the size of holdings; allowing a 1/10th-power (or ₹5 lakh) minority to *force* a poll ensures that a decision truly reflects **capital at risk**, not a chance headcount in the room — which is why the poll result, once taken, **displaces** the show-of-hands result entirely.

*(Full-marks tip: Give all three points of distinction (majority, notice of intention, reserved matters/filing) and both limbs of the Sec 109 poll threshold (1/10th voting power OR ₹5 lakh). Deduction: stating only the 75% vs simple-majority difference.)*

---

### Q46. Ch: Management & Administration — Annual General Meeting: timing, first AGM, extension & default (Sec 96) (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Examine each of the following independent situations concerning the holding of the **Annual General Meeting** under Sec 96 of the Companies Act, 2013:

| # | Situation |
|---|---|
| (i) | Horizon Ltd. was **incorporated on 5th January 2025**. When must it hold its **first AGM**, and what is the last permissible date? |
| (ii) | Horizon Ltd.'s financial year ends **31st March 2026**. It held its 2025 AGM on 30-Sep-2025. By when must the **next (2026) AGM** be held, considering both the 6-month and the 15-month rule? |
| (iii) | The company anticipates it cannot hold the 2026 AGM in time due to a delayed audit. What relief is available and from whom? |
| (iv) | Can the AGM be held on a **national holiday** at **7:00 p.m.**? |

**Answer:**
**Governing provision — Sec 96:**

**(i) First AGM (Horizon Ltd., incorporated 05-Jan-2025):**
A company must hold its **first AGM within 9 months** from the close of its **first financial year**. 
- Incorporated 05-Jan-2025 → first financial year is a period that may extend to **31-Mar-2026** (a company incorporated on/after 1st January may take its first FY up to the 31st March of the following year).
- First FY closes **31-Mar-2026** → first AGM must be held **within 9 months** → last date = **31-Dec-2026.**
- Note: for the **first AGM**, the "not more than 15 months gap" and "6 months from FY-end" rules do **not** apply — only the 9-month rule.

**(ii) Subsequent AGM (2026):** For every AGM other than the first, **two** conditions must both be satisfied:
- It must be held **within 6 months** of the close of the financial year (FY ends 31-Mar-2026 → by **30-Sep-2026**); **and**
- The **gap between two AGMs must not exceed 15 months** (previous AGM 30-Sep-2025 + 15 months = **30-Dec-2026**).
The **earlier of the binding limits** governs → the 2026 AGM must be held **on or before 30-Sep-2026** (the 6-month rule bites first).

**(iii) Extension:** The **Registrar** may, for any **special reason**, extend the time for holding an AGM (other than the first AGM) by a period **not exceeding 3 months**. So Horizon can apply to the Registrar; if granted, the deadline of 30-Sep-2026 can be extended up to **31-Dec-2026**. The **first AGM cannot be extended.**

**(iv) Day, time and place:** Every AGM must be held —
- during **business hours (9:00 a.m. to 6:00 p.m.)**;
- on a day that is **NOT a National Holiday**;
- at the **registered office** or some other place within the city/town/village where the registered office is situate.
A meeting at **7:00 p.m.** violates the business-hours rule, and holding it on a **National Holiday** is prohibited. Hence such a meeting is **invalid**. (An AGM *may* be held on a public holiday that is not a *National* Holiday, but not on a National Holiday.)

**Conclusion:** (i) First AGM by **31-Dec-2026**; (ii) 2026 AGM by **30-Sep-2026** (6-month rule bites before the 15-month gap); (iii) Registrar may extend by up to 3 months (not the first AGM); (iv) A 7:00 p.m. meeting on a National Holiday is **invalid** on both counts.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The AGM rules exist to guarantee members a **regular, predictable, once-a-year accountability event** — hence the *twin* leash for ordinary AGMs (6 months from year-end AND a 15-month ceiling between meetings): the 6-month rule keeps the accounts *fresh*, while the 15-month rule prevents a company from drifting by choosing a late year-end. For the **first** AGM the law is more relaxed (9 months, no 15-month cap) because a new company needs runway to complete its first accounts. Extension is entrusted to the **Registrar** and denied for the first AGM precisely because the first meeting's generous 9-month window already accommodates start-up delay. The business-hours / no-National-Holiday / local-place rules protect members' *practical ability to attend*. The classic trap is to apply the 15-month rule to the *first* AGM, or to let the later 15-month date override the earlier 6-month date — always the *earlier* binding date governs.

*(Full-marks tip: Separate first-AGM (9 months only) from subsequent AGM (6-month AND 15-month, earlier governs), give the Registrar's 3-month extension (not for first AGM), and the day/time/place trio. Deduction: applying the 15-month rule to the first AGM or picking the later date.)*

---

### Q47. Ch: Management & Administration — Extraordinary General Meeting on requisition; default power (Sec 100) (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Members of Falcon Industries Ltd. (a company with share capital) holding **11% of the paid-up capital carrying voting rights** submit a valid requisition to the Board on **1st August 2025** demanding an EGM to remove a director. The Board **fails to move on the requisition**. Advise the requisitionists on (a) whether their 11% holding is sufficient to requisition, (b) the timeline within which the Board must act and by when the meeting must be held, (c) their rights if the Board does not call the meeting, and (d) recovery of expenses.

**Answer:**
**Governing provision — Sec 100:**

**(a) Threshold to requisition:** The Board **shall** call an EGM on the requisition of members holding, on the date of receipt of the requisition, **not less than 1/10th (10%) of the paid-up share capital** carrying voting rights (or, for a company without share capital, 1/10th of total voting power). Falcon's requisitionists hold **11% > 10%** → the requisition is **valid**. The requisition must **state the matters** for consideration and be signed by the requisitionists.

**(b) Board's timeline (Sec 100(4)):**
- The Board must, **within 21 days** of receipt of a valid requisition, **proceed to call** a meeting.
- The meeting itself must be held **within 45 days** of the receipt of the requisition.
- Receipt: 01-Aug-2025 → Board must proceed to call by **22-Aug-2025**; meeting to be held on or before **15-Sep-2025.**

**(c) Requisitionists' self-help power (Sec 100(4) & (6)):** If the Board **does not proceed within 21 days** to call a meeting to be held within 45 days, the **requisitionists themselves** (or those representing a majority in value of them) may **call the meeting themselves** — but such a self-called meeting must be held **within 3 months** from the date of the requisition (i.e., on or before **01-Nov-2025**). The manner of calling shall, as far as possible, be the same as that in which meetings are called by the Board.

**(d) Recovery of expenses (Sec 100(6)):** Any **reasonable expenses** incurred by the requisitionists in calling the meeting (because of the Board's default) shall be **reimbursed to them by the company**, and the company shall in turn **recover such sums from the fees or remuneration payable to the defaulting directors** who were responsible for the failure. This makes the delinquent directors ultimately bear the cost.

**Conclusion/Advice:** The 11% holding is sufficient (≥10%). The Board had to *proceed to call* by 22-Aug and hold the meeting by 15-Sep-2025. Since it failed, the requisitionists may **call the EGM themselves, to be held on or before 01-Nov-2025**, and **recover their reasonable expenses from the company**, which will deduct them from the defaulting directors' remuneration.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Sec 100 is a **minority-empowerment** provision — it prevents an entrenched Board from stifling shareholder oversight by refusing to convene meetings on inconvenient topics (like its own directors' removal). The mechanism has teeth precisely because the Board's inaction does not stall the process: if directors drag their feet past 21 days, the *members* seize the initiative and call the meeting themselves, and the law then makes the **defaulting directors personally pay** for the disruption via the expense-recovery route. This cost-shifting is the deterrent that makes the right meaningful. The trap is to confuse the **two clocks** — the Board's "45 days from requisition" versus the requisitionists' self-called "3 months from requisition" — or to think the members' only remedy is to sue; the statute gives them a direct, self-executing power.

*(Full-marks tip: State the 10% threshold, then the 21-day / 45-day Board clock AND the requisitionists' 3-month self-help clock, plus expense recovery from directors. Deduction: merging the 45-day and 3-month periods, or omitting the director-reimbursement sting.)*

---

### Q48. Ch: Management & Administration — Annual Return: contents, signing & certification (Sec 92) (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Advise on the following aspects of the **Annual Return** under Sec 92 for the following three companies for FY ending 31-Mar-2025:

| Company | Nature | Paid-up capital | Turnover |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ridge Ltd. | Listed public company | ₹8 cr | ₹60 cr |
| Vale Pvt. Ltd. | Private company | ₹12 cr | ₹55 cr |
| Brook Pvt. Ltd. (Small company) | Private (small co.) | ₹1.5 cr | ₹18 cr |

Address (a) who must **sign** the annual return in each case, (b) which companies require **certification by a Company Secretary in practice (Form MGT-8)**, and (c) the **time-limit for filing** the annual return with the Registrar and the consequence of delay.

**Answer:**
**Governing provision — Sec 92:**

**(a) Signing of the Annual Return (Sec 92(1)):**
The annual return (Form MGT-7 / MGT-7A) shall be signed by —
- a **director AND the Company Secretary**; or, where there is **no Company Secretary, by a Company Secretary in practice**.
- **Exception for OPC, small company and (by rules) certain private/small companies:** the annual return may be signed by the **company secretary, or where there is no CS, by the director** of the company. Small companies file the abridged **MGT-7A**.

Applying:
- **Ridge Ltd. (listed):** must be signed by a **director and the CS** (a listed company must have a whole-time CS).
- **Vale Pvt. Ltd. (paid-up ₹12 cr):** as it crosses the threshold requiring a whole-time CS (paid-up ≥ ₹10 cr), it must have a CS; signed by a **director and the CS**.
- **Brook Pvt. Ltd. (small company):** files **MGT-7A**, signed by the **CS, or where there is no CS, by a director**.

**(b) Certification by CS in practice — Form MGT-8 (Sec 92(2)):**
MGT-8 certification by a **PCS** is required for —
- every **listed company**, and
- every company having **paid-up capital ≥ ₹10 crore OR turnover ≥ ₹50 crore.**

Applying:
- **Ridge Ltd.:** listed → **MGT-8 required.**
- **Vale Pvt. Ltd.:** paid-up ₹12 cr (≥ ₹10 cr) **and** turnover ₹55 cr (≥ ₹50 cr) → **MGT-8 required.**
- **Brook Pvt. Ltd.:** paid-up ₹1.5 cr (< ₹10 cr) and turnover ₹18 cr (< ₹50 cr) → **MGT-8 NOT required.**

**(c) Time-limit for filing & consequence (Sec 92(4) & (5)):**
- The annual return must be filed with the Registrar **within 60 days** from the date on which the **AGM is held**, or where **no AGM is held**, within 60 days from the **date on which the AGM should have been held**, together with a statement of reasons for not holding it.
- **Consequence of delay:** the company and every officer in default is liable to **penalty** (a base penalty plus a per-day continuing penalty subject to a cap), and a **PCS certifying MGT-8 with false certification** is also liable to penalty.

**Conclusion:** Ridge and Vale require both director+CS signing and **MGT-8** certification; Brook (small company) files **MGT-7A**, may be signed by a director if there is no CS, and needs **no MGT-8**. All must file **within 60 days of the AGM** (or the due date of the AGM), failing which penalties accrue.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The annual return is the members' and public's **annual snapshot of ownership and governance** — shareholding, changes, indebtedness, KMP — so the law scales the assurance to the company's significance. **Signing** by a director *and* CS puts internal accountability on the return; **MGT-8 certification** by an independent PCS is an *external* audit of compliance, demanded only where the stakes are high (listed, or large by capital/turnover) because that is where mis-statement can harm the most stakeholders. **Small companies** are given a lighter regime (MGT-7A, no MGT-8) to reduce compliance cost proportionate to their limited public impact. The 60-day clock runs from the *AGM* (or its due date) because the return must reflect the position finalised at that meeting. The trap is to apply MGT-8 by only one metric — it triggers on paid-up ≥ ₹10 cr **OR** turnover ≥ ₹50 cr, so failing one but meeting the other still triggers it (Vale meets both; but even one would suffice).

*(Full-marks tip: Get the MGT-8 dual trigger (₹10 cr paid-up OR ₹50 cr turnover, plus all listed), the small-company MGT-7A/signing relief, and the 60-day-from-AGM filing rule. Deduction: requiring both metrics jointly for MGT-8, or missing the "no AGM held" filing rule.)*

---

### Q49. Ch: Management & Administration — Minutes: recording, evidentiary value & prohibitions (Sec 118) (Marks: 6) [Theory]

**Question:** The Chairman of Aster Ltd. wishes to (i) **omit** from the minutes a defamatory remark made by a member, and (ii) exercise discretion over what is recorded. A member counters that "minutes must record everything said." Examine the correct legal position on preparation of minutes under Sec 118, the Chairman's discretion, the evidentiary value of minutes, and whether minutes may be maintained loose-leaf.

**Answer:**
**Governing provision — Sec 118:**

**Preparation & time-limit:** Every company must **prepare and keep minutes** of every general meeting, and of every Board/committee meeting, and enter them in the minute books **within 30 days** of the conclusion of the meeting. Each page must be **initialled/signed and dated** by the Chairman of the same or next meeting.

**Chairman's discretion (Sec 118(5)):** The Chairman has **absolute discretion** to **exclude** from the minutes any matter which, in his opinion —
- is or could reasonably be regarded as **defamatory** of any person; or
- is **irrelevant or immaterial** to the proceedings; or
- is **detrimental to the interests of the company.**

Therefore the member's assertion that "minutes must record everything said" is **wrong**. The Chairman **may** omit the defamatory remark — indeed he is empowered to do so. However, minutes **must give a fair and correct summary of the proceedings** and must contain a **fair record of the actual decisions/resolutions**; discretion to omit does not extend to falsifying decisions.

**Evidentiary value (Sec 118(7)):** Minutes kept in accordance with Sec 118 are **evidence of the proceedings** recorded therein — they carry a **presumption** that the meeting was duly called and held, all proceedings duly took place, and all appointments are valid, until the contrary is proved.

**Loose-leaf / manner of keeping (Sec 118(10) & Secretarial Standards):** Minutes must be kept in **books** with pages consecutively numbered; **loose-leaf** minute books are permitted **only if bound periodically** and safeguarded against tampering. **Pasting or attaching** minutes onto pages, or keeping them in a manner that allows tampering, is **not permitted.** Companies must observe the **Secretarial Standards (SS-2 for general meetings)** on minutes.

**Tampering — offence (Sec 118(12)):** If a person **tampers** with the minutes, he is **punishable** with imprisonment and/or fine — reflecting the seriousness of the minute book as the authentic record.

**Conclusion:** The Chairman **may lawfully omit** the defamatory remark (Sec 118(5)) — the "record everything" claim is incorrect — but he **cannot omit or alter the actual decisions**, must complete minutes within 30 days, and the minutes then serve as **prima facie evidence** of the proceedings. Loose-leaf is allowed only if periodically bound and tamper-proof.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Minutes are meant to be a **reliable, tamper-proof record of decisions**, not a verbatim transcript — hence the Chairman is trusted with discretion to strip out defamatory, irrelevant or company-damaging matter, so the permanent record is not weaponised or cluttered. But that discretion is bounded: it covers *commentary*, never the *substance of resolutions*, because the evidentiary presumption under Sec 118(7) — that whatever the minutes say did happen — would be worthless if a Chairman could edit the decisions themselves. The strict rules on loose-leaf binding and the criminal sanction for tampering exist for the same reason: the value of minutes as legal evidence depends entirely on their integrity. The error to avoid is treating minutes as a full transcript; the law deliberately makes them a *curated but faithful* record.

*(Full-marks tip: State the 30-day rule, the Sec 118(5) discretion (defamatory/irrelevant/detrimental), the Sec 118(7) evidentiary presumption, and the loose-leaf-if-bound rule. Deduction: saying minutes must record everything, or that the Chairman can alter recorded decisions.)*

---

### Q50. Ch: Management & Administration — Integrated: notice, quorum, resolution & voting on a fundamental change (Marks: 10) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Titan Alloys Ltd. (a public company, **1,200 members**, share capital ₹10 cr) convenes an EGM to pass a **special resolution** to alter its Articles of Association. Examine the validity of the meeting and the resolution in light of the following facts and advise the Board:

| # | Fact |
|---|---|
| 1 | Notice of 21 clear days was given, but the notice **did not mention** the intention to propose the item as a special resolution; the explanatory statement under Sec 102 was also **not annexed**. |
| 2 | At the appointed time only **13 members were personally present**; 10 more attended by proxy. |
| 3 | On the resolution, on a **show of hands**, 8 voted for and 5 against; the Chairman declared it "passed as a special resolution." |
| 4 | A member holding **12% of voting power** then demanded a **poll**; on poll, votes cast were **68% in favour and 32% against.** |

**Answer:**
**Issue 1 — Validity of notice (Sec 101, 102, 114):**
A special resolution under **Sec 114(2)** requires the **intention to propose it as a special resolution to be stated in the notice**, and **Sec 102** requires an **explanatory statement** for special business. Here the notice **omitted both**. Consequently:
- The item **cannot be validly transacted as a special resolution** — the express-intention requirement is **mandatory**, not directory.
- The absence of the Sec 102 explanatory statement is a further **fatal defect** for special business.
**→ The resolution is invalid for want of proper notice, regardless of the voting outcome.**

**Issue 2 — Quorum (Sec 103):**
For a public company with **1,200 members** (more than 1,000, up to 5,000), quorum = **15 members personally present**. Only **13** were personally present; **proxies do not count** toward quorum. **→ Quorum of 15 was NOT present**, so the meeting itself was **not validly constituted**; it ought to have been adjourned under Sec 103(2). Any business transacted without quorum is **invalid**.

**Issue 3 — Voting on a show of hands (Sec 114):**
Even ignoring the above, on a show of hands 8 for : 5 against does **not** meet the special-resolution requirement that votes in favour be **at least three times** the votes against (8 is **not ≥ 3 × 5 = 15**). So the Chairman's declaration that it "passed as a special resolution" on a show of hands was **wrong** — it did not even achieve the 3:1 ratio.

**Issue 4 — Poll (Sec 109, 114):**
The demand for poll by a member holding **12% voting power** is valid (≥ 1/10th). On poll, **68% for : 32% against**. For a special resolution, votes in favour must be **≥ 3 × votes against**, i.e., in favour must be **≥ 75% of votes cast**. Here in favour is only **68% (< 75%)**; 68 is **not ≥ 3 × 32 (=96)**. **→ Even on the poll, the special resolution FAILS** (68% falls short of the 75% needed).

**Conclusion/Advice:** The purported special resolution is invalid on **four independent grounds**: (i) the notice did not state the intention to propose a special resolution and omitted the Sec 102 statement; (ii) **quorum was absent** (13 < 15; proxies don't count); (iii) the show-of-hands 8:5 did not meet the 3:1 special-majority ratio; and (iv) even on poll, **68% < 75%**. The Board must **re-issue a proper notice** (flagging the special resolution with a Sec 102 explanatory statement), ensure quorum, and secure a genuine **≥75%** majority. The AoA alteration cannot stand.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** This problem layers the *four independent gates* every fundamental resolution must clear, and failing **any one** is fatal — a lesson in why examiners love integrated facts. The **notice-of-intention** rule (Sec 114) protects members' right to know *in advance* that a constitutional change is coming, so they can attend or send a proxy; you cannot cure this at the meeting. **Quorum** (personally present, proxies excluded) ensures the decision has a real deliberative base. The **3:1 / 75% supermajority** ensures a fundamental change commands broad consent, not a chance majority in a thin room — and note the poll result (68%), though a clear majority, still fails because a special resolution is not about *winning* but about clearing a *high fixed bar*. The tempting error is to fixate only on the vote count and miss that the resolution was dead on arrival for defective notice and absent quorum; another is to think 68% "clearly passed" — it passes an *ordinary* resolution but not a *special* one.

*(Full-marks tip: Score each of the four gates separately — notice/Sec 102, quorum (13<15, proxies excluded), show-of-hands 3:1 failure, and poll 68%<75%. State that any single failure invalidates. Deduction: treating 68% as sufficient for a special resolution, or counting proxies for quorum.)*

---

### Q51. Ch: Management & Administration — Voting by electronic means, postal ballot & show of hands (Sec 108, 110) (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Examine the following with reference to the Companies Act, 2013:

| # | Situation |
|---|---|
| (i) | Meadow Ltd., a **listed company with 3,000 members**, wishes to pass all resolutions at its AGM purely on a **show of hands** and refuses to provide e-voting. Is this permissible? |
| (ii) | Crest Ltd. proposes to transact the business of **removal of a director (Sec 169)** and **buy-back beyond limits** through **postal ballot only**. Advise on the validity. |
| (iii) | Pine Ltd. provided **remote e-voting**; a member who **cast his vote by remote e-voting** also attends the meeting and wants to **vote again on a poll** at the meeting. Can he? |
| (iv) | Whether a **One Person Company (OPC)** is required to transact items by postal ballot. |

**Answer:**
**(i) Mandatory e-voting (Sec 108 & rules):** Every **listed company**, and every company having **not less than 1,000 shareholders**, is **required to provide members the facility to exercise their vote by electronic means (remote e-voting)** on resolutions. Meadow Ltd. is **listed with 3,000 members** — it **must** provide e-voting; it **cannot** confine voting to a show of hands. Refusing e-voting is a **contravention**. **→ Not permissible.**

**(ii) Business NOT to be transacted by postal ballot (Sec 110):** Certain items **must be** transacted by postal ballot (prescribed items), **but** the statute/rules **exclude** from postal ballot the items of **"ordinary business"** and **any business in respect of which members have a right to be heard at the meeting** — most importantly, **matters requiring the appointment/removal of directors and auditors** where the person has a **right to make representations and be heard**. Removal of a director under **Sec 169** carries a statutory right of the director to be **heard** at the meeting — hence it **cannot be transacted by postal ballot**; it must go to an actual meeting. (Buy-back and similar items may be transacted by postal ballot where prescribed, but the removal item cannot.) **→ Removal of director by postal ballot is invalid.**

**(iii) Double voting after remote e-voting (Sec 108 rules):** A member who has already **cast his vote by remote e-voting** **may attend** the meeting but shall **NOT be allowed to vote again** at the meeting on the same resolution; his e-vote stands. If he attends, he can participate in discussion but cannot re-vote. **→ He cannot vote again on the poll.**

**(iv) OPC and postal ballot:** For a **One Person Company** (and companies with **≤ 200 members**? — no), specifically an **OPC** and companies **required to transact only through the sole member**, the postal ballot provisions do **not apply** in the same way — an **OPC is exempt** from the postal ballot requirement because its single member's decision entered in the minutes book suffices (Sec 122). **→ OPC need not use postal ballot.**

**Conclusion:** (i) Meadow Ltd. **must** offer e-voting (listed / ≥1,000 members) — show-of-hands-only is impermissible; (ii) **removal of a director cannot be done by postal ballot** because of the director's right to be heard; (iii) a remote e-voter **cannot vote again** at the meeting; (iv) an **OPC is exempt** from postal ballot.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Each rule protects a distinct value. **Mandatory e-voting** for large/listed companies exists because members are geographically dispersed and a show-of-hands-only regime would effectively disenfranchise everyone not physically in the room — technology is used to widen participation. The **postal-ballot exclusions** rest on the principle of **natural justice / right to be heard**: where a person (a director being removed, an auditor) has a statutory right to make representations *and be heard at the meeting*, that right is meaningless if the matter is decided by a silent postal ballot — so such items are reserved for a live meeting. The **no-double-voting** rule preserves the integrity of "one member, one vote" — a member cannot amplify his voice by voting twice through two channels. The **OPC exemption** reflects that a single-member company has no minority to protect and no meeting to hold — Sec 122 lets the sole member simply record decisions. The trap is to think *all* prescribed items can be postal-balloted; the right-to-be-heard items are carved out.

*(Full-marks tip: Nail (i) the listed-OR-≥1,000-members e-voting trigger, (ii) the right-to-be-heard exclusion for director removal from postal ballot, (iii) no re-voting after remote e-voting, and (iv) the OPC exemption. Deduction: allowing director-removal by postal ballot, or permitting a second vote at the meeting.)*

### Q52. Ch: Declaration & Payment of Dividend — Dividend out of Free Reserves under Rule 3 (Marks: 8) [Problem]
**Question:** During FY 2024–25 Zenith Ltd earned inadequate profits and its Board proposes to declare dividend by drawing from accumulated free reserves. The following data is available. Examine whether the proposed dividend of 12% is valid and, if not, compute the maximum dividend the company may lawfully declare.

| Particulars | Amount (₹) |
|---|---|
| Paid-up equity share capital | 2,00,00,000 |
| General reserve (free reserve) | 1,50,00,000 |
| Securities premium | 40,00,000 |
| Capital redemption reserve | 30,00,000 |
| Net loss for FY 2024–25 (before any adjustment) | 8,00,000 |
| Rate of dividend declared — 2021–22 | 10% |
| Rate of dividend declared — 2022–23 | 14% |
| Rate of dividend declared — 2023–24 | 12% |

**Solution:**
Governing law: Second proviso to **Sec 123(1)** read with **Rule 3 of the Companies (Declaration and Payment of Dividend) Rules, 2014** — where profits are inadequate/absent, dividend may be drawn from accumulated profits transferred to free reserves, subject to FOUR cumulative conditions.

**WN-1 — Eligible free reserves.** Only reserves "freely available for distribution" count. Securities premium and Capital Redemption Reserve are NOT free reserves (statutory, restricted-use). Hence free reserves = General reserve = ₹1,50,00,000. (Paid-up capital + free reserves = 2,00,00,000 + 1,50,00,000 = ₹3,50,00,000.)

**WN-2 — Condition 1: Rate ceiling.** Rate of dividend ≤ average of the rates at which dividend was declared in the 3 immediately preceding years = (10 + 14 + 12)/3 = **12%**. Proposed 12% just equals the cap → satisfied.

**WN-3 — Condition 2: Maximum amount that can be drawn.** Amount drawn from accumulated profits ≤ 1/10th (10%) of (paid-up capital + free reserves) = 10% × 3,50,00,000 = **₹35,00,000**.

**WN-4 — Condition 3: Set-off of current loss first.** The amount so drawn shall FIRST be used to set off the loss of the current year = ₹8,00,000. Amount left for dividend = 35,00,000 − 8,00,000 = **₹27,00,000**.

**WN-5 — Condition 4: Residual reserve floor.** Balance of reserves after such withdrawal must not fall below 15% of paid-up capital = 15% × 2,00,00,000 = ₹30,00,000. Reserve after full ₹35,00,000 withdrawal = 1,50,00,000 − 35,00,000 = ₹1,15,00,000 > ₹30,00,000 → satisfied.

**WN-6 — Dividend the proposed 12% would require.** 12% × 2,00,00,000 = ₹24,00,000. But the fund available *for dividend* after mandatory loss set-off is only ₹27,00,000, and drawing to pay ₹24,00,000 dividend needs total withdrawal of 24,00,000 + 8,00,000 = ₹32,00,000 ≤ ₹35,00,000 cap → within limit.

**Statement Showing Maximum Lawful Dividend**

| Test | Limit | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Rate cap (avg of 3 yrs) | 12% | Proposed 12% = cap → OK |
| Max drawal (10% of cap+FR) | ₹35,00,000 | OK |
| Less: current-year loss set off | (₹8,00,000) | mandatory first charge |
| Amount available for dividend | ₹27,00,000 | — |
| Residual reserve floor (15% of cap) | ₹30,00,000 | Reserve left ₹1,15,00,000 → OK |

**Answer:** The proposed dividend of 12% (₹24,00,000) is **valid** — all four Rule 3 conditions are met (it needs a total drawal of ₹32,00,000, within the ₹35,00,000 cap, after setting off the ₹8,00,000 loss, leaving reserves of ₹1,15,00,000). The absolute maximum the company could distribute is ₹27,00,000 (i.e. 13.5% of capital) constrained by the 10% drawal cap net of loss set-off.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Rule 3 exists to stop a company with a bad year from "raiding" its past reserves to keep up dividend appearances and mislead investors about performance. Each condition guards a different mischief: the *rate cap* prevents paying MORE in a lean year than the recent average; the *10% drawal cap* limits how much history you can consume; the *loss set-off first* ensures reserves plug the hole before rewarding shareholders; and the *15% floor* keeps a permanent cushion so the company is never hollowed out. The classic trap is treating securities premium and CRR as "free reserves" — they are statutorily earmarked (Sec 52/55) and inflate the base wrongly. The second trap is forgetting the loss set-off is a *first charge* on the drawn amount, not a separate deduction from capital.

*(Full-marks tip: Examiners reward showing all four tests explicitly and excluding securities premium/CRR from free reserves. Common deductions: computing the 15% floor on (capital+reserves) instead of paid-up capital alone, and skipping the mandatory current-loss set-off.)*

---

### Q53. Ch: Declaration & Payment of Dividend — Depreciation, Transfer to Reserve & Interim Dividend (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Examine the validity of each of the following Board decisions of Aarav Ltd for FY 2024–25 with reasons:
(i) The Board declared a final dividend of 15% without providing depreciation for the year, arguing that depreciation is only a book entry.
(ii) The Board resolved to transfer 20% of profits to general reserve before dividend, and separately claims that such transfer is compulsory.
(iii) On 10 February 2025 the Board declared an interim dividend of 18% out of the profits of FY 2024–25, even though the company had incurred a net loss during the period 1 April 2024 to 31 December 2024. Average dividend of the three preceding years was 11%.

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 123** and its provisos.

(i) **Invalid.** Sec 123(1)(a)/(b): dividend can be declared only out of profits *arrived at after providing for depreciation* in accordance with Schedule II. Depreciation is a real charge reflecting the consumption of assets used to earn the profit; ignoring it overstates distributable profit and returns capital as dividend. The "mere book entry" argument fails — the statute makes depreciation a non-negotiable pre-condition. Decision is illegal.

(ii) **Partly wrong.** Under the Companies Act, 2013 transfer to reserve before dividend is **voluntary**, not compulsory (the erstwhile mandatory-transfer rule of the 1956 Act was omitted). A company *may* transfer such percentage of profits as it considers appropriate. So a 20% transfer is permissible, but the Board's claim that it is *compulsory* is legally incorrect.

(iii) **Invalid as declared.** Sec 123(3) permits interim dividend out of surplus in P&L and out of profits of the current financial year. But the proviso says: if the company has incurred a loss during the current FY *up to the end of the quarter immediately preceding the date of declaration*, the interim dividend shall not be declared at a rate higher than the average dividend declared during the three immediately preceding financial years. Here loss up to 31 Dec 2024 (quarter preceding 10 Feb 2025) exists, so the cap is 11%. An 18% interim dividend is invalid; the Board may declare at most 11%.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** All three limbs test whether "profit" is *real* and *prudently* distributed. Depreciation must be charged because dividend law protects creditors — capital must be maintained, and paying dividends out of undepreciated profit silently distributes capital. The reserve-transfer point tests whether students still carry the outdated 1956-Act reflex; the 2013 Act deliberately made it discretionary to give boards flexibility. The interim-dividend proviso addresses a specific mischief: a company doing badly for most of the year could still declare a lavish interim dividend on a temporary cash surplus, so the law throttles the rate to the recent average when the year-to-date shows a loss.

*(Full-marks tip: For (iii) the marks hinge on identifying the "quarter immediately preceding" loss trigger and the average-of-3-years cap. Do not confuse voluntary reserve transfer with the old compulsory slabs — a frequent error.)*

---

### Q54. Ch: Declaration & Payment of Dividend — Unpaid Dividend & Transfer of Shares to IEPF (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Mr. Kabir held 5,000 equity shares in Meghdoot Ltd. Dividend was declared for FY 2016–17 through FY 2023–24, but Kabir never encashed any of the eight dividends. The company failed to transfer the FY 2016–17 unpaid dividend to the Unpaid Dividend Account within the statutory time. In 2025 the company proposes to transfer Kabir's shares to the IEPF. Kabir objects, claiming the shares are his property and cannot be taken away. Examine (a) the company's default on the unpaid dividend account, (b) whether transfer of shares to IEPF is valid, and (c) Kabir's remedy.

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 124** and **Sec 125** read with IEPF Rules.

(a) **Default on Unpaid Dividend Account.** Sec 124(1): where dividend declared is not paid/claimed within 30 days of declaration, the company must, within 7 days from expiry of those 30 days, transfer the unpaid amount to a special "Unpaid Dividend Account" in a scheduled bank. Failing this, Sec 124(3) makes the company liable to pay interest @ 12% p.a. from the date of default, and the interest enures to the benefit of the members in proportion to the unpaid amount. So Meghdoot Ltd is liable for 12% interest on the FY 2016–17 dividend.

(b) **Transfer of shares to IEPF — valid.** Sec 124(5): any money in the Unpaid Dividend Account remaining unpaid/unclaimed for **7 years** from the date it became due is transferred to the IEPF. Crucially, Sec 124(6): *all shares* in respect of which dividend has not been paid or claimed for **7 consecutive years or more** shall also be transferred to the IEPF (in the manner prescribed). Kabir has not claimed dividend for eight consecutive years (2016–17 to 2023–24). Therefore the transfer of the 5,000 shares to IEPF is **valid** — his "it's my property" objection fails because the statute overrides ordinary title to protect long-abandoned investor wealth and route it to a public fund.

(c) **Remedy.** Kabir is not deprived of value. Under Sec 124(6) proviso / Sec 125(3)(a), the claimant is entitled to **claim back both the transferred shares and the unpaid dividend from the IEPF Authority** by making an application in the prescribed form (Form IEPF-5). His economic interest survives; only custody moves to the Fund.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The seven-year architecture balances two policy goals: it stops companies from perpetually holding "orphan" dividends and shares (which they could misuse or benefit from), while never extinguishing the true owner's claim. That is why the design is *transfer, not forfeiture* — the shares and money go to a government fund but remain reclaimable forever by the rightful owner. The common misconception (Kabir's) is that private property cannot be moved without consent; the answer is that Sec 124(6) is a statutory override justified by the mischief of unclaimed wealth, and the safeguard is the reclaim mechanism, not immunity from transfer. Note the trigger for share transfer is non-claiming of *dividend* for 7 consecutive years — not non-transfer of money.

*(Full-marks tip: State all three time-limits (30 days, 7 days, 7 years), the 12% interest for default, AND the reclaim right via IEPF-5. Losing the reclaim remedy or confusing forfeiture with transfer is the usual deduction.)*

---

### Q55. Ch: Declaration & Payment of Dividend — Punishment for Non-Payment & Exceptions (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Solstice Ltd declared a final dividend at its AGM on 20 August 2024. By 25 September 2024, dividend had not been paid to several shareholders. Examine the consequences under Sec 127, and whether the company/directors can escape liability in the following independent situations: (i) a shareholder had given contradictory instructions on where to remit the dividend and the company could not comply; (ii) the dividend was lawfully adjusted against a debt due from the shareholder to the company; (iii) the delay was due to a bank strike within the company's control.

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 127** and its proviso.

**Main rule:** Dividend once declared must be paid within **30 days** of declaration. Declaration on 20 Aug 2024 → last date 19 Sep 2024. Non-payment by 25 Sep = default. Consequence: **every director knowingly a party** to the default is punishable with imprisonment up to 2 years AND fine not less than ₹1,000 per day of continuing default; the **company** is liable to pay simple interest @ 18% p.a. during the default period.

**Exceptions (proviso to Sec 127) — no offence is committed where:**
(i) **Contradictory/valid instructions could not be complied with, and this was communicated** — if the shareholder gave directions that the company could not comply with and the company informed him, **no default** arises. Solstice escapes liability here.
(ii) **Lawful adjustment against a debt** — where the dividend was lawfully adjusted against a sum due from the shareholder, **no default**. Solstice escapes liability.
(iii) **Bank strike within control** — the proviso protects against causes "not due to any default on the part of the company / beyond its control". A strike *within* the company's control is NOT a valid excuse → **default stands**, directors and company liable.

**Conclusion:** Situations (i) and (ii) fall within the statutory exceptions and attract no penalty; situation (iii) does not, and liability under Sec 127 applies.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Sec 127 is a shareholder-protection provision — once a company declares a dividend, it becomes a *debt* owed to members, so the law enforces prompt payment with harsh personal liability on directors to deter foot-dragging. But the proviso recognises that non-payment is only culpable when it is the company's fault. The distinguishing principle across the three fact-patterns is **fault and control**: the company is excused only where the obstacle originates with the shareholder or is genuinely beyond the company's control. A strike "within its control" fails this test because the company could have prevented or resolved it — the exception is about blamelessness, not mere inconvenience.

*(Full-marks tip: Quote the 30-day limit, the dual penalty (director imprisonment + 18% interest by company), and map each fact to the correct proviso clause. The trap is (iii) — students often reflexively excuse "strike" as force majeure, missing the "within control" qualifier.)*

---

### Q56. Ch: Declaration & Payment of Dividend — Dividend, Free Reserves & Set-off of Past Losses (Marks: 6) [Problem]
**Question:** For FY 2024–25 Orion Ltd has profits but carries brought-forward losses and unabsorbed depreciation. Determine the amount of profit legally available for dividend out of current-year profits under Sec 123(1).

| Particulars | Amount (₹) |
|---|---|
| Net profit for FY 2024–25 after tax and after providing current-year depreciation | 40,00,000 |
| Brought-forward debit balance in P&L (accumulated loss) | 22,00,000 |
| Unabsorbed depreciation of earlier years (included in the above ₹22,00,000: ₹9,00,000) | 9,00,000 |
| Balance of general reserve (free reserve) | 5,00,000 |

**Solution:**
Governing law: **Sec 123(1)** — dividend out of current profits requires setting off the *lower* of brought-forward losses and unabsorbed depreciation of previous years before profits become distributable. (This mirrors the third proviso preventing distribution while past losses/depreciation remain unwiped.)

**WN-1 — Identify the two competing figures.**
- Accumulated business loss carried forward (excluding unabsorbed depreciation) = 22,00,000 − 9,00,000 = **₹13,00,000**.
- Unabsorbed depreciation of previous years = **₹9,00,000**.

**WN-2 — Apply the "lower of" set-off rule.** Before declaring dividend out of current profits, the company must set off the *lower* of (brought-forward loss) and (unabsorbed depreciation) = lower of ₹13,00,000 and ₹9,00,000 = **₹9,00,000**.

**WN-3 — Compute distributable profit.**
Distributable = Current profit − set-off = 40,00,000 − 9,00,000 = **₹31,00,000**.

**Statement Showing Profit Available for Dividend**

| Particulars | ₹ |
|---|---|
| Net profit for FY 2024–25 (after depreciation & tax) | 40,00,000 |
| Less: lower of B/f loss (₹13,00,000) and unabsorbed depreciation (₹9,00,000) | (9,00,000) |
| **Profit available for dividend** | **31,00,000** |

**Answer:** ₹31,00,000 is legally available for dividend out of current-year profits.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The rule protects capital by ensuring a company does not pay dividends while it is still, in substance, recovering from past losses — but the legislature chose the *lower* of the two figures (not both, not the higher) as a deliberate, calibrated restraint. Setting off the smaller amount strikes a balance: it prevents the harsher outcome of blocking dividends until *all* past losses/depreciation are wiped, while still requiring meaningful erosion of accumulated deficits. The common error is deducting the full ₹22,00,000 (both losses + depreciation) or the higher figure — that misreads the provision, which asks only for the *lower* of loss vs unabsorbed depreciation.

*(Full-marks tip: Explicitly separate business loss from unabsorbed depreciation, then take the LOWER. Deducting the full accumulated balance is the standard mark-losing mistake.)*

---

### Q57. Ch: Declaration & Payment of Dividend — Payment in Cash, Capitalisation & Prohibition (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Comment on the validity of the following proposals of Vayu Ltd:
(i) The Board proposes to pay dividend to certain shareholders "in kind" by distributing sample products of the company instead of cash.
(ii) The company, having defaulted in repayment of deposits accepted from the public and the default continuing, proposes to declare dividend for FY 2024–25.
(iii) The company proposes to capitalise its profits to issue fully paid bonus shares, and calls this a "dividend in the form of bonus shares."

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 123(5)** and the general scheme of dividend law.

(i) **Invalid.** Sec 123(5): dividend shall be paid only in **cash** (payable by cheque, warrant, or electronic mode) to the registered shareholder or to his order. Distribution of goods/products "in kind" as dividend is not permitted. (The only exception recognised is capitalisation of profits into bonus shares, which is a distinct mechanism, not dividend in kind.) Hence the proposal is invalid.

(ii) **Invalid while default subsists.** Sec 123(6): a company that has failed to comply with **Sec 73 and Sec 74** (acceptance/repayment of deposits) shall **not**, so long as such failure continues, declare any dividend on its equity shares. Since the deposit-repayment default continues, Vayu Ltd is barred from declaring dividend for FY 2024–25. It must first cure the default.

(iii) **Not a dividend, but valid as a bonus issue.** Capitalising profits to issue fully paid bonus shares is governed by Sec 63, not the dividend provisions; calling it "dividend in the form of bonus shares" is a misnomer. It is valid *as a bonus issue* provided Sec 63 conditions are met (authorised by articles, recommended by Board, approved in general meeting, out of free reserves/securities premium/CRR, etc.), but it must not be characterised or accounted for as a cash dividend.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The cash-only rule exists to protect shareholders from receiving illiquid or over-valued goods in lieu of a genuine return, and to keep dividend an objective, equal, per-share entitlement. The deposit-default bar (Sec 123(6)) enforces a creditor-first priority: a company that cannot repay depositors has no business rewarding shareholders — paying dividend while defaulting would prefer owners over creditors, inverting the risk hierarchy. The bonus-share point tests conceptual precision: a bonus issue capitalises reserves into share capital (no cash leaves the company and no distribution occurs), so it is structurally the opposite of a dividend and lives under a different section.

*(Full-marks tip: Cite Sec 123(5) for cash-only and Sec 123(6) for the deposit-default bar precisely. The subtle mark is distinguishing bonus shares (Sec 63, capitalisation) from dividend — do not conflate the two.)*

---

### Q58. Ch: Accounts of Companies — CSR Applicability & Spend Computation (Sec 135) (Marks: 10) [Problem]
**Question:** Determine whether CSR provisions apply to Helios Ltd for FY 2024–25 and, if so, compute the amount the company is required to spend on CSR. Also state the treatment of any shortfall.

| Financial Year | Net Worth (₹ cr) | Turnover (₹ cr) | Net Profit u/s 198 (₹ cr) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2021–22 | 180 | 640 | 3.20 |
| 2022–23 | 210 | 720 | 6.50 |
| 2023–24 | 240 | 860 | 4.80 |
| 2024–25 (current) | 265 | 940 | 7.10 |

**Solution:**
Governing law: **Sec 135(1)** (applicability), **Sec 135(5)** (2% spend), and the Companies (CSR Policy) Rules, 2014.

**WN-1 — Applicability test (Sec 135(1)).** CSR applies if, **during the immediately preceding financial year** (i.e. FY 2023–24), the company has EITHER: net worth ≥ ₹500 cr, OR turnover ≥ ₹1,000 cr, OR net profit ≥ ₹5 cr.
FY 2023–24 figures: Net worth ₹240 cr (< 500), Turnover ₹860 cr (< 1,000), **Net profit ₹4.80 cr (< 5)**. On the face of it, none of the thresholds is crossed in 2023–24.

**WN-2 — Which "preceding year"?** Sec 135(1) tests the *immediately preceding financial year*. For FY 2024–25 that is FY 2023–24, where net profit is ₹4.80 cr — below ₹5 cr — and net worth/turnover are also below limits. **Therefore CSR is NOT triggered for FY 2024–25.** (The current year's ₹7.10 cr profit is irrelevant to the *trigger*; it would test applicability for FY 2025–26.)

**WN-3 — Spend computation (hypothetical, had it applied).** Had CSR applied, Sec 135(5) requires spending **at least 2% of the average net profit (u/s 198) of the three immediately preceding financial years**:
Average = (3.20 + 6.50 + 4.80)/3 = 14.50/3 = ₹4.8333 cr.
2% × 4.8333 = **₹9.67 lakh** would have been the obligation.

**Statement**

| Test | Requirement | Helios (FY 2023–24) | Met? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Net worth | ≥ ₹500 cr | ₹240 cr | No |
| Turnover | ≥ ₹1,000 cr | ₹860 cr | No |
| Net profit u/s 198 | ≥ ₹5 cr | ₹4.80 cr | No |

**Answer:** CSR provisions do **not** apply to Helios Ltd for FY 2024–25, because in the immediately preceding FY 2023–24 it crossed none of the three thresholds (profit ₹4.80 cr < ₹5 cr). Consequently there is **no mandatory CSR spend** for FY 2024–25 and no shortfall question arises. (Applicability must be re-tested each year; based on FY 2024–25 profit of ₹7.10 cr, CSR will apply for FY 2025–26, with the spend computed as 2% of the average net profit of 2022–23, 2023–24 and 2024–25.)

**Note on shortfall (for completeness):** Where CSR *does* apply, any unspent amount relating to an *ongoing project* must be transferred within 30 days of year-end to a separate "Unspent CSR Account" and spent within 3 financial years; unspent amounts *not* relating to ongoing projects must be transferred within 6 months of year-end to a fund specified in Schedule VII (e.g. PM National Relief Fund).

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The single most examined trap in Sec 135 is *which year's figures* trigger applicability versus *which years' figures* fix the amount. Applicability keys off the **immediately preceding** financial year (a single-year test), so the tempting jump to the healthy current-year profit is wrong — the statute wants a *lagged* trigger so a company knows in advance whether it is caught. The *quantum*, by contrast, uses a **three-year average** to smooth out volatility so the obligation is not whipsawed by one exceptional year. Using "net profit" here means net profit computed under **Sec 198** (not book profit), which strips out capital profits and certain items to give a normalised managerial-remuneration-style profit. Missing the ₹4.80 cr < ₹5 cr distinction — treating 4.80 as "close enough" — is a fatal error; thresholds are bright lines.

*(Full-marks tip: Nail the immediately-preceding-year trigger vs three-year-average quantum split, use Sec 198 profit, and state the shortfall/Unspent-CSR-Account mechanics even when concluding non-applicability. The examiner rewards re-testing for the next year.)*

---

### Q59. Ch: Accounts of Companies — Maintenance, Place & Period of Books (Sec 128) (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Examine the following with reference to Sec 128:
(i) Nimbus Ltd maintains its books of account only at a branch office in another city, not at its registered office, and has not intimated the Registrar. Is this valid?
(ii) The company maintains all its books in electronic form on a server located outside India, with periodic back-ups also kept only abroad. Comment.
(iii) After a search-and-seizure and an ongoing investigation, the company proposes to destroy books of account that are 7 years old, relying on the general 8-year retention rule. Advise.

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 128** and the Companies (Accounts) Rules, 2014.

(i) **Permissible with conditions.** Sec 128(1) requires books to be kept at the registered office, BUT the proviso allows all or any of the books to be kept at *any other place in India* as the Board decides, provided the company files a **notice in writing with the Registrar (Form AOC-5) within 7 days**, giving the full address of that other place. So keeping books at the branch is valid *only if* the Board so decided and AOC-5 was filed; since Nimbus has not intimated the Registrar, it is **non-compliant**.

(ii) **Partly non-compliant.** Books may be kept in electronic form. But the Accounts Rules require that where books are maintained electronically, the information must remain **accessible in India** so as to be usable for subsequent reference, and a **back-up of the books maintained/received from outside India must be kept in servers physically located within India** on a periodic basis. Keeping the only back-up abroad violates this. The company must maintain an India-located back-up and ensure accessibility from India.

(iii) **Advice: do not destroy.** Sec 128(5): books together with vouchers must be kept in good order for **not less than 8 financial years** immediately preceding the current year. Books only 7 years old cannot be destroyed. Further, the proviso: where an **investigation has been ordered**, the Central Government may direct that books be kept for a **longer period**. With an ongoing investigation, destruction is impermissible; the company must retain the records until at least 8 years AND for any longer period directed. Destroying them would also risk penal and adverse inference consequences.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Sec 128 balances operational flexibility with regulatory traceability. Books may move around India and go electronic because modern business is distributed and digital — but the *quid pro quo* is transparency (file AOC-5) and sovereignty over data (India-located, accessible back-ups) so that regulators and auditors are never locked out. The retention rule exists so that a full audit trail survives long enough to detect and prosecute historic wrongdoing; the investigation-extension proviso recognises that a fraud may surface late, so records must be frozen while scrutiny is live. The recurring trap is treating "8 years" as an absolute right to destroy thereafter — it is a *minimum*, overridden upward by an investigation order.

*(Full-marks tip: Cite AOC-5 + 7-day filing for (i), the India back-up/accessibility rule for (ii), and the 8-year minimum PLUS investigation extension for (iii). Forgetting the investigation override is the common deduction.)*

---

### Q60. Ch: Accounts of Companies — Financial Statements, Consolidation & Non-Compliance (Sec 129) (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Comment on the following under Sec 129:
(i) Aether Ltd, having two subsidiaries and one associate, prepares only standalone financial statements, arguing consolidation is optional for unlisted companies.
(ii) The company's financial statements do not give a true and fair view because a material provision was deliberately omitted to inflate profits. Who is liable and what is the consequence?
(iii) The Board wishes to know whether a "salient features" statement of subsidiaries must accompany the financial statements, and in what form.

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 129** and the Companies (Accounts) Rules, 2014.

(i) **Incorrect.** Sec 129(3): where a company has one or more subsidiaries **or associate companies**, it shall, *in addition to* its standalone financial statements, prepare **consolidated financial statements (CFS)** of the company and all its subsidiaries and associates, in the same form and manner, and lay them before the AGM. This is **not optional** based on listing status. Aether Ltd must prepare CFS covering both subsidiaries and the associate. (Limited exemptions exist for certain wholly-owned intermediate subsidiaries, but the blanket "optional for unlisted" claim is wrong.)

(ii) **Liability for false/non-true-and-fair statements.** Sec 129(1) mandates that financial statements give a **true and fair view** and comply with the applicable accounting standards. Sec 129(7): if a company contravenes Sec 129, the **managing director, whole-time director in charge of finance, CFO, or any other person charged by the Board with compliance** (or, absent such person, all directors) shall be punishable with imprisonment up to 1 year and/or fine ₹50,000–₹5,00,000. Deliberate omission of a material provision to inflate profits squarely attracts this — the officers in default are liable.

(iii) **Yes — Form AOC-1.** Under Sec 129(3) proviso and the Accounts Rules, a separate statement containing the **salient features of the financial statements of subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures** must be attached, in **Form AOC-1**. This lets shareholders see each group entity's key figures without wading through full accounts.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Consolidation is mandatory because a group's economic reality cannot be judged from a parent's standalone accounts — intra-group transactions, subsidiary losses, and associate exposures are invisible standalone, so CFS exists to give investors a *group-wide* true and fair view. The "optional for unlisted" belief is a stubborn myth; the trigger is *having* a subsidiary/associate, not being listed. Section 129(7) personalises liability on the officer actually responsible for compliance because true-and-fair reporting fails when a specific person games it, and deterrence works only if it bites that individual. AOC-1 is the transparency bridge — a digestible summary so the group picture is accessible.

*(Full-marks tip: Stress that CFS is triggered by subsidiaries OR associates OR JVs regardless of listing, name Form AOC-1, and identify the specific officers-in-default under Sec 129(7). Saying "consolidation is only for listed companies" loses the core mark.)*

---

### Q61. Ch: Accounts of Companies — Financial Statement Signing, Board's Report & Circulation (Sec 134) (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Examine, with reasons, the validity of the following in relation to Cygnus Ltd (a company that is not an OPC):
(i) The financial statements were signed by only the company secretary and the CFO, no director having signed them.
(ii) The Board's Report did not contain the Directors' Responsibility Statement or any disclosure on conservation of energy, the Board considering these "immaterial."
(iii) The Board's Report was signed by the CFO on behalf of the Board.

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 134**.

(i) **Invalid.** Sec 134(1): the financial statements, including consolidated ones, shall be approved by the Board and signed on behalf of the Board by at least the **chairperson (if authorised) OR two directors, one of whom shall be the managing director** where there is one, and the CEO (if a director), the CFO and the company secretary **wherever they are appointed**. In every case there must be director signatures. Signing only by the CS and CFO, with no director, is invalid.

(ii) **Invalid.** Sec 134(3): the Board's Report must include, among many mandatory disclosures, the **Directors' Responsibility Statement** (Sec 134(5)) and **conservation of energy, technology absorption, foreign exchange earnings and outgo**. These are statutorily mandated contents, not discretionary; the Board cannot omit them on a self-serving "immateriality" view. The Report is defective.

(iii) **Invalid.** Sec 134(6): the Board's Report and any annexures shall be **signed by the chairperson of the company if authorised by the Board, or where he is not so authorised, by at least two directors, one of whom shall be the managing director, or by the director where there is one director.** The CFO cannot sign the Board's Report on behalf of the Board — signing authority is confined to directors/chairperson.
