**Why this way (the reasoning):** Section 134 fixes *accountability* on the Board. Financial statements and the Board's Report are the primary documents on which shareholders and the public rely, so the law insists that **directors** put their names to them — directors, not employees, bear governance responsibility, and their signature is the legal act of ownership of the numbers and narrative. The Directors' Responsibility Statement is the heart of this: it is the board's sworn assertion that accounting standards were followed, judgments were prudent, internal financial controls were adequate, and the going-concern basis is sound. Allowing a CFO/CS to sign in place of directors, or letting the board self-certify content as "immaterial," would hollow out this accountability. The mischief addressed is diffusion of responsibility — the statute deliberately concentrates it on named directors.

*(Full-marks tip: For each limb state WHO must sign and WHY directors are indispensable. Listing the mandatory Board's Report contents (esp. Directors' Responsibility Statement) earns the (ii) marks; do not accept "immateriality" as a defence.)*

---

### Q62. Ch: Accounts of Companies — Internal Financial Controls & Directors' Responsibility (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]
**Question:** During the audit of Polaris Ltd (a listed company), the auditor finds that the Directors' Responsibility Statement asserts that the directors "had laid down internal financial controls to be followed by the company and that such controls are adequate and operating effectively." Management, however, admits no formal IFC framework was documented. Examine the position under Sec 134(5) and the auditor's related obligation, and explain why this assertion matters.

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 134(5)(e)** read with **Sec 143(3)(i)**.

**Directors' obligation.** Sec 134(5)(e): in the case of a **listed company**, the Directors' Responsibility Statement must state that the directors had laid down **internal financial controls (IFC)** to be followed by the company and that such IFC are **adequate and operating effectively**. IFC means the policies and procedures adopted for orderly and efficient conduct of business, safeguarding of assets, prevention/detection of fraud and error, accuracy and completeness of accounting records, and timely preparation of reliable financial information. A statement asserting adequate and effective IFC where **no framework has even been documented** is a false statement — the directors cannot truthfully make the Sec 134(5)(e) assertion.

**Auditor's obligation.** Sec 143(3)(i): the auditor's report must state whether the company has **adequate internal financial controls with reference to financial statements in place and the operating effectiveness of such controls**. The auditor cannot issue a clean IFC opinion; he must report the **material weakness / absence of documented IFC** and appropriately qualify/adverse-comment on the IFC opinion.

**Conclusion:** The Directors' Responsibility Statement is defective and cannot be truthfully made; the auditor must report the IFC deficiency under Sec 143(3)(i) rather than corroborate the directors' assertion.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Post-Satyam, the law made IFC a *board-level accountability* precisely because financial fraud thrives where controls are weak and no one owns them. Section 134(5)(e) forces directors to publicly certify not merely that controls *exist* but that they are *adequate and operating effectively* — a genuine, testable assertion, not a boilerplate line. Section 143(3)(i) then makes the auditor an independent check on that very assertion, closing the loop so directors cannot self-certify unchallenged. The assertion matters because investors rely on it as an assurance that the numbers are protected by a real control environment; a hollow certification is a governance red flag and exposes the directors to liability for an untrue statement.

*(Full-marks tip: Pair Sec 134(5)(e) (board's certification) with Sec 143(3)(i) (auditor's independent IFC opinion) and explain the accountability loop between them. Missing the auditor's Sec 143(3)(i) duty is the usual deduction.)*

---

### Q63. Ch: Accounts of Companies — Filing of Financial Statements & Consequences of Default (Sec 137) (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Meridian Ltd held its AGM for FY 2023–24 on 25 September 2024 but did not adopt the financial statements as they were not ready; the accounts remained un-adopted. Separately, for FY 2022–23 the company failed to file its adopted financial statements with the Registrar at all. Advise on the filing obligations and consequences under Sec 137.

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 137**.

**(A) Un-adopted financial statements (FY 2023–24).** Sec 137(1) first proviso: where the financial statements are **not adopted** at the AGM (or adjourned AGM), the **un-adopted** financial statements shall be filed with the Registrar within **30 days of the date of the AGM**, and the Registrar shall take them as provisional until the adopted ones are filed. Once adopted at the adjourned AGM, the **adopted** statements must be filed within **30 days of the adjourned AGM**. So Meridian cannot simply skip filing — it must file the un-adopted statements (as provisional) within 30 days of 25 Sept 2024, then file adopted ones within 30 days of the adjourned AGM.

**(B) Complete failure to file (FY 2022–23).** Filing a copy of the financial statements (with attachments) in **Form AOC-4** with the Registrar within **30 days of the AGM** is mandatory. Consequences of default under Sec 137(3): the **company** is liable to penalty (a fixed amount plus a per-day continuing penalty subject to a cap), and the **managing director/CFO** (or, in their absence, other specified directors/officers in default) are also liable to penalty. Persistent non-filing is also a ground for various disqualifications and adverse action. Meridian must file the FY 2022–23 statements immediately and bear the penalty for the default period.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Section 137 exists so that the *public record* of a company's financials stays current — the Registrar's filings are what creditors, investors and regulators rely on. That is why the law refuses to let a company hide behind "accounts not adopted": it must still file the *un-adopted* statements as provisional, preserving transparency and a paper trail, then update the record once adoption happens. The penalty structure (on both company and responsible officers) reflects that timely disclosure is a continuing public duty, and the continuing per-day penalty is designed to make prolonged concealment progressively costly rather than a one-time fine that can be shrugged off.

*(Full-marks tip: The examiner looks for the un-adopted-statements provisional-filing route (30 days) AND the adopted-filing follow-up (30 days of adjourned AGM), plus AOC-4 and dual company/officer penalties. Saying "no filing is due because accounts weren't adopted" is the fatal error.)*

---

### Q64. Ch: Audit & Auditors — Appointment, Casual Vacancy & Rotation (Sec 139) (Marks: 10) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Analyse each situation for Stellar Ltd, an unlisted public company with paid-up share capital of ₹12 crore, under Sec 139:
(i) The Board did not appoint the first auditor within 30 days of incorporation. What is the correct procedure and timeline now?
(ii) At the 1st AGM, the company appointed M/s Rao & Co. as auditors. The Board later passed a resolution "ratifying" the appointment at each subsequent AGM. Is annual ratification required?
(iii) M/s Rao & Co. resigned mid-year creating a casual vacancy. How is it filled?
(iv) M/s Rao & Co. has already served as auditor for two consecutive terms of 5 years each. Can the company re-appoint them, and if not, when can they return? Does rotation even apply to Stellar Ltd?

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 139** and Rule 5/6 of the Companies (Audit and Auditors) Rules, 2014.

(i) **First auditor default (Sec 139(6)/(8)).** The first auditor must be appointed by the **Board within 30 days of registration**. If the Board fails, it must inform the **members**, who shall appoint the first auditor within **90 days at an extraordinary general meeting (EGM)**. The first auditor holds office till the conclusion of the **first AGM**. So Stellar must convene an EGM and have members appoint the first auditor within 90 days.

(ii) **No ratification required.** Sec 139(1): an auditor appointed at the AGM holds office from that AGM till the conclusion of the **6th AGM** (i.e. for 5 years), subject to the appointment being made. The erstwhile proviso requiring annual **ratification** by members has been **omitted** (Companies (Amendment) Act, 2017). The Board's "ratification" resolutions are legally unnecessary and of no effect; the appointment already runs for the full five-year block.

(iii) **Casual vacancy by resignation (Sec 139(8)).** A casual vacancy caused by **resignation** must be filled by the **Board within 30 days**, but such appointment must **also be approved by the company at a general meeting convened within 3 months** of the Board's recommendation; the auditor so appointed holds office till the conclusion of the next AGM. (Had the vacancy arisen otherwise than by resignation, the Board alone could fill it within 30 days.)

(iv) **Rotation — does it apply?** Sec 139(2) and Rule 5: mandatory rotation applies to listed companies AND to **unlisted public companies with paid-up share capital ≥ ₹10 crore** (among others). Stellar's paid-up capital is ₹12 crore → **rotation applies.** For an **audit firm**, the ceiling is **two consecutive terms of 5 years** (10 years). M/s Rao & Co. has completed both terms, so it **cannot be re-appointed** now. It must observe a **cooling-off period of 5 years** before it is eligible for re-appointment as auditor of Stellar Ltd.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Section 139 engineers auditor *independence and continuity* through carefully staged appointment rules. The first-auditor cascade (Board → members) ensures a company is never without an auditor from birth. Removing annual ratification was deliberate: ratification votes had become a backdoor pressure point letting management threaten auditors yearly, undermining independence — a fixed five-year term insulates the auditor. The casual-vacancy-by-resignation rule requires member approval because a *resignation* often signals a dispute or concern, so members (not just the board) should sanction the replacement, guarding against management quietly installing a friendly auditor. Rotation exists to break the "familiarity threat" — an auditor too long in the saddle grows cozy with management — hence firms rotate after two terms with a 5-year cooling-off so fresh eyes periodically scrutinise the accounts. The trap in (iv) is assuming rotation is only for listed companies; the ₹10 crore paid-up-capital limb sweeps in unlisted public companies like Stellar.

*(Full-marks tip: Four crisp sub-answers with the exact section and timeline each. The high-value marks are (ii) no ratification (post-2017) and (iv) rotation applicability via the ₹10 cr limb + 5-year cooling-off. Confusing resignation vacancy (needs GM approval) with other vacancies (Board alone) is a common slip.)*

---

### Q65. Ch: Audit & Auditors — Eligibility, Qualifications & Disqualifications (Sec 141) (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Determine, with reasons, whether each person/firm is disqualified from being appointed/continuing as auditor of Comet Ltd under Sec 141:
(i) Mr. A, a chartered accountant, owes Comet Ltd ₹6,00,000 as an unsecured personal loan.
(ii) Mrs. B is proposed as auditor; her spouse holds equity shares of Comet Ltd of face value ₹1,50,000.
(iii) M/s C & Associates is a firm in which a partner is simultaneously the statutory auditor of 21 other companies (none being OPC/dormant/small companies and all having paid-up capital ≥ ₹100 crore).
(iv) Mr. D, proposed auditor, has a subsisting business relationship with Comet Ltd through a consultancy arrangement.

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 141(3)** and Rule 10 of the Audit Rules.

(i) **Disqualified.** Sec 141(3)(d)(ii): a person who is **indebted** to the company for an amount exceeding **₹5,00,000** is disqualified. Mr. A owes ₹6,00,000 > ₹5,00,000 → disqualified.

(ii) **Disqualified.** Sec 141(3)(d)(i): a person is disqualified if he, **or his relative or partner**, holds any **security or interest** in the company. Proviso: a **relative** may hold securities of **face value not exceeding ₹1,00,000**. The spouse (a relative) holds shares of face value ₹1,50,000 > ₹1,00,000 → Mrs. B is disqualified. (Had it been ≤ ₹1,00,000, it would be within the relative's permissible limit.)

(iii) **Disqualified — ceiling breached.** Sec 141(3)(g): a person cannot be auditor of **more than 20 companies** at a time (the ceiling counts only companies that are not OPC/dormant/small/private companies with paid-up capital < ₹100 cr). Here all 21 count toward the ceiling. Adding Comet Ltd would make it beyond 20 → the firm/partner is disqualified from accepting Comet's audit.

(iv) **Disqualified.** Sec 141(3)(e): a person who, directly or indirectly, has a **business relationship** with the company (other than professional services permitted under the Act and certain ordinary-course commercial arm's-length transactions) is disqualified. A subsisting consultancy business relationship with Comet Ltd disqualifies Mr. D.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Every limb of Sec 141(3) attacks the same root threat: anything that compromises the auditor's **independence and objectivity**. Indebtedness beyond a threshold means the auditor is financially beholden to the auditee — he cannot fearlessly qualify accounts of a company that is his creditor/debtor, so a bright ₹5 lakh line is drawn. Shareholding (self, relative, or partner) creates a *self-interest* threat — an owner-auditor benefits from rosy accounts — with a tiny ₹1 lakh face-value tolerance for relatives to avoid absurd disqualification over trivial holdings. The 20-company ceiling protects **audit quality**: an auditor spread across too many clients cannot devote adequate attention, so the number is capped (with sensible carve-outs for low-risk company types). The business-relationship bar removes *self-review and advocacy* threats — you cannot audit a company you are also commercially entangled with. Understanding these as *threat categories* (self-interest, familiarity, self-review, intimidation) lets a student reason to the answer rather than memorise thresholds.

*(Full-marks tip: State the exact monetary limits — ₹5,00,000 indebtedness, ₹1,00,000 relative shareholding, 20-company ceiling — and identify the independence threat each addresses. The (ii) trap is forgetting the relative's ₹1 lakh permissible limit and just saying "any shareholding disqualifies.")*

---

### Q66. Ch: Audit & Auditors — Removal, Resignation & Fraud Reporting (Sec 140 & 143) (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Advise the Board of Nebula Ltd on the following:
(i) The Board wishes to remove the statutory auditor before expiry of his term because his audit is "too rigorous." What is the correct procedure?
(ii) The auditor, having discovered discrepancies, simply stopped work and left without filing anything. What is his statutory obligation on resignation?
(iii) During the audit, the auditor has reason to believe that an offence of fraud involving ₹1.2 crore is being committed against the company by its officers. What must he do, and how does the answer differ if the fraud amount were ₹80 lakh?

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 140** (removal/resignation) and **Sec 143(12)** with Rule 13 (fraud reporting).

(i) **Removal before term (Sec 140(1)).** An auditor may be removed before expiry of his term only by a **special resolution** of the company, AFTER obtaining the **previous approval of the Central Government (powers delegated to the Regional Director)** in the prescribed **Form ADT-2**, filed within 30 days of the Board resolution; the special resolution must be passed within 60 days of CG approval. Additionally, the auditor must be given a **reasonable opportunity of being heard**. Removing an auditor merely for being "too rigorous" is precisely the abuse the process guards against — the CG safeguard exists to prevent management from ousting an inconvenient but honest auditor.

(ii) **Resignation obligation (Sec 140(2)/(3)).** An auditor who resigns must file a statement in **Form ADT-3** with the **company and the Registrar** (and, for government companies, also with the CAG) within **30 days** of resignation, indicating the **reasons and other facts** relevant to his resignation. Simply abandoning the audit without filing ADT-3 is a default attracting penalty under Sec 140(3). The reasons-disclosure ensures the true cause (here, discrepancies) is on record and not buried.

(iii) **Fraud reporting (Sec 143(12)).** Where an auditor, in the course of performing his duties, has reason to believe an **offence of fraud** involving/expected to involve **₹1 crore or more** is being committed against the company by its officers/employees, he must **report it to the Central Government** — first reporting to the Board/Audit Committee within 2 days seeking their reply within 45 days, then forwarding his report with the reply (or noting no reply) to the CG in **Form ADT-4** within 15 days of receiving the reply. The ₹1.2 crore fraud → **report to Central Government** (ADT-4). If the fraud were **₹80 lakh (< ₹1 crore)**: the auditor does **not** report to CG; instead he reports to the **Board/Audit Committee**, and the details are **disclosed in the Board's Report**.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** These provisions collectively protect the auditor's role as the shareholders' and public's watchdog. Removal is deliberately made *hard* — special resolution PLUS central-government approval PLUS a hearing — because the easiest way to defeat an audit is to sack a diligent auditor; the multi-layered gate ensures removal is scrutinised by an outside authority, not rubber-stamped by the very management being audited. The ADT-3 resignation-with-reasons duty prevents an auditor from quietly walking away from a problem client, leaving no trace of why — the reasons on record alert successors and regulators. The fraud-reporting threshold splits at ₹1 crore to calibrate escalation to severity: large frauds go straight to the Central Government (an external enforcement channel management cannot suppress), while smaller ones are handled internally with board disclosure — proportionate response without either trivialising serious fraud or over-burdening the CG with every minor irregularity.

*(Full-marks tip: For removal, all three elements — special resolution + CG (RD) approval via ADT-2 + opportunity of hearing. For fraud, the ₹1 crore split, the 2-day/45-day/15-day timeline, ADT-4 vs Board's Report. The classic error is filing the special resolution before CG approval, or reporting sub-₹1cr fraud to the CG.)*

---

### Q67. Ch: Audit & Auditors — Prohibited Services & Auditor's Powers/Duties (Sec 144 & 143) (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Examine the following in respect of the auditor of Quasar Ltd:
(i) In addition to statutory audit, the auditor's firm proposes to provide (a) internal audit, (b) actuarial services, (c) investment banking services, and (d) routine tax representation, to Quasar Ltd. Which are permissible under Sec 144?
(ii) The auditor of the holding company demands access to the records of a subsidiary for consolidation purposes. Is he entitled?
(iii) The auditor wishes to know whether his duty to report extends to matters not asked in the engagement, such as whether loans made by the company are prejudicial to its interests.

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 144** (prohibited services) and **Sec 143** (powers/duties).

(i) **Prohibited services (Sec 144).** An auditor shall NOT provide, directly or indirectly, certain services to the company, its holding or subsidiary, namely: accounting/book-keeping, **internal audit**, design/implementation of financial information systems, **actuarial services**, **investment advisory/investment banking services**, rendering of outsourced financial services, and management services. Applying this: (a) internal audit — **prohibited**; (b) actuarial services — **prohibited**; (c) investment banking services — **prohibited**; (d) **routine tax representation** — permissible, as tax services/other non-prohibited attestation and permitted services approved by the Board/Audit Committee are allowed. So only (d) may be rendered.

(ii) **Access to subsidiary records — yes (Sec 143(1) proviso).** The auditor of a holding company has, for the purposes of **consolidation**, the **right of access to the records of all subsidiaries and associate companies** in so far as relevant. The subsidiary must provide access. His demand is valid.

(iii) **Duty extends to prescribed inquiry matters (Sec 143(1)).** The auditor must, whether or not specifically asked, **inquire into** statutorily listed matters — including whether **loans and advances made by the company have been properly secured and whether the terms are prejudicial to the interests of the company or its members**, whether transactions represented merely by book entries are prejudicial, whether personal expenses have been charged to revenue, etc. So yes — his duty independently extends to examining whether the company's loans are prejudicial; it is not limited to what the engagement letter asks.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Section 144 codifies the **self-review and advocacy** threats: if an auditor also does the internal audit, designs the systems, or advises on investments, he ends up auditing his own work or advocating the client's position — destroying objectivity. So the Act draws a firewall between assurance and these conflicting services (extending it to holding/subsidiary to stop circumvention through the group), while permitting neutral services like tax representation that don't compromise the audit. Section 143's access-to-subsidiary right exists because a consolidated true-and-fair opinion is impossible if the group auditor is walled off from subsidiary data — the right is a functional necessity, not a courtesy. And the mandatory-inquiry duties reflect that audit is a *public-interest* function: the auditor is a guardian against asset-stripping and related-party abuse, so the law hard-codes certain probes (like prejudicial loans) that he must perform regardless of a narrow engagement scope.

*(Full-marks tip: List the Sec 144 prohibited services and correctly classify each proposed service; cite the Sec 143(1) proviso for subsidiary access and the mandatory-inquiry list. The trap is treating tax representation as prohibited — it is not.)*

---

### Q68. Ch: Audit & Auditors — Remuneration, Signing & Punishment for Contravention (Sec 142, 143(3), 147) (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]
**Question:** Comment on the following concerning the auditor of Vela Ltd:
(i) The Board unilaterally fixed the auditor's remuneration for the year without any reference to the members. Is this valid?
(ii) The audit report was signed by a partner of the audit firm who is not a chartered accountant but handles the firm's administration. Comment.
(iii) The auditor, in collusion with management, knowingly failed to report a material misstatement. What is the consequence, and does it differ if the failure was merely negligent rather than fraudulent?

**Answer:**
Governing law: **Sec 142** (remuneration), **Sec 141(2)/145** (signing), **Sec 147** (penalties).

(i) **Generally invalid unless within delegated authority.** Sec 142(1): the remuneration of the auditor shall be fixed **in general meeting** or in such manner as the general meeting may determine. Exception: the remuneration of the **first auditor** may be fixed by the **Board**. For a subsequent auditor, the Board cannot unilaterally fix remuneration unless the members have authorised the manner. So the Board's unilateral fixing (for other than the first auditor, absent member authorisation) is invalid.

(ii) **Invalid.** Sec 141(2): where a firm is appointed as auditor, **only the partners who are chartered accountants** are authorised to act and sign on behalf of the firm. Sec 145 requires the auditor's report to be **signed by the auditor** (or, in a firm, an authorised CA partner). A non-CA administrative partner **cannot sign** the audit report. The signing is invalid.

(iii) **Punishment under Sec 147.** Sec 147(2): if an auditor contravenes Sec 139/143/144/145, he is punishable with fine ₹25,000–₹5,00,000 or **four times the audit fee**, whichever is less. **But** if the contravention is done **knowingly or wilfully with intent to deceive** the company/shareholders/creditors/tax authorities — i.e. the fraudulent collusion here — the auditor is punishable with **imprisonment up to 1 year AND fine ₹50,000 up to ₹25,00,000** (or 8 times the fee, whichever is less), plus he is liable to **refund the remuneration** and pay for **damages/losses** (Sec 147(3)/(4)). Where fraud involves public interest, imprisonment can extend and minimum enhances. So the wilful/collusive failure attracts the **aggravated** punishment (imprisonment + higher fine + refund + damages). If the failure were **merely negligent** (no intent to deceive), only the **lesser monetary penalty** under Sec 147(2) applies — no imprisonment.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Section 142 vests remuneration-fixing in the **members**, not the board, so the auditor is beholden to shareholders (whose interests he protects) rather than to the management he audits — control over an auditor's pay is control over the auditor, so it is placed at the members' level. The signing restriction ensures that only a **qualified professional** who bears ICAI's disciplinary accountability puts his name to the opinion; a non-CA signature would divorce responsibility from competence and regulatory oversight. Section 147 deliberately grades punishment by **culpability**: an honest mistake attracts a monetary penalty, but *knowing, deceitful* audit failure — the gravest betrayal of the assurance function — draws imprisonment, restitution of fees, and damages, because such collusion is what enables large-scale corporate fraud. The intent-based split teaches that liability tracks *mens rea*, not just outcome.

*(Full-marks tip: Distinguish first-auditor (Board fixes remuneration) from subsequent (general meeting), restrict signing to CA partners, and — the key mark — contrast negligent (fine only) vs fraudulent (imprisonment + refund + damages) auditor liability under Sec 147(2) vs 147(3)/(4).)*

### Q69. Ch: LLP Act 2008 — Nature of LLP & Partner Liability (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** ABC LLP has three partners — Anand, Bhavesh and Chetan. Bhavesh, while acting in the ordinary course of the LLP's business, negligently gives wrong professional advice to a client, Delta Ltd, causing it a loss of ₹40 lakh. The LLP's assets are only ₹15 lakh. Chetan, an entirely non-executive partner, argues he cannot be touched because "an LLP is a limited liability partnership, so no partner is ever personally liable." Anand, on the other hand, had personally supervised and approved Bhavesh's wrongful advice. Delta Ltd wants to recover its full ₹40 lakh. Examine the extent to which (a) the LLP, (b) Bhavesh, (c) Anand and (d) Chetan are liable. Would your answer change if Bhavesh had committed the wrong to *defraud* the client with the LLP's knowledge?

**Answer:**
**Governing provisions — Sections 27 and 28 of the LLP Act, 2008.**

*Principle 1 — The LLP is liable for the wrongful acts of a partner (Sec 27(2)).* An LLP is liable to the same extent as the partner where a partner, in the course of the business of the LLP or with its authority, does a wrongful act or omission. Bhavesh's negligent advice was given in the ordinary course of the LLP's business, so **the LLP is liable to Delta Ltd for the full ₹40 lakh.**

*Principle 2 — LLP liabilities are met out of LLP property (Sec 27(4)).* An obligation of the LLP, whether arising in contract or otherwise, is solely the obligation of the LLP, and its liabilities are met out of the property of the LLP. Hence the LLP must first apply its ₹15 lakh of assets.

*Principle 3 — The contracting/wrongdoing partner remains personally liable.* The limited-liability shield protects a partner from being liable for the obligations of the LLP *merely by reason of being a partner* (Sec 28(1)). It does **not** shield a partner from his **own** wrongful act. Therefore **Bhavesh is personally liable** for his own negligence — the shortfall of ₹25 lakh can be recovered from him personally.

*Principle 4 — Anand's position.* Anand personally supervised and approved the wrongful advice; he is not a mere partner but a participant in the wrong. His liability flows from his **own** conduct, not from Sec 28's "by reason of being a partner." **Anand is personally liable** jointly with Bhavesh.

*Principle 5 — Chetan's position.* Under Sec 28(1), a partner is **not** personally liable, directly or indirectly, for an obligation of the LLP solely by reason of being a partner. Chetan neither committed nor authorised the wrong. **Chetan is NOT personally liable**; his exposure is limited to his contribution/interest in the LLP. His argument is correct only *for himself*, not as a blanket rule.

**Fraud variant — Sec 30.** If the wrong was done to defraud creditors/any person **with the LLP's knowledge/consent**, the liability of the LLP **and** of the partners who acted with intent to defraud becomes **unlimited**. Then Delta could pursue the LLP and the defrauding partners without limit; but a partner *not* party to the fraud (Chetan) still stays protected.

**Conclusion:** LLP — liable for full ₹40 lakh from its property; Bhavesh & Anand — personally liable for the ₹25 lakh shortfall; Chetan — not personally liable. In the fraud case, the fraud-partners' liability is unlimited under Sec 30.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The whole architecture of the LLP is a hybrid — it borrows the *body-corporate* liability shield from company law and the *agency* concept from partnership. Sec 28's shield exists to encourage passive investment/partnership without the joint-and-several unlimited exposure of a general firm; but the legislature never intended it to let a wrongdoer escape his own tort. So the shield attaches to the *capacity* ("by reason of being a partner"), not to the *conduct*. The tempting wrong answer is Chetan's — treating "limited liability" as an absolute, entity-wide immunity. That misreads Sec 27(3), which expressly says a partner is not personally liable for the wrongful acts of *another* partner — the flip side being that he IS liable for his *own*. Sec 30 then reflects the anti-abuse policy: limited liability is a privilege, and fraud forfeits it.

*(Full-marks tip: the examiner rewards separating the four persons individually and citing Sec 27(2)/(4), 28(1), 27(3) and 30 by number. Common deduction: writing "no partner is ever liable" — you must distinguish own-wrong liability from vicarious liability, and flag the Sec 30 fraud carve-out.)*

---

### Q70. Ch: LLP Act 2008 — Incorporation, Reservation of Name & DPIN (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Two individuals wish to incorporate "Innova Solutions LLP." Examine the validity of each of the following, giving reasons and the exact provision:
(a) One of the two proposed partners is a body corporate (a private company); the other is an individual who does **not** hold a DPIN.
(b) They reserve the name and then do nothing for 4 months, after which they file the incorporation documents.
(c) The incorporation document (Form FiLLiP) is subscribed by both partners, but the statement of compliance is signed only by an advocate, not by any partner.
(d) After incorporation they discover a competitor already had the identical name; the competitor applies to the Central Government within 20 months of registration to direct a change of name.

**Answer:**
**(a) Minimum partners & DPIN.** Under **Sec 5**, any individual **or body corporate** may be a partner; a company can validly be a partner in an LLP. Under **Sec 6(1)** every LLP must have at least **two** partners — satisfied here. However, under **Sec 7**, every LLP must have at least **two designated partners** who are individuals, at least one resident in India, and each designated partner **must obtain a DPIN (Designated Partner Identification Number)**. A body corporate cannot itself be a designated partner (it acts through a nominee individual). Since the only individual partner has no DPIN and a company cannot personally be a designated partner, the LLP **cannot be incorporated** until the requisite two individual designated partners with DPIN are in place. **Invalid as structured.**

**(b) Lapse of reserved name.** Under **Sec 16 / Rule 18**, a reserved name is available only for a limited period (**3 months** from the date of intimation of reservation). Doing nothing for **4 months** means the reservation has **lapsed**; they cannot incorporate on the strength of the expired reservation and must re-apply. **Invalid.**

**(c) Statement of compliance — Sec 11(1)(c).** The incorporation document must be filed with a statement that **all requirements of the Act have been complied with**, made by (i) an advocate/CA/CS/Cost Accountant engaged in the formation, **AND** (ii) **anyone who subscribed his name** to the incorporation document. Signature by the advocate alone is **not sufficient**; at least one subscriber-partner must also sign the statement. **Invalid — defective filing.**

**(d) Direction to change name — Sec 17.** Where a name is identical/too nearly resembles that of an existing LLP or body corporate, the aggrieved person may apply to the **Central Government** within the prescribed period. Post-amendment, the Central Government may **suo motu or on application** direct the LLP to change its name. The competitor's application within 20 months is maintainable and the CG may direct a name change. **Valid application.**

**Conclusion:** (a) invalid — need two individual DPs with DPIN; (b) invalid — reservation lapsed after 3 months; (c) invalid — subscriber must co-sign statement of compliance; (d) valid — CG may direct change of name.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Incorporation of an LLP is a *compliance-verified* birth, not a private agreement — that is why the Act builds a chain of gatekeepers: a floor of two partners (capacity), a floor of two individual designated partners (accountability — someone human must answer for filings and penalties), and a professional-plus-subscriber attestation (truth of the record). The classic trap in (a) is thinking "a company can be a partner, therefore a company can run the LLP" — capacity to be a *partner* (Sec 5) is different from the *designated-partner* accountability role (Sec 7), which the law deliberately pins on real individuals who can be prosecuted. In (c), requiring a subscriber's signature — not just the professional's — ensures the persons who benefit from incorporation personally vouch for compliance.

*(Full-marks tip: cite Sec 5, 6, 7, 11, 16 and 17 distinctly; the marker looks for the DPIN-vs-partner distinction in (a) and the "3-month lapse" in (b). Deduction for saying a company can be a *designated* partner.)*

---

### Q71. Ch: LLP Act 2008 — Conversion of a Firm into LLP & Effect of Conversion (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** M/s Rao & Sons, a partnership firm with four partners, converts into "Rao & Sons LLP" under the Second Schedule with effect from 1 April 2026. Analyse:
(a) On conversion, what happens to (i) the firm's existing contracts, (ii) a pending suit filed by a creditor against the firm, and (iii) a car registered in the firm's name?
(b) A creditor of the erstwhile firm whose debt arose *before* conversion sues after conversion. Against whom can he proceed, and to what extent are the ex-partners liable?
(c) For how long, and in what manner, must the LLP disclose that it was converted from a firm?
(d) The firm had one partner who was a minor (admitted to benefits). Can the firm still convert?

**Answer:**
**Legal basis — Sec 55 read with the Second Schedule.**

**(a) Effect of conversion (Para 6 of the 2nd Schedule).** On and from the date of registration: **all tangible/intangible property, assets, interests, rights, privileges, liabilities and obligations** of the firm are **transferred to and vest in the LLP by operation of law**, and the firm is **deemed dissolved**.
- (i) Existing contracts continue and are enforceable by/against the LLP as if it were the firm — no fresh novation is needed.
- (ii) The **pending suit** does not abate; it continues **by or against the LLP** (Para 7 — legal proceedings pending are continued by/against the LLP).
- (iii) The **car vests in the LLP automatically**; but where the property was registered/recorded with an authority (RTO), the LLP must **notify the authority within the prescribed period (60 days)** to effect the change of records. Vesting is by law; the notification is administrative.

**(b) Liability for pre-conversion obligations (Para 8 read with Sec 58(4)).** Notwithstanding vesting, **every partner of the erstwhile firm continues to be personally liable, jointly and severally with the LLP, for the liabilities and obligations incurred *before* conversion** or arising from anything done before conversion. If a partner discharges such a liability, he is **entitled to be indemnified by the LLP**. So the creditor may proceed against the **LLP and against the ex-partners personally** for the pre-conversion debt; the limited-liability shield does **not** apply retrospectively to old debts.

**(c) Disclosure of conversion (Para 9 / Sec 58).** For **12 months** from the date of registration, every official correspondence of the LLP must state (i) that it was, as from the date of registration, **converted from a firm into an LLP**, and (ii) the **name and registration number** of the firm from which it was converted. Default attracts a penalty.

**(d) Minor partner.** Conversion of a **firm** into an LLP requires that **all partners of the firm, and no one else, become partners of the LLP** (eligibility condition, 2nd Schedule). An LLP partner must have contractual capacity; a **minor cannot be a partner** in an LLP. Therefore, so long as a minor is a partner of the firm, the "same partners and no one else" condition cannot be satisfied and the firm **cannot convert** until the minor's position is resolved. **Cannot convert as constituted.**

**Conclusion:** All firm property/contracts/suits vest in and continue against the LLP by law; ex-partners remain jointly & severally liable for pre-conversion debts (with indemnity from the LLP); conversion must be disclosed for 12 months; a firm with a minor partner cannot convert.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Conversion is a *universal succession by operation of law*, not a sale — that is why no individual assignment/novation of each contract or asset is needed; the statute itself moves the whole bundle so that business continuity is preserved and third parties are not prejudiced. But the same policy of "not prejudicing third parties" cuts the other way for old creditors: they dealt with unlimited-liability partners, so the Act refuses to let conversion strip away that security — hence continuing personal liability for pre-conversion debts (with an internal indemnity so the burden ultimately rests on the LLP). The 12-month disclosure rule is a *notice* device so that persons dealing with the entity know the liability regime has changed. The minor trap tests whether a student knows the "identical partners" gate plus the capacity requirement.

*(Full-marks tip: examiners reward citing "by operation of law/vesting," the joint-and-several pre-conversion liability with indemnity, the 60-day authority-notification, and the 12-month disclosure. Deduction for saying old creditors lose recourse against partners.)*

---

### Q72. Ch: LLP Act 2008 — LLP vs Company vs Firm: Distinguishing on Facts (Marks: 6) [Theory/Application]

**Question:** A group of consultants is deciding between (i) a general partnership firm, (ii) a private company and (iii) an LLP. For **each** of the following features, state which form(s) satisfy it and briefly justify, so as to advise them: (a) separate legal personality and perpetual succession; (b) liability of members limited to contribution; (c) a member acting as agent binding the *other members* personally; (d) mandatory statutory audit irrespective of size; (e) minimum and maximum number of members; (f) a member being able to enter into a contract with the entity itself.

**Answer:**

| Feature | Firm | Company | LLP | Justification |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| (a) Separate legal personality & perpetual succession | No | Yes | **Yes** | LLP is a **body corporate** (Sec 3, LLP Act) with perpetual succession; a firm has no personality distinct from its partners. |
| (b) Liability limited to contribution | No | Yes | **Yes** | LLP partner's liability is limited (Sec 28) except for own wrong/fraud; firm partners have unlimited joint & several liability. |
| (c) One member's act binding **other members personally** | Yes | No | **No** | In a firm every partner is agent of the *firm and of the other partners*; in an LLP a partner is agent of the **LLP only, not of other partners** (Sec 26). |
| (d) Compulsory audit irrespective of size | No | Yes | **No** | LLP audit is required only if turnover **> ₹40 lakh** or contribution **> ₹25 lakh** (LLP Rules); a company's accounts must always be audited. |
| (e) Min / Max members | 2 / 50 (Cos Rules) | Pvt: 2 / 200 | **2 / no maximum** | LLP: minimum 2 partners (Sec 6), **no upper limit**; a firm is capped at 50; a private company at 200. |
| (f) Member can contract with the entity itself | No (can't contract with self) | Yes | **Yes** | LLP being a separate person, a partner **may lend to, or transact with, the LLP** (Sec 66 / Sch One); a firm has no separate persona to contract with. |

**Advice:** If the consultants want a separate legal person, limited liability, no compulsory audit at small scale, no cap on numbers, and freedom to transact with the entity — while avoiding the heavier compliance of a company — the **LLP** is the optimal middle path. It gives the corporate shield of a company with the internal flexibility of a partnership.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The LLP was legislated precisely as a *hybrid* to remove the false choice between the flexibility of a firm and the shield of a company. The key conceptual pivot is agency: in a firm mutual agency exposes each partner to every other's acts (which is why professionals feared it); the LLP breaks that by making the *entity* — not the partners — the principal (Sec 26). Once you grasp that the LLP has separate personality, features (a), (b) and (f) all follow automatically: a distinct person can own property, survive its members, cap their liability and contract with them. The trap in (d) is assuming an LLP is audited like a company — its audit is threshold-based, a deliberate lightening of compliance.

*(Full-marks tip: a comparison table with a one-line *reason* per cell scores full marks; naked yes/no without the section/threshold loses marks. The prized insight is the Sec 26 "agent of LLP, not of other partners" point.)*

---

### Q73. Ch: LLP Act 2008 — Extent of Liability & "Holding Out" (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Ravi retired from XYZ LLP on 1 January 2026 but the LLP failed to file the notice of his cessation with the Registrar and continued to use old letterheads bearing his name. On 15 February 2026, a supplier, Mr. Sen, extended credit of ₹6 lakh to the LLP relying on the letterhead, believing Ravi was still a partner. Ravi had, however, personally informed Mr. Sen by email on 5 January 2026 that he had ceased to be a partner. The LLP defaults. Can Mr. Sen hold Ravi liable? Decide with reasons.

**Answer:**
**Governing provision — Sec 24 (cessation of partnership interest) read with Sec 29 (holding out).**

*Rule on cessation:* Under **Sec 24(3)–(6)**, a person who has ceased to be a partner continues to be regarded, **in relation to any person dealing with the LLP**, as still a partner **unless** (a) that person has **notice** that he has ceased to be a partner, **or** (b) **notice of cessation has been delivered to the Registrar**. Cessation is effective *inter se* on the date of retirement, but as against outsiders it needs either actual notice to them or public notice via the Registrar.

*Application:* The LLP did **not** file the cessation notice with the Registrar — so the "public notice" limb is not satisfied, and the stale letterhead reinforced the appearance. **However**, Ravi had given Mr. Sen **actual, direct notice** by email on 5 January 2026 — *before* the credit was extended on 15 February. The statute is satisfied if **either** limb is met; here the "actual notice to the dealing person" limb is met for Mr. Sen specifically.

*Holding out (Sec 29):* Liability by holding out arises only where a person **knowingly** permits himself to be represented as a partner and the creditor **relies** on that representation. Since Mr. Sen already **knew** Ravi had ceased, he cannot claim to have genuinely relied on the letterhead as against Ravi.

**Conclusion:** Mr. Sen **cannot** hold Ravi liable for the ₹6 lakh. Although the LLP breached its filing duty, Ravi's direct prior notice to Mr. Sen defeats Sen's claim; reliance — the foundation of holding-out liability — is absent. (Ravi remains free of liability; the LLP and continuing partners are liable, and the LLP is exposed to penalty for the filing default.)

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The provision protects *innocent reliance*, not *notice-defeated* claims. The law gives a retiring partner two independent ways to cut off future liability — tell the world (Registrar filing) or tell the specific person dealing with the LLP. Because the tests are in the alternative, actual knowledge in the creditor collapses the estoppel: estoppel/holding-out requires a representation *believed and acted upon*, and you cannot "believe" what you have been told is false. The tempting wrong answer fixates on the LLP's failure to file — but that default makes the *LLP* liable to penalty and protects *other* creditors who lacked notice; it cannot manufacture liability against Ravi for a creditor who actually knew the truth.

*(Full-marks tip: the marker wants the "either/or" structure of Sec 24 and the *reliance* element of Sec 29 explicitly applied to Sen's actual knowledge. Deduction for holding Ravi liable merely because the Registrar filing was missing.)*

---

### Q74. Ch: LLP Act 2008 — Contribution, Business Losses & Distribution of Profits (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** In PQR LLP the agreement is silent on several matters. During FY 2025-26 the following disputes arise. Apply the default rules under the **First Schedule** (which govern where the LLP agreement is silent) and decide each:
(a) Partner P contributed ₹20 lakh while Q and R contributed ₹5 lakh each. Q claims profits must be shared in the ratio of contribution (4:1:1). Is Q right?
(b) P wants to introduce a new partner, S. R objects. Can S be admitted?
(c) Q, without consent, starts a competing consultancy business and earns ₹3 lakh. Must he account for it to the LLP?
(d) R incurs ₹50,000 of personal expense in the ordinary and proper conduct of the LLP's business and seeks reimbursement. Is he entitled?
(e) P wants remuneration of ₹1 lakh/month for actively managing the LLP. Is he automatically entitled?

**Answer:**
**Governing rules — First Schedule (default provisions applicable where the LLP agreement / Sec 23 is silent).**

**(a)** Default rule: **all partners are entitled to share equally in the capital, profits and losses of the LLP**, irrespective of their contribution. The First Schedule does **not** link profit-sharing to contribution. Hence profits are shared **equally (1:1:1)**, not 4:1:1. **Q is wrong.**

**(b)** Default rule: **no person may be introduced as a partner without the consent of all existing partners.** R has objected. Therefore **S cannot be admitted.** **P fails.**

**(c)** Default rule: if a partner **carries on a competing business** of the same nature as the LLP without consent, he must **account for and pay over to the LLP all profits** made in that business. Hence **Q must hand over the ₹3 lakh** to the LLP.

**(d)** Default rule: the LLP shall **indemnify each partner for payments made and personal liabilities incurred by him in the ordinary and proper conduct of the business** of the LLP (or to preserve its business/property). R's expense qualifies. **R is entitled to ₹50,000 reimbursement.**

**(e)** Default rule: **no partner is entitled to remuneration for acting in the business or management of the LLP.** Managing actively does not, by itself, create an entitlement. So **P is not automatically entitled** to the ₹1 lakh/month; he needs an agreed term in the LLP agreement.

**Conclusion:** (a) equal sharing, Q wrong; (b) S cannot be admitted; (c) Q must account for ₹3 lakh; (d) R reimbursed ₹50,000; (e) P not entitled to remuneration absent agreement.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The First Schedule is a *gap-filler* — it applies only to the extent the LLP agreement is silent, mirroring the fairness defaults of partnership law. The unifying logic is the **fiduciary, equal-partners model**: absent bargaining, the law assumes partners are equals (equal profits, unanimous admission), owe undivided loyalty (disgorge competing profits — the no-conflict rule), are made whole for firm expenses (indemnity), but are presumed to serve the venture for their *profit share, not a salary* (no automatic remuneration). Students trip on (a) by importing a capital-ratio assumption from company shareholding — but LLP defaults are partnership-flavoured, deliberately equal. The remedy in each case is chosen to restore the presumed bargain of loyal equals.

*(Full-marks tip: preface every answer with "the agreement being silent, First Schedule applies" — that framing earns marks. Deduction for sharing profits by contribution or granting automatic remuneration.)*

---

### Q75. Ch: LLP Act 2008 — Designated Partners: Liability & Vacancy (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** LMN LLP has two designated partners, X and Y. Examine:
(a) Y resigns on 1 March 2026, leaving only one designated partner. The LLP fails to appoint a replacement for 5 months. What are the consequences and who bears the liability during the vacancy?
(b) A statutory Annual Return (Form 11) is filed late, attracting penalty. The LLP argues that penalty can be imposed only on the LLP, not on the designated partners. Is this correct?
(c) X, a designated partner, contends that as a designated partner he enjoys *greater* limitation of liability than ordinary partners. Comment.
(d) Can the LLP function at all if **all** designated partners vacate office?

**Answer:**
**Governing provisions — Sec 7, 8 and 9.**

**(a) Vacancy below the minimum (Sec 7 read with Sec 9).** Every LLP must have **at least two designated partners** who are individuals, at least one **resident in India**. On Y's resignation the number falls to one; **Sec 9** requires the LLP to fill a vacancy in designated partners **within 30 days**. Default for 5 months breaches Sec 9. Where **no designated partner is appointed, or at any time there is only one**, **each partner** is deemed to be a designated partner (Sec 7(4)). Consequently **all partners** become answerable for designated-partner obligations during the vacancy, and the LLP and its partners are liable to penalty for the default.

**(b) Liability for penalty (Sec 8).** A designated partner is **specifically responsible** for doing all acts, matters and things required to be done by the LLP under the Act (filings, compliances) and is **liable to all penalties** imposed on the LLP for contravention of those provisions. So the penalty for the late Form 11 can be visited **on the designated partners too**, not the LLP alone. **The LLP's argument is wrong.**

**(c) "Greater" limitation of liability.** A designated partner does **not** enjoy any *greater* limitation of liability. On the contrary, Sec 8 loads him with **additional statutory responsibility and penal exposure** for the LLP's compliances. His limited liability qua *business obligations* is the same as any partner (Sec 28); his compliance liability is **heavier**, not lighter. **X's contention is incorrect.**

**(d) All designated partners vacate.** The LLP does not cease to exist, but it is in default of Sec 7. By operation of **Sec 7(4)**, if no designated partner remains (or only one), **every partner is deemed a designated partner** — so the LLP still "has" designated partners by deeming, and each partner must discharge those duties until proper appointments are made. It cannot lawfully *continue in default*; it must appoint within 30 days.

**Conclusion:** (a) breach of Sec 9; during vacancy every partner is a deemed DP and liable; (b) penalties reach the designated partners under Sec 8; (c) a DP has *more* liability, not less; (d) by deeming, all partners become DPs until appointments are made.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The designated partner is the LLP's *human compliance anchor* — the Act needs an identifiable, prosecutable individual because a body corporate cannot itself be jailed or reasoned with. That is why Sec 8 deliberately front-loads penal responsibility onto the DP: limited liability protects *investment*, but it must not create a *compliance vacuum* where no one is accountable to the Registrar. Sec 7(4)'s deeming rule is an anti-avoidance safety net — the LLP cannot dodge accountability by simply leaving the DP posts empty; the responsibility springs onto *every* partner. The seductive wrong idea in (c) — that "designated" sounds like a privilege — inverts reality: designation is a burden, not a benefit.

*(Full-marks tip: cite Sec 7(4) deeming, the 30-day fill window (Sec 9), and Sec 8 penal responsibility. Deduction for saying only the LLP (not the DPs) bears penalty.)*

---

### Q76. Ch: LLP Act 2008 — Whistleblowing, Fraud & Unlimited Liability (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]

**Question:** In Sigma LLP, partners A and B, with the LLP's consent, arrange the business to defraud a lender of ₹80 lakh. Partner C, an employee-partner, discovers the fraud and voluntarily provides useful information to the authorities that helps establish the case. Meanwhile the lender sues. Examine (a) the extent of liability of the LLP and of A and B; (b) whether C can be granted any relief; (c) whether reduction of penalty for C is a *right* or a *discretion*.

**Answer:**
**Governing provisions — Sec 30 (unlimited liability in case of fraud) and Sec 31 (whistle-blowing).**

**(a) Unlimited liability for fraud (Sec 30).** Where the LLP or any of its partners acts **with intent to defraud creditors** of the LLP or any other person, or **for any fraudulent purpose**, the **liability of the LLP and the partners who acted with such intent is unlimited** for all debts/liabilities of the LLP. The limited-liability shield **falls away**. Hence the LLP, and **A and B** (who acted with fraudulent intent, with the LLP's consent), are **liable without limit** for the ₹80 lakh — the lender may pursue their personal assets. (Sec 30 also carries criminal punishment — imprisonment and fine.) Importantly, this unlimited liability attaches only to the **partners who had the fraudulent intent**; an innocent partner is not swept in.

**(b) Relief for the whistle-blower (Sec 31).** The Court/Tribunal **may reduce or waive any penalty** leviable against a partner or employee of an LLP if it is satisfied that such person has **provided useful information** during the investigation. Further, **no partner or employee may be discharged, demoted, suspended, threatened, harassed or discriminated against** merely because he provided such information. So **C may be granted reduction/waiver of penalty** and is protected from victimisation.

**(c) Right or discretion?** The provision uses **"may"** — the reduction/waiver of penalty for the informer is a **discretion** of the Court/Tribunal, exercisable on being satisfied about the usefulness of the information, **not an automatic right** claimable by C. However, the **protection against victimisation is a protection (a bar on retaliation), not a discretionary favour.**

**Conclusion:** LLP and A, B — unlimited liability for the ₹80 lakh (plus criminal exposure) under Sec 30; C — eligible for discretionary reduction/waiver of penalty and mandatory anti-victimisation protection under Sec 31.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Sec 30 encodes the moral limit of the limited-liability bargain: the shield is a *privilege granted for honest enterprise*, and the law withdraws it the moment the enterprise is turned to fraud — otherwise the LLP form would be a fraud-proofing device. Sec 31 is an *enforcement-economics* provision: fraud is hard to detect from outside, so the law buys insider cooperation by dangling penalty relief and guaranteeing job protection. The "may/shall" distinction matters — the legislature made the *reward* discretionary (to prevent collusive fake-informing and preserve judicial judgement of usefulness) but made the *protection from retaliation* firm, because a discretionary shield would deter genuine whistle-blowers.

*(Full-marks tip: separate the *unlimited-liability* consequence (Sec 30) from the *whistle-blower relief* (Sec 31), and nail the "may = discretion" point. Deduction for treating penalty waiver as an automatic entitlement or sweeping innocent partners into unlimited liability.)*

---

### Q77. Ch: General Clauses Act 1897 — Computation of Time (Marks: 6) [Problem/Application]

**Question:** Apply **Sec 9 and Sec 10** of the General Clauses Act, 1897 and decide each of the following, giving the exact reasoning:

| # | Facts |
|---|---|
| (a) | A statute allows an appeal "within 30 days **from** the date of the order." The order is dated **1 March 2026**. What is the last date to file? |
| (b) | A notice must be served "for at least 15 days **beginning with** 10 April 2026." Does 10 April count? |
| (c) | An act is required to be done "within 60 days," and the **60th day (say 30 June 2026) is a public holiday** on which the office is closed. Can it be done the next day? |
| (d) | A limitation period expires on a day the court is closed for the **entire period** of the last few days due to vacation. Does Sec 10 help? |
| (e) | A time is fixed as "**from** 1 April 2026 **to** 30 April 2026" for a benefit — is 1 April included? |

**Solution:**

**WN-1 — The "from" rule (Sec 9).** Where a period is reckoned **"from"** a specified day or event, and the word "from" is used, the **first day is excluded** ("from" excludes; "to" includes for the closing day). So for a period computed *from* an event, exclude the day of the event.

**WN-2 — The "closed day" rule (Sec 10).** Where any act is allowed to be done **within a prescribed period**, and the **last day** falls on a day when the office/court is **closed**, the act is **deemed done in time if done on the next day the office is open**. Sec 10 saves only the *last* day, not intermediate days, and does **not** apply where the Limitation Act already provides (its own Sec 4).

**Application:**

| # | Decision | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| (a) | Last date = **31 March 2026** | "From the date of the order" → Sec 9 excludes 1 March; count 30 days from 2 March; the 30th day is 31 March. |
| (b) | **Yes, 10 April counts** | "Beginning with" (unlike "from") **includes** the first day; Sec 9's exclusion is displaced by express words. |
| (c) | **Yes — do it on 1 July 2026** | Sec 10: last day (30 June) is a closed holiday → act done on next working day is in time. |
| (d) | Sec 10 saves it **only if the *last* day is a closed day** | Sec 10 keys off the closure of the **last** day; if the last day is closed, the next open day suffices. (For suits/appeals under the Limitation Act, Sec 4 of that Act — not Sec 10 GCA — governs.) |
| (e) | **Yes, 1 April is included** | Sec 9 excludes the first day **only when "from" is used**; where a benefit runs "from…to…", the closing day (30 April) is included, and for an inclusive benefit window the opening day is treated as included per the drafting; the safe reading gives the party the full stated span. |

**Answer:** (a) 31 March 2026; (b) yes; (c) yes, 1 July 2026; (d) Sec 10 saves the last day if it is a closed day; (e) 1 April included.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Sec 9 exists to end the perennial ambiguity of whether "from a date" includes that date — the legislature made a firm choice: **"from" excludes the first day and "to" includes the last**, so drafters and citizens compute uniformly. Notice the discipline it imposes: the exclusion is triggered by the *word* "from"; use a different connector ("beginning with," "commencing on") and the first day is back in. Sec 10 rests on a fairness principle — the law should not penalise a person for failing to do the impossible: you cannot file in a court that is shut, so the closure of the *final* day rolls the deadline to the next open day. The classic trap is applying Sec 10 to an *intermediate* closed day (it doesn't) or to a Limitation-Act matter (there, Sec 4 of the Limitation Act, a special law, prevails over the general Sec 10).

*(Full-marks tip: state the "from excludes / to includes" rule verbatim and show the day-count for (a); flag that Sec 10 yields to the Limitation Act. Deduction for counting the order date itself in (a).)*

---

### Q78. Ch: General Clauses Act 1897 — Effect of Repeal (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** The (imaginary) "Old Trade Act, 1990" is repealed by the "New Trade Act, 2026" with effect from 1 April 2026. Consider these situations under **Sec 6** (and Sec 6A / Sec 24 where relevant) of the General Clauses Act, 1897 and advise:
(a) A trader committed an offence under the Old Act in January 2026; prosecution had not yet begun on 1 April 2026. Can he still be prosecuted after repeal?
(b) A licence was granted, and a penalty proceeding was **pending**, under the Old Act on 1 April 2026. What happens to the pending proceeding?
(c) The New Act is **silent** on repeal-savings. Does the trader escape liability?
(d) A **rule/notification** issued under the Old Act was in force. Is it automatically dead on repeal, or does it survive under the New Act?
(e) Suppose instead the Old Act is not repealed but merely **re-enacted with modifications**; a reference in another statute pointed to a section of the Old Act. What happens to that reference?

**Answer:**
**Governing provision — Sec 6 (effect of repeal), with Sec 6A and Sec 24.**

**Core principle (Sec 6):** Unless a **different intention appears**, the repeal of an enactment does **not**: (i) revive anything not in force; (ii) **affect the previous operation** of the repealed Act or anything duly done thereunder; (iii) **affect any right, privilege, obligation or liability acquired, accrued or incurred**; (iv) affect any **penalty, forfeiture or punishment incurred** in respect of an offence committed; or (v) affect any **investigation, legal proceeding or remedy** in respect of such right/liability/penalty — and any such proceeding or remedy may be **instituted, continued or enforced** as if the repealing Act had not been passed.

**(a)** The offence was **committed** in January 2026 — a **liability/penalty was incurred** before repeal. Under Sec 6(c)/(e), that liability survives repeal, and a **prosecution may still be instituted** after 1 April 2026 as if the Old Act were not repealed. **He can be prosecuted.**

**(b)** A **pending proceeding** in respect of an accrued liability is expressly saved: it **continues** under the Old Act as if the repeal had not happened. The licence already granted (a thing "duly done") also stands. **Proceeding continues.**

**(c)** Sec 6 applies **precisely because** the New Act is silent — it is a *default* saving that operates "unless a different intention appears." Silence means **no contrary intention**, so the savings **apply** and the trader does **not** escape. **He remains liable.** (Only an express provision wiping out past liability would change this.)

**(d) Continuation of subordinate legislation (Sec 24).** Under **Sec 24**, where an Act is repealed and re-enacted, any **rule, notification, order, etc.** made under the repealed Act **continues in force** and is deemed to have been made under the corresponding provision of the new Act **so far as it is not inconsistent** with the new Act, until superseded. So the rule/notification **survives** (if not inconsistent). It is **not** automatically dead.

**(e) Construction of references (Sec 8).** Where an Act is repealed and re-enacted, a **reference in any other enactment to the repealed provision** is construed as a reference to the **corresponding provision of the re-enacted law** (Sec 8). The cross-reference therefore **automatically re-points** to the new section; it does not become meaningless.

**Conclusion:** (a) prosecutable; (b) pending proceeding continues; (c) silence ≠ escape — Sec 6 saves liability; (d) subordinate legislation survives under Sec 24 if not inconsistent; (e) the reference re-points under Sec 8.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** At common law, repeal was drastic — it wiped the slate as if the Act never existed, defeating accrued rights and pending prosecutions. Sec 6 was enacted to **prevent that injustice**: it draws a line between the *future* operation of a law (which repeal ends) and the *past-crystallised* rights and liabilities under it (which repeal must not disturb). The engine of the section is the phrase "**unless a different intention appears**" — so the burden is on whoever claims escape to point to contrary words; **silence preserves, not destroys.** Sec 24 and Sec 8 extend the same continuity logic to *delegated legislation* and *cross-references*, so that a mere change of the parent statute does not leave a vacuum. The trap in (c) is thinking "the new Act said nothing about old offences, so they lapse" — exactly backwards; Sec 6 is designed for that silence.

*(Full-marks tip: quote the five limbs of Sec 6, hinge everything on "unless a different intention appears," and bring in Sec 24 (subordinate legislation) and Sec 8 (references). Deduction for saying silence lets the offender escape.)*

---

### Q79. Ch: General Clauses Act 1897 — "May" vs "Shall": Directory or Mandatory (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Examine the effect of the words "may" and "shall" in the following, and decide whether the requirement is mandatory or directory, applying the principles of statutory interpretation and the General Clauses Act, 1897:
(a) A section reads: "The authority **may**, if it thinks fit, grant a licence." A qualified applicant is refused without reasons and claims the authority "**must**" grant it because "may" here means "shall."
(b) A rule reads: "An application **shall** be accompanied by a fee of ₹500." An applicant files without the fee and argues "shall" is merely directory.
(c) A statute says a public officer "**shall** decide the appeal within 90 days." The officer decides on the 100th day; the appellant says the decision is void for breach of "shall."
(d) A provision: the Registrar "**may** call for information," and a company refuses, saying "may" gives it discretion to decline.

**Answer:**
**Principle:** Words are read in their ordinary sense, but "may"/"shall" take their colour from the **object, context and consequence** of the provision. "May" is generally **enabling/discretionary**, "shall" generally **imperative**; but courts read "may" as "shall" where a **public duty coupled with a right** exists, and read "shall" as directory where the object is served without invalidating for breach and no prejudice results.

**(a)** "May…if it thinks fit, grant" — the words expressly confer **discretion** ("if it thinks fit"). This is **directory/enabling**, not a duty to grant. The applicant cannot compel a grant; but the discretion must be exercised **reasonably and with reasons** (a licensing power is a public power). So the applicant's claim that "may = must grant" **fails**; his remedy is to challenge the refusal as arbitrary/unreasoned, not to demand the licence as of right.

**(b)** "Shall be accompanied by the fee" — the fee is a **condition precedent** to a valid application; without it the application is **defective/incomplete**. Here "shall" is **mandatory**: the object (a valid, funded application) is defeated if the fee can be skipped. Applicant's "directory" argument **fails**.

**(c)** "Shall decide within 90 days" for a **public officer performing a public duty** where **no consequence of nullity is prescribed** and third-party rights would be prejudiced by voiding the decision — such a time-limit is generally **directory**. Breach exposes the officer to accountability but does **not** render the belated decision **void**. So the 100th-day decision is **valid**; "shall" here is directory as to the time-frame.

**(d)** "May call for information" — the power to call for information is coupled with the **public purpose** of regulation; when a public authority is given a power to be used for the benefit of the public/for enforcing a right, "may" is read as a **duty to exercise it in appropriate cases**, and the *recipient* has **no discretion to refuse** a lawful requisition. The company's refusal is **untenable**; "may" empowers the Registrar, it does not license the company to disobey.

**Conclusion:** (a) discretionary — no right to compel grant; (b) mandatory — fee is a precondition; (c) directory — belated decision valid; (d) company must comply — "may" empowers the Registrar, not a right to refuse.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The mandatory/directory divide is decided by **purpose and consequence**, not by the dictionary. Ask: *does the object of the provision require strict compliance, and does the statute attach a nullity to breach?* Where a power is given to a public body **for the benefit of a person who has a right** (licensing done properly, information called for regulation), the "enabling" word carries an **implied duty** — this prevents an authority from defeating a right by inaction. Conversely, time-limits on officials are usually directory because voiding their decisions would punish the *innocent citizen* for the *official's* delay — an absurd result the legislature cannot have intended. The trap is mechanical literalism: reading every "shall" as fatal and every "may" as a free pass. The (c) result especially surprises students — but the test is *consequence and prejudice*, and no nullity is prescribed.

*(Full-marks tip: for each item state the object → attach/decline a nullity → conclude. Cite the "power coupled with duty" idea for (a)/(d) and "no penalty of nullity → directory" for (c). Deduction for a flat rule "shall = always mandatory.")*

---

### Q80. Ch: General Clauses Act 1897 — Service of Documents (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Apply **Sec 27 of the General Clauses Act, 1897** ("meaning of service by post") and decide, with reasons, in each case whether valid service is made out:

| # | Facts |
|---|---|
| (a) | A notice is **properly addressed, pre-paid and sent by registered post** to the addressee's correct address. It is returned "unclaimed." The addressee says he never received it. Is service complete? |
| (b) | The sender posts by **ordinary (un-registered) post** and produces no proof of posting. The addressee denies receipt. |
| (c) | The letter is registered and correctly addressed, but the sender **cannot prove it was pre-paid**. |
| (d) | The addressee proves the letter was **actually delivered on 12 May 2026**, though in the ordinary course it should have arrived on 8 May. From which date is service effective? |

**Answer:**
**Provision — Sec 27, General Clauses Act, 1897.** Where an Act authorises/requires a document to be served **by post**, then, unless a different intention appears, service is **deemed to be effected** by **(i) properly addressing, (ii) pre-paying and (iii) posting by registered post** a letter containing the document; and, unless the contrary is proved, service is deemed to have been effected **at the time at which the letter would be delivered in the ordinary course of post.**

**(a)** All three conditions — proper address, pre-payment, **registered** post — are satisfied. Sec 27 raises a **presumption of due service**; "unclaimed" return does **not** rebut it, because a person cannot defeat service by refusing/failing to collect. The **deemed delivery in the ordinary course** stands. **Service is complete.** (Mere denial of receipt is not the "contrary proof" that rebuts the presumption.)

**(b)** Sec 27's presumption applies only to **registered** post. **Ordinary post** with **no proof of posting** gets **no statutory presumption**; the sender must prove actual service. On these facts (denial + no proof) **service is not established.**

**(c)** The presumption requires **pre-payment**. If pre-payment cannot be shown, a **condition of Sec 27 is missing** and the deeming does not operate; the sender must otherwise prove service. **Service not made out under Sec 27.**

**(d)** The presumption of "ordinary-course" delivery is **rebuttable** — "unless the contrary is proved." Here the **actual date of delivery (12 May 2026) is proved**, which displaces the presumed date (8 May). **Service is effective on 12 May 2026** (the proved actual date prevails over the deemed date).

**Conclusion:** (a) served (presumption not rebutted by "unclaimed"); (b) not served (no presumption for ordinary post without proof); (c) not served under Sec 27 (pre-payment unproved); (d) served on the **proved actual date, 12 May 2026**.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Sec 27 solves a practical evidentiary problem: a sender cannot peer inside the postal system, so the law gives him a **presumption** once he has done the three things within his control — address correctly, pre-pay, and use **registered** post (registration provides the audit trail). The policy is that a recipient **should not defeat service by his own inaction** — refusing or failing to claim a properly sent registered letter is treated as constructive service, otherwise every notice could be dodged. But the presumption is a *default, not a fiction of last resort*: it yields the moment the **contrary is proved** — so if the actual delivery date is established, that real fact governs (item d). The registered-vs-ordinary distinction (items a vs b) is the crux students miss: only **registered** post triggers the deeming; ordinary post throws the burden of proving actual receipt back on the sender.

*(Full-marks tip: list the three Sec 27 conditions (address, pre-pay, registered) and the rebuttable "ordinary course" deeming. The high-value points are "unclaimed ≠ non-service" and "registered only." Deduction for granting the presumption to ordinary post.)*

---

### Q81. Ch: General Clauses Act 1897 — Definitions & "Person"/Gender/Number (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Applying the interpretation rules in the General Clauses Act, 1897, decide each:
(a) A penal section says "**whoever** contravenes… shall be punished." A **company** contravenes. Can a company be a "person" so as to be prosecuted, given words importing the masculine gender and the singular?
(b) A statute uses "**he/his**" throughout in describing an office-holder. Does this exclude a woman from holding the office?
(c) A rule refers to "a **document**." Does it include information stored in **electronic form**?
(d) A section speaks of "an **immovable property**." Does it include **benefits arising out of land** and **standing timber**?

**Answer:**
**Governing provision — Sec 3 (definitions) read with Sec 13, General Clauses Act, 1897.**

**(a) "Person" (Sec 3(42)).** "Person" **includes any company or association or body of individuals, whether incorporated or not.** Therefore a **company is a "person"** and "whoever" catches it. Under **Sec 13**, words importing the **masculine gender include females**, and words in the **singular include the plural** and vice-versa — so the singular/masculine drafting is no bar. **The company can be prosecuted** (subject to the punishment being one a company can suffer — e.g., fine, since imprisonment cannot be inflicted on a corporate; courts read the punishment down to fine where imprisonment is impossible).

**(b) Gender (Sec 13(1)).** Words importing the **masculine gender are taken to include females**, unless a contrary intention appears. Hence "he/his" does **not exclude a woman**; a woman may hold the office. **No exclusion.**

**(c) "Document" (Sec 3(18)).** "Document" means any matter expressed/described upon any substance by letters, figures or marks. Read with the modern statutory framework (and the inclusive intent of the definition), a document embraces recorded matter; the **electronic record** is included where the context/allied law so provides. So **electronic form is covered.**

**(d) "Immovable property" (Sec 3(26)).** "Immovable property" **includes land, benefits to arise out of land, and things attached to the earth or permanently fastened to anything attached to the earth**, but **excludes standing timber, growing crops and grass**. Therefore **benefits arising out of land are included**, but **standing timber is NOT** immovable property. **Partly yes, partly no.**

**Conclusion:** (a) company is a "person," prosecutable (fine); (b) "he" includes "she"; (c) document includes electronic form; (d) benefits out of land — yes; standing timber — no.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The General Clauses Act is the **"dictionary of all statutes"** — its definitions apply to every Central Act unless that Act says otherwise, saving the legislature from re-defining common words each time and producing consistency across the statute book. Sec 3(42) deliberately makes "person" wide so that artificial entities cannot slip through penal and regulatory nets merely because they are not natural persons — a core anti-avoidance idea. Sec 13's gender/number rule prevents drafting shorthand ("he," singular) from being weaponised to exclude women or to escape liability by pointing to the singular. The subtle trap is (d): "standing timber" *looks* attached to land but is **statutorily excluded** because it is grown to be cut — its value is as movable timber, so the law classifies it as movable.

*(Full-marks tip: quote Sec 3(42) and Sec 13 verbatim and remember the standing-timber/growing-crops **exclusion** from immovable property. Deduction for saying a company cannot be a "person" or that "he" excludes women.)*

---

### Q82. Ch: LLP Act 2008 — Foreign LLP, Conversion of Private Company & Effect (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** "Zenith Pvt Ltd" (a private company with 6 shareholders and secured loans from a bank) proposes to convert into "Zenith LLP" under the **Third Schedule** of the LLP Act, 2008. Examine:
