(a) The eligibility conditions the company must satisfy before it can convert.
(b) Whether the pending **secured loan** and the bank's **charge** are an obstacle, and what consent is needed.
(c) The effect of conversion on the company's **assets, liabilities, and its very existence**.
(d) A creditor of the erstwhile company (debt of pre-conversion date) sues after conversion — is the limited-liability shield available for that old debt?
(e) One shareholder wants to remain **only a shareholder** and not become a partner. Is that permissible?

**Answer:**
**Legal basis — Sec 56 read with the Third Schedule.**

**(a) Eligibility to convert a private company (Para 1, Third Schedule).** A private company may convert into an LLP if: (i) there is **no security interest subsisting** in its assets at the time of application, **or** the secured creditor **consents**; and (ii) **all shareholders of the company, and no one else, become partners of the LLP** (with their capital contributions). All must be individuals or bodies eligible to be partners.

**(b) Secured loan / charge.** A subsisting charge is **not an absolute bar**, but conversion is permitted only if the **secured creditor (bank) gives its consent / the security interest is cleared.** So Zenith must obtain the **bank's consent** (or discharge the charge) before conversion; otherwise the application fails the eligibility condition.

**(c) Effect of conversion (Para 6–7).** On registration: **all tangible and intangible property, assets, interests, rights, privileges, liabilities and obligations** of the company **vest in the LLP by operation of law**, and the **company is deemed dissolved** and removed from the Registrar of Companies' records. Contracts, pending proceedings, employment continue with/against the LLP.

**(d) Liability for pre-conversion debt.** As with firm conversion, **liabilities and obligations of the company that arose before conversion continue**, and the LLP is bound by them. The limited-liability shield of the LLP protects partners for the LLP's *own* future obligations, but the **pre-existing liabilities pass to and are enforceable against the LLP**; where the statute/agreement or Third Schedule imposes continuing responsibility, the creditor's pre-conversion claim is fully preserved (the shield cannot be used to evade obligations that had already accrued against the company). The old creditor's claim **survives against the LLP.**

**(e) Shareholder who won't become a partner.** The Third Schedule requires that **all shareholders — and no one else — become partners of the LLP.** If even one shareholder declines to become a partner, the **"all shareholders become partners" condition fails**, and the company **cannot convert** while he remains a non-participating shareholder. His shares/position must be resolved first. **Not permissible as desired.**

**Conclusion:** (a) no subsisting security without consent + all shareholders become partners; (b) bank's consent essential; (c) universal vesting in the LLP + company dissolved; (d) pre-conversion creditor's claim survives against the LLP; (e) a shareholder cannot opt out — all must become partners or conversion fails.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Conversion is again a **universal succession** — the LLP inherits the company whole, so there is business continuity and no need to re-assign each asset or novate each contract. But the Third Schedule guards two constituencies whose consent the change of form would otherwise bypass: **secured creditors** (whose bargained security must not be silently diluted by a change of debtor-form — hence their consent) and **existing owners** (hence the "all shareholders, and no one else, become partners" rule, which prevents conversion from being a device to squeeze out or import members). The limited-liability point in (d) tests the same principle as firm-conversion: limited liability is *prospective*; it cannot rewrite obligations that had already crystallised against the company. The (e) trap is assuming an LLP can have passive "shareholders" — it cannot; an LLP has *partners*, not shareholders, so a member who won't be a partner has no place in it.

*(Full-marks tip: cite Third Schedule Para 1 (no subsisting security/consent + all shareholders become partners) and the vesting/dissolution effect. The consent-of-secured-creditor and "all shareholders become partners" conditions are the scoring points. Deduction for allowing conversion despite an unconsented charge or a hold-out shareholder.)*

---

### Q83. Ch: General Clauses Act 1897 — Commencement, Coming into Operation & Powers (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Apply the General Clauses Act, 1897 to advise on the following, each with the exact section:
(a) An Act is passed and receives assent on **10 March 2026** but is silent about when it comes into force. From what date does it operate? (Sec 5)
(b) A rule-making section empowers the Government to "make rules." Does this power also include the power to **add to, amend, vary or rescind** those rules? (Sec 21)
(c) A statute empowers an authority "to appoint" an officer. Does that power carry with it the power to **suspend or dismiss** the officer? (Sec 16)
(d) A notification is required to be **published in the Official Gazette**; the Government issues it but publishes late. When does it take effect, and what is the significance of Gazette publication? (Sec 5 / general principle)
(e) A power is conferred to be exercised "**from time to time**" — what does this add?

**Answer:**
**(a) Commencement (Sec 5).** Where a Central Act is silent as to commencement, it comes into force on the **day it receives the assent** — of the President (for a Central Act). Hence the Act operates from **10 March 2026** (the date of assent), not the date of passing or publication. (If the Act itself fixes a date or empowers the Government to notify a date, that governs instead.)

**(b) Power to make includes power to amend/rescind (Sec 21).** Where a power to **issue notifications, orders, rules or bye-laws** is conferred, that power **includes** a power, exercisable in the like manner and subject to the like conditions, to **add to, amend, vary or rescind** them. So the Government **can amend or rescind** the rules it made. **Yes.**

**(c) Power to appoint includes power to suspend/dismiss (Sec 16).** Where a power to **make an appointment** is conferred, the authority having that power also has, **unless a different intention appears**, the power to **suspend or dismiss** any person appointed in exercise of that power. So the appointing authority **can suspend/dismiss** the officer. **Yes.**

**(d) Publication in the Official Gazette.** Delegated legislation/notifications that the parent Act requires to be **published in the Official Gazette** generally take legal effect **on the date of such publication** (unless the notification itself specifies a later date). Gazette publication is the **mode of authentic promulgation** — it is how the State gives the citizen legally-recognised notice; until published as required, the notification does not acquire operative force. So a late-published notification takes effect **from the (late) date of Gazette publication**, not the date it was signed.

**(e) "From time to time" (Sec 14 / general).** A power conferred to be exercised "from time to time" (and, under **Sec 14**, powers conferred by a Central Act **may be exercised from time to time as occasion requires**) signifies that the power is **not exhausted by a single exercise** — it may be used **repeatedly, as often as the occasion arises**. It prevents the argument that once exercised, the power is spent.

**Conclusion:** (a) in force from 10 March 2026 (date of assent); (b) yes — Sec 21 allows amendment/rescission; (c) yes — Sec 16 carries the power to suspend/dismiss; (d) effective on Gazette publication date; (e) the power is exercisable repeatedly, not spent by one use.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** These sections all embody the principle that a **grant of power carries with it the incidents necessary to make it effective** — the legislature should not have to spell out every consequential power. Sec 21 (amend/rescind) reflects that a law-maker who can create rules must be able to keep them current, else delegated legislation would ossify; the safeguard is "in the like manner and subject to like conditions," so amendment cannot bypass the procedure used to make the rule. Sec 16 mirrors administrative reality — the authority that hires must be able to discipline, otherwise appointments would be irrevocable. Sec 5 provides a **default commencement** so no Act is left dateless. Gazette publication is the constitutional courtesy of *notice* — secret law cannot bind. The recurring trap is assuming a power, once used, is exhausted; Sec 14's "from time to time" squarely negatives that.

*(Full-marks tip: pin each answer to the numbered section — Sec 5, 21, 16, 14 — and state the "like manner/like conditions" limitation on Sec 21 and the "unless different intention appears" caveat on Sec 16. Deduction for dating commencement from the day of passing rather than assent.)*

---

### Q84. Ch: LLP Act 2008 + General Clauses Act 1897 — Integrated: Filing Timelines, Service & Repeal (Marks: 10) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Integrate the LLP Act, 2008 with the General Clauses Act, 1897 and resolve the following linked issues for "Orion LLP":

| # | Facts |
|---|---|
| (a) | A change in partners occurs on **1 April 2026**. Sec 25 of the LLP Act requires the LLP to file notice of the change "**within 30 days**." The 30th day falls on a **Sunday when the Registrar's office is closed**. When is the filing still in time? |
| (b) | The Registrar issues a show-cause notice to the LLP by **registered post, properly addressed and pre-paid**, to Orion's registered office. It is returned "**refused**." The LLP says it was never served. |
| (c) | Sec 25 uses "**shall file within 30 days**." Orion argues "shall" is directory, so late filing has no consequence. |
| (d) | Suppose the LLP (Amendment) provisions had **repealed** an earlier penalty clause after Orion's default but **before** adjudication. Can Orion escape the penalty that had already been incurred? |
| (e) | A partner claims the "**30 days**" should be counted **including** 1 April 2026 (the day of the change). Is he right? |

**Answer:**
This problem stitches LLP timelines to the General Clauses Act's computation-of-time, service, interpretation and repeal rules.

**(a) Closed-day extension — Sec 10, GCA + Sec 9.** The 30-day window under Sec 25 of the LLP Act is a "prescribed period" for doing an act. Under **Sec 9**, the period runs **from** 1 April, so **1 April is excluded** and counting begins 2 April. If the **30th day is a Sunday/closed day**, **Sec 10, GCA** deems the act done in time **if done on the next day the Registrar's office is open** — i.e., the following Monday. **Filing on the next open day is valid.**

**(b) Service by post — Sec 27, GCA.** The notice was **properly addressed, pre-paid and sent by registered post** to the **registered office** — Sec 27's three conditions are met, raising a **presumption of due service** at the time it would be delivered in the ordinary course. A **"refused" endorsement does NOT defeat service** — a party cannot escape by refusing a properly-sent registered letter; refusal is treated as **constructive/deemed service**. Orion's bare denial is not "contrary proof." **Service is complete.**

**(c) "Shall" — mandatory or directory.** Here "shall file within 30 days" is backed by a **specific penal consequence** in the LLP Act for default (additional fee/penalty on the LLP and designated partners under Sec 8). Where the statute **attaches a penalty to non-compliance**, the filing duty is treated as **mandatory** in substance; even if the *time-limit* is read as directory (so a late filing is still accepted), the **default attracts penalty**. Orion cannot escape the consequence by labelling "shall" directory. **Argument fails.**

**(d) Repeal of penalty clause — Sec 6, GCA.** Under **Sec 6**, unless a different intention appears, repeal does **not affect any penalty or liability already incurred** in respect of a default, nor any legal proceeding/remedy, which may be **instituted, continued or enforced** as if the clause were not repealed. Orion's default and consequent **penalty were already incurred before repeal** — so the penalty **survives** and can still be adjudicated. **Orion cannot escape.**

**(e) Computation "from" the event — Sec 9, GCA.** "Within 30 days" of the change is reckoned **from** 1 April 2026; **Sec 9 excludes the first day**, so 1 April is **not** counted — counting starts 2 April. The partner is **wrong** to include 1 April.

**Conclusion:** (a) valid if filed on the next open day (Sec 10); (b) served — "refused" = deemed service (Sec 27); (c) late filing attracts penalty — cannot be dodged as "directory"; (d) penalty already incurred survives repeal (Sec 6); (e) 1 April excluded (Sec 9).

**Why this way (the reasoning):** This question shows why the **General Clauses Act is the operating system running underneath every special Act** — the LLP Act sets the *substantive* duty ("file within 30 days"), but *how* you count the days (Sec 9), *what happens if the last day is a holiday* (Sec 10), *when postal service counts* (Sec 27), and *what a later repeal does to an accrued penalty* (Sec 6) all come from the GCA. The unifying logic across the parts is **fairness without loopholes**: Sec 10 protects the citizen who genuinely cannot file on a closed day, while Sec 27 denies the citizen who *chooses* not to receive notice; Sec 6 protects the public interest by not letting a repeal wipe crystallised liabilities. The two big traps are (b) — thinking "refused" means "not served" (it is the opposite) — and (e)/(a) — mis-counting by including the trigger day or forgetting the holiday extension.

*(Full-marks tip: for a 10-marker, expressly name the GCA section that solves each LLP-timeline sub-issue — Sec 9, 10, 27, 6 — and state the LLP Act duty (Sec 25/Sec 8) it plugs into. The examiner specifically rewards "refused = deemed service" and "penalty incurred survives repeal." Deduction for including the trigger date or treating late filing as consequence-free.)*

### Q85. Ch: Interpretation of Statutes — Literal vs Golden Rule (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** A penal provision in a State Municipal Act reads: *"Whoever, having been previously convicted of an offence under this section, again commits the same offence, shall be punished with imprisonment which may extend to two years."* Mr. X was tried and convicted for the substantive offence and, in the *same* trial (a single judgment), was found to have committed the offence on two distinct dates. The Magistrate treated the earlier of the two dates as a "previous conviction" and imposed the enhanced two-year sentence. X contends the enhanced punishment cannot apply because on the date he committed the second act he had not yet "been previously convicted" — there was no prior *judgment* against him. Applying the rules of interpretation, examine whether the enhanced sentence is valid.

**Answer:**
**Governing principles.** The *Literal (Grammatical) Rule* requires that where the words of a statute are plain and unambiguous, effect must be given to them in their ordinary, natural and grammatical meaning, irrespective of consequences. The *Golden Rule* is a modification: the literal meaning is followed **unless** it leads to an absurdity, repugnance or inconsistency, in which case the court may depart to the extent necessary to avoid that result. A cardinal supplementary rule is that **penal statutes are construed strictly in favour of the accused** — where two reasonable constructions are possible, the one lenient to the subject is preferred.

**Application.** The phrase "having been previously convicted... again commits the same offence" is *sequential* on its plain terms: it presupposes (i) a *conviction*, i.e. a completed judicial finding of guilt, followed in time by (ii) a fresh commission of the offence. A "conviction" in law means the recording of guilt by a competent court, not merely the commission of an act. On the facts, both acts were committed *before* any judgment; the first act had not ripened into a "conviction" when the second act was done. Reading "previously convicted" to include a mere earlier *act* tried in the same proceeding does violence to the ordinary meaning of "convicted".

Here the Literal Rule and the Golden Rule converge. The literal meaning is clear and produces no absurdity — it simply means enhanced punishment is a device against *recidivism* (re-offending after being caught and convicted), which requires a prior conviction to have existed at the time of the second offence. Since none existed, the trap the Magistrate fell into (equating "act" with "conviction") fails.

**Conclusion.** The enhanced two-year sentence is **invalid**. X can be punished only for the substantive offence at the ordinary (unenhanced) scale. The provision bites only on a person who re-offends *after* an existing conviction.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The concept being tested is that a repeat-offender/enhancement clause is anti-recidivism law — its whole purpose is to punish the person who, having been formally warned by a conviction, offends *again*. That purpose is defeated if you count acts done before any conviction ever existed, because the deterrent (the conviction) was not in place to be defied. The Literal Rule protects the accused here because "convicted" is a legal term of art meaning an adjudicated finding of guilt, not the raw act. The tempting wrong approach — treating the earlier date as a "previous conviction" — collapses two distinct legal ideas (commission and conviction) and, in a *penal* statute, chooses the harsher of two readings, which the strict-construction rule forbids.

*(Full-marks tip: examiners reward (i) naming Literal + Golden + strict-construction-of-penal-statutes together, and (ii) the precise point that "conviction" ≠ "commission". Marks are lost for merely defining the rules without applying the sequence-in-time logic to X's facts.)*

---

### Q86. Ch: Interpretation of Statutes — Mischief Rule (Heydon's Case) (Marks: 10) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Before amendment, a Debt Recovery statute allowed a borrower to seek an injunction restraining a bank from selling secured property. Banks complained that borrowers routinely obtained injunctions to stall recovery indefinitely. Parliament then inserted a section: *"No injunction shall be granted by any court in respect of any action taken or to be taken in pursuance of any power conferred under this Act."* A borrower now argues that the bar applies only to "actions" already crystallised, and that a court may still injunct at the *pre-action* stage (before the bank issues its demand notice). The bank argues the bar is total. Applying the Mischief Rule and any subsidiary aids, resolve the dispute.

**Answer:**
**Governing principle — the Mischief Rule (Heydon's Case, 1584).** To suppress the mischief and advance the remedy, the court asks four questions: (1) What was the common law / the law *before* the Act? (2) What was the *mischief and defect* for which the earlier law did not provide? (3) What *remedy* has Parliament resolved to cure the disease? (4) The *true reason* of the remedy — the court must then construe the words so as to *suppress the mischief and advance the remedy*, and suppress subtle inventions and evasions.

**Application of the four steps.**
1. *Prior law:* borrowers could obtain injunctions restraining sale of secured assets.
2. *Mischief:* this power was abused to stall lawful recovery indefinitely, defeating the very object of a speedy-recovery statute and locking up bank funds.
3. *Remedy:* an absolute statutory bar — "No injunction shall be granted... in respect of any action taken **or to be taken**."
4. *True reason:* to close every avenue by which the recovery process could be judicially frozen.

The borrower's construction — that the bar reaches only crystallised actions and leaves the pre-action stage open — is precisely the "subtle invention and evasion" the rule commands courts to suppress. The words "**or to be taken**" are decisive: Parliament expressly extended the bar to *future/contemplated* action, i.e. the pre-action stage. To allow a pre-action injunction would reopen the very hole the amendment was enacted to plug, so a court "advancing the remedy" must reject it.

Subsidiary aids reinforce this: the *non-obstante* breadth ("No injunction... by any court"), and the presumption that Parliament does not enact futile amendments — a construction that leaves the mischief alive is presumed unintended.

**Conclusion.** The statutory bar is **total**; no injunction lies at either the pre-action or post-action stage. The borrower's remedy, if any, is confined to the appellate/statutory mechanism the Act itself provides, not a civil injunction.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The Mischief Rule is purposive — it treats a statute as a cure for a specific disease and reads doubtful words as medicine, not poison. The student must see *why* the literal reading and the purposive reading here point the same way: the phrase "or to be taken" is the textual hook, and the mischief (stalling recovery) is the purpose that fills it out. The wrong approach — a hyper-literal slicing of "action taken" to exclude future action — is exactly the "evasion" Heydon warns against, and it would render the amendment pointless, violating the presumption against surplusage. Purposive construction wins because a remedial provision must be read to *advance*, not frustrate, its object.

*(Full-marks tip: reproduce all four Heydon questions and *map each to the facts*; then anchor the conclusion on the words "or to be taken". Answers that state Heydon abstractly but never identify the specific mischief or the operative phrase lose half the marks.)*

---

### Q87. Ch: Interpretation of Statutes — Harmonious Construction (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** A single enactment contains two sections. Section 12 says: *"Every appeal shall be filed within 30 days of the order."* Section 47 says: *"Notwithstanding anything contained in this Act, the Appellate Authority may, for reasons to be recorded, entertain an appeal after the expiry of the period of limitation if it is satisfied that there was sufficient cause."* A litigant filed an appeal on the 45th day; the Registry rejected it as barred by Section 12. The litigant relies on Section 47. The Department argues the two sections are in direct conflict and that the specific 30-day limit in Section 12 must prevail. Apply the rule of Harmonious Construction and advise.

**Answer:**
**Governing principle.** The rule of *Harmonious Construction* holds that a statute must be read as a whole and, when two provisions of the same statute appear to conflict, the court must so interpret them that both are given effect and neither is rendered otiose (redundant). A construction that reduces one provision to a dead letter is to be avoided; the court seeks the "broad general purpose" the legislature intended both to serve. Where reconciliation is truly impossible, a *non-obstante* ("notwithstanding") clause, and the maxim that the later/special provision controls, may tilt the balance.

**Application.** Sections 12 and 47 are **not** in genuine conflict; they operate in *different fields*. Section 12 fixes the *normal* period of limitation (30 days) — it is the general rule for the diligent appellant. Section 47 is an *enabling condonation* provision that opens only *after* limitation has expired and only on proof of "sufficient cause" with recorded reasons. Read harmoniously: Section 12 governs the ordinary case; Section 47 is the escape valve for the deserving late appellant. Each retains full work — Section 12 is not defeated (it still fixes the default and puts the burden on the late-comer to show cause), and Section 47 is not surplusage.

The Department's reading (Section 12 always prevails) would render Section 47 wholly redundant — a result the rule forbids. Moreover, Section 47 opens with a *non-obstante* clause ("Notwithstanding anything in this Act"), which is Parliament's express instruction that, on the limited question of condonation, Section 47 overrides Section 12's rigid bar.

**Conclusion.** The Registry's rejection is **wrong in law**. The appeal filed on the 45th day is not automatically barred; the Appellate Authority must examine whether "sufficient cause" existed and, if so, may condone the 15-day delay by a reasoned order. Advise the litigant to file a condonation application under Section 47.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** Harmonious construction rests on the presumption that a legislature does not contradict itself within one Act and does not enact useless words. The insight the student must grasp is that a *general limitation* and a *condonation power* are complementary, not competing — one sets the norm, the other relieves against it in hardship. The trap is to see "30 days" and "after the expiry of limitation" as a head-on clash and pick a winner; that destroys one section. The *non-obstante* clause is the clincher because it is the drafter's own signal of which provision yields on the point of overlap. Giving effect to both preserves both the discipline of limitation and the equity of condonation.

*(Full-marks tip: the marks are in the phrase "operate in different fields" plus the "render Section 47 otiose" argument and the recognition of the non-obstante clause. Merely defining harmonious construction without showing how both survive earns little.)*

---

### Q88. Ch: Interpretation of Statutes — Noscitur a Sociis & Ejusdem Generis (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** A taxing entry levies duty on *"tobacco, cigars, cigarettes, biris and other similar products."* A manufacturer of chewing tobacco (zarda) claims his product is not covered because it is not smoked. Separately, a licensing rule requires a permit for *"any theatre, cinema hall, circus, or other place of public amusement."* The authority seeks to require a permit for a private members-only yoga studio. Using *noscitur a sociis* and *ejusdem generis*, examine both claims.

**Answer:**
**Governing principles.**
- *Noscitur a sociis* ("it is known by its associates"): the meaning of a doubtful word is gathered from the words with which it is coupled; a word takes colour from its neighbours.
- *Ejusdem generis* ("of the same kind"): where general words follow an enumeration of specific things forming a distinct *genus* (class), the general words are confined to things of the *same kind* as those enumerated. It applies only if the specific words form a recognisable category and the general words are not shown to have a wider intent.

**Application — Claim 1 (chewing tobacco).** The specific words "cigars, cigarettes, biris" all denote *smoking* forms of tobacco. But the entry *begins* with the standalone word "**tobacco**", which is the genus itself, not merely a smoking form. Because "tobacco" is expressly named as a class term, ejusdem generis cannot be used to *cut down* the entry to only smoking products — the genus is tobacco in all its forms. Applying noscitur cautiously, "other similar products" is coloured by tobacco products generally. Chewing tobacco (zarda) is a tobacco product and is therefore **covered**. The manufacturer's claim fails.

**Application — Claim 2 (yoga studio).** "Theatre, cinema hall, circus" form a clear genus: *places of public entertainment/amusement to which the general public resorts*. The general words "or other place of public amusement" are confined by ejusdem generis to that same kind. A **private, members-only yoga studio** is (i) not open to the public and (ii) directed at physical discipline/health, not amusement — it falls outside the genus. The permit requirement therefore does **not** apply.

**Conclusion.** Chewing tobacco is taxable; the yoga studio needs no permit.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The lesson is that these maxims are servants, not masters — they help find intention, but yield to an express class-word. In Claim 1 the presence of the umbrella word "tobacco" defeats the ejusdem-generis argument, because you cannot shrink a genus that the statute itself names; the tempting error is to let the smoking examples narrow it. In Claim 2 the maxim genuinely applies because the listed items *do* form a coherent class (public amusement) and the general phrase is meant to catch more of the *same* class — not to leap to any activity in a building. Recognising *when the maxim applies and when it is displaced* is the whole point.

*(Full-marks tip: the discriminator is spotting that "tobacco" is itself a genus-word (so ejusdem generis fails there) while "public amusement" has a genuine genus (so it applies). Candidates who apply the maxim mechanically to both claims lose marks.)*

---

### Q89. Ch: Interpretation of Statutes — Internal Aids: Proviso, Explanation, Non-obstante (Marks: 8) [Theory]

**Question:** "The function of a *proviso*, an *Explanation*, an *exception*, and a *non-obstante clause* are fundamentally different, and confusing them leads to wrong construction." Analyse each internal aid, explain the distinct role it plays, and illustrate with a short example how misreading one for another distorts a provision.

**Answer:**
**1. Proviso.** A proviso *carves out an exception* from the main enactment; its normal function is to *qualify or limit* the generality of the preceding words, not to enlarge them. The settled rule: a proviso must be read with the main clause and is presumed to deal only with the subject-matter of that clause — it cannot travel beyond it. *Example:* "Directors may be appointed for 5 years; **provided that** no director shall hold office beyond age 70." The proviso does not create a new power; it clips the main power.

**2. Explanation.** An Explanation is appended to *clarify* the meaning of the main provision, to remove doubt, or to include/exclude something by way of clarification. It does not ordinarily *widen or narrow* the section's scope beyond making explicit what is implicit — though a "deeming" Explanation may extend meaning. Its role is *interpretive*, harmonising with the main clause, not overriding it. *Example:* "Turnover means value of supplies. **Explanation** — turnover excludes taxes." This clarifies, it does not override.

**3. Exception.** An exception *exempts* something that would otherwise fall within the main provision — it is broader/blunter than a proviso and removes a class from the operation of the section altogether.

**4. Non-obstante clause ("Notwithstanding anything contained in...").** This is an *overriding* provision. It declares that, in case of conflict, the clause it introduces shall *prevail* over the provisions mentioned. Its function is the *opposite* of a proviso: a proviso *subtracts from* its own section, whereas a non-obstante clause *overrides other* provisions. Its overriding effect is, however, limited to the extent of actual repugnancy.

**Illustration of misreading.** Suppose a section: "Notwithstanding anything in Section 5, tax shall be charged at 10%; provided that goods exported shall bear nil rate." If one mistakes the *proviso* (nil rate on exports — a carve-out from *this* section) for a *non-obstante* effect (overriding *other* sections), one might wrongly conclude exports are exempt from the *whole Act*. Conversely, treating the *non-obstante* clause as a mere proviso would wrongly let Section 5 override the 10% charge. The proviso limits *inwards*; the non-obstante clause overrides *outwards*.

**Conclusion.** Each aid answers a different question: the proviso and exception *restrict* the section itself; the Explanation *clarifies* it; the non-obstante clause *elevates* it over rival provisions. Correct construction depends on assigning each its proper, distinct role.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The core idea is *direction of effect*. A proviso and an exception point *inward* (they subtract from their own host section); an Explanation points *sideways* (it interprets the same section); a non-obstante clause points *outward* (it defeats other sections). Students routinely conflate proviso and non-obstante because both signal "special treatment", but they pull in opposite directions — one narrows, one overrides. Anchoring each to its direction of effect prevents the classic error of using a mere clarificatory Explanation to rewrite the substantive charge, or letting a local proviso exempt goods from the entire statute.

*(Full-marks tip: examiners want the *contrast* — proviso subtracts inward vs non-obstante overrides outward — plus one worked illustration. Listing definitions without the "direction of effect" contrast caps the marks.)*

---

### Q90. Ch: Interpretation of Statutes — External Aids & "Statutes in Pari Materia" (Marks: 6) [Theory]

**Question:** In construing an ambiguous phrase in a taxing statute, a court is invited to look at (a) the Statement of Objects and Reasons, (b) parliamentary debates, (c) a dictionary, (d) an earlier repealed Act dealing with the same subject, and (e) a foreign decision on similarly-worded legislation. State the extent to which each *external aid* may legitimately be used, and explain the doctrine of statutes *in pari materia*.

**Answer:**
**Principle — external aids.** External aids are materials *outside* the four corners of the statute that a court may consult to resolve ambiguity. They may be used only when the language is *not plain*; they cannot override clear words.

- **(a) Statement of Objects and Reasons (SOR):** Admissible to ascertain the *background, the mischief and the object* the legislature sought to remedy, and the state of affairs that led to the Bill. It is **not** conclusive of the meaning of the enacted words — the section may go wider or narrower than the SOR — but it is a legitimate aid to context.
- **(b) Parliamentary debates:** Historically excluded, but now admissible in a *limited* way — the speech of the mover/Minister may be referred to for the *purpose and mischief*, though individual members' opinions are of little weight and cannot control the meaning of clear words.
- **(c) Dictionary:** May be consulted to find the ordinary meaning of a word, but *cautiously* — the meaning must fit the statutory context; the sense appropriate to the subject-matter is preferred over a general dictionary sense.
- **(d) Earlier repealed Act on the same subject (in pari materia):** Legitimately usable — see below.
- **(e) Foreign decision on similarly-worded law:** Of *persuasive* value only; useful where the statutory scheme and language are comparable, but not binding and subordinate to Indian context and precedent.

**Doctrine of *in pari materia*.** Statutes "upon the same matter or subject" are to be read *together* as though they formed one system, because the legislature is presumed to have been consistent and to have intended the later law to be construed in harmony with the settled meaning of the earlier. Thus an earlier Act (even if repealed), its judicial interpretation, and its scheme may illuminate an ambiguous term in the later Act dealing with the same subject.

**Conclusion.** All five may be *consulted* to resolve ambiguity, but each carries *different weight*: SOR and Minister's speech for object/mischief; dictionary for ordinary meaning (context-checked); in pari materia statutes as strong contextual aids; foreign decisions as merely persuasive. None can defeat plain statutory language.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The unifying principle is *hierarchy of authority*: the enacted text is supreme, and external aids enter only through the door of ambiguity, ranked by how reliably they reveal legislative intent. SOR and Minister's speech are close to intent (so useful for *object*, not for fixing exact scope); dictionaries risk importing a meaning alien to the context; foreign law is furthest removed (persuasive only). *In pari materia* rests on the presumption of legislative consistency — the same lawmaker on the same subject speaks with one voice. The exam trap is to treat any of these as *conclusive*; their proper role is to *confirm or clarify*, never to rewrite unambiguous words.

*(Full-marks tip: state the "only if ambiguous" gate first, then grade each aid by weight, and define in pari materia with the "consistency presumption". Treating debates or a dictionary as binding is the standard error.)*

---

### Q91. Ch: Interpretation of Statutes — "May" / "Shall" — Directory vs Mandatory (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** A statute provides: *"The assessing officer **shall** give the assessee 15 days' notice before completing an assessment,"* and elsewhere: *"The Commissioner **may** condone delay in filing a return."* In one case, the officer gave only 10 days' notice; in another, the Commissioner refused even to consider a condonation request. Examine, applying the principles that distinguish mandatory from directory provisions, whether (i) the assessment is vitiated, and (ii) the Commissioner acted lawfully.

**Answer:**
**Governing principle.** The words "shall" and "may" are *not* conclusive of whether a provision is mandatory or directory. The court looks to the *intention of the legislature* gathered from (i) the object and purpose of the provision, (ii) whether it protects a *private right* or a public/procedural convenience, and (iii) the *consequences* attached to non-compliance. A key test: if a provision is designed to protect a person and non-compliance causes *prejudice*, it is usually **mandatory**; if it is merely regulatory of procedure and no serious consequence flows, it may be **directory**. "May" is generally enabling/discretionary, but becomes *obligatory* ("shall") where a duty is coupled with a power to be exercised for the benefit of a person who has a right to its exercise.

**Application (i) — the 15-day notice ("shall").** This notice is a *safeguard of the assessee's right* to be heard (natural justice) before an adverse order. Its purpose is protective, and short notice causes real prejudice (less time to gather evidence). Hence "shall" here is **mandatory**; giving only 10 days breaches a mandatory condition and the assessment is **vitiated** (voidable at the assessee's instance on showing prejudice).

**Application (ii) — condonation ("may").** "May condone" confers a *discretion*. But discretion coupled with a duty must be *exercised*, not abdicated. The Commissioner is entitled to *refuse* condonation on merits after applying his mind, but he is **not** entitled to *refuse to consider* the request at all. By declining even to examine "sufficient cause", he failed to exercise a discretion the law required him to exercise judicially. That refusal is **unlawful**; the matter must go back for consideration on merits.

**Conclusion.** (i) The assessment is vitiated for breach of a mandatory notice. (ii) The Commissioner acted unlawfully by not applying his mind; "may" gave him discretion in outcome, not liberty to ignore the application.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The concept is that the *label* ("shall"/"may") is a starting point, not the answer — the legislature's *purpose* and the *consequence of default* decide mandatory vs directory. A provision protecting an individual's right and causing prejudice on breach is read strictly (mandatory), because to read it as directory would let officers trample the very right it guards. Conversely, "may" is discretionary, but the deeper principle is that a public authority holding a discretionary power for citizens' benefit has a *duty to consider* — non-exercise is itself an illegality. The double trap: assuming "shall" is always mandatory (not so if merely procedural) and "may" is always a free choice (not so where it must at least be considered).

*(Full-marks tip: the marks lie in the *prejudice* test for "shall" and the *duty-to-consider* point for "may". Answers that say "shall = mandatory, may = directory" mechanically are marked down.)*

---

### Q92. Ch: Interpretation of Statutes — General Clauses Act & Effect of Repeal (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Section 18 of an old Act imposed a penalty for late filing and prescribed prosecution. A new Act repeals the old Act with effect from 1 April 2026 and re-enacts the subject with a *lower* penalty. Mr. Y committed a default on 15 January 2026 (under the old Act). Proceedings were still pending on 1 April 2026. Y argues that because the old Act stands repealed, the pending prosecution lapses and no penalty survives. The Department argues the old penalty continues. Applying the General Clauses Act, 1897 and the interpretive principles on repeal, advise which penalty (if any) applies.

**Answer:**
**Governing principle — Section 6, General Clauses Act, 1897.** Unless a *different intention appears*, the repeal of an enactment does **not**: (a) revive anything not in force; (b) affect the *previous operation* of the repealed Act or anything duly done thereunder; (c) affect any *right, privilege, obligation or liability* acquired, accrued or incurred under it; (d) affect any *penalty, forfeiture or punishment* incurred in respect of any offence committed against the repealed Act; or (e) affect any *investigation, legal proceeding or remedy* in respect of such right/liability/penalty — and any such proceeding may be *continued* as if the repealing Act had not been passed. Section 6 preserves *accrued* liabilities against the effect of repeal; a "simple repeal" does not wipe out past liability.

**Application.** Y's default occurred on 15 January 2026 — *before* the repeal. By that date a *liability to penalty* had already been **incurred** under the old Act, and a legal proceeding was pending. Under Section 6(c), (d) and (e), that accrued liability and the pending prosecution *survive* the repeal and may be continued as if the old Act were still in force — *unless the repealing Act shows a different intention*. Here the new Act merely re-enacts with a lower penalty; it does not expressly wipe out past defaults. Therefore Y's liability is **not extinguished**.

**Which penalty?** The offence was *committed under the old Act*, so the penalty *incurred* is the old (higher) one, preserved by Section 6(d). The lower penalty in the new Act applies prospectively to defaults on/after 1 April 2026, unless the new Act expressly makes the reduced penalty applicable to pending cases (a "different intention"). *Caveat:* if the matter were a *criminal* penalty and a constitutional beneficial-construction argument (reduction of sentence) were available, a court might extend the lighter penalty — but on the plain General Clauses Act position, the old penalty governs.

**Conclusion.** The pending prosecution does **not** lapse; Y remains liable, and (absent a contrary intention in the new Act) at the *old* penalty rate. Advise Y that the repeal gives him no escape, though he may argue for the benefit of the reduced penalty if the new Act's language permits.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The principle is that a repeal operates *prospectively* on the *life* of the statute but does not erase rights and liabilities that had already *crystallised* — otherwise every wrongdoer could escape by waiting for the law to change. Section 6 is a statutory "savings" that presumes Parliament intends past transactions to remain governed by the old law unless it says otherwise. The student must distinguish *when the liability was incurred* (crystallisation date) from *when proceedings conclude*: liability attaches at the default date, so the old law's penalty is the one "incurred". The trap is thinking "repeal = clean slate"; Section 6 exists precisely to defeat that argument, and it yields only to an *express or implied contrary intention* in the repealing Act.

*(Full-marks tip: cite Section 6 clauses (c), (d), (e) specifically, fix the liability to the *default date*, and flag the "unless a different intention appears" rider. Omitting the crystallisation-date logic is the common lapse.)*

---

### Q93. Ch: FEMA, 1999 — Current vs Capital Account Classification (Marks: 10) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Mr. Arjun, a resident individual, undertakes the following transactions in a financial year. Classify EACH as a **current account transaction** or a **capital account transaction** under FEMA, 1999, giving the reasoning for your classification, and state whether it is *generally permissible* (subject to conditions) or *restricted/prohibited*.

| # | Transaction |
|---|-------------|
| 1 | Payment of USD 40,000 towards import of raw material for his business |
| 2 | Acquisition of a flat in London for USD 200,000 for investment |
| 3 | Repayment of interest of USD 5,000 on a foreign currency loan taken earlier |
| 4 | Gift of USD 15,000 to his son studying abroad |
| 5 | Purchase of USD 30,000 worth of shares of a foreign listed company |
| 6 | Payment of USD 3,000 as premium on a life insurance policy taken from a foreign insurer |

**Answer:**
**Governing definitions (FEMA, 1999).**
- **Sec 2(e) — Capital account transaction:** a transaction which *alters the assets or liabilities, including contingent liabilities, outside India of persons resident in India, or assets/liabilities in India of persons resident outside India* — i.e. it changes the *stock* of foreign assets/liabilities.
- **Sec 2(j) — Current account transaction:** a transaction *other than* a capital account transaction, and includes (without limitation): payments due in connection with *foreign trade, other current business, services, and short-term banking/credit facilities in the ordinary course*; *payments due as interest on loans* and as *net income from investments*; *remittances for living expenses* of family abroad; and *expenses in connection with foreign travel, education and medical care* of parents, spouse and children. The residual test: if it does not create/alter a foreign asset or liability, it is current.

**Classification of each item.**

| # | Transaction | Classification | Reasoning | Permissibility |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | USD 40,000 import payment | **Current** | Payment in connection with *foreign trade* — no foreign asset/liability created | Generally permissible (Sec 5) |
| 2 | USD 200,000 London flat (investment) | **Capital** | *Acquires an immovable asset outside India* — alters foreign assets | Permissible under LRS, but exceeds the USD 250,000 head only if aggregated; permitted subject to LRS limit |
| 3 | USD 5,000 loan interest | **Current** | *Interest on a loan* is expressly a current account transaction under Sec 2(j) | Generally permissible |
| 4 | USD 15,000 gift to son abroad | **Current** | A gift/remittance for maintenance/living of a relative is a current account transaction | Permissible within LRS limit |
| 5 | USD 30,000 foreign shares | **Capital** | Acquisition of *foreign securities* alters foreign assets | Permissible under LRS |
| 6 | USD 3,000 foreign life-insurance premium | **Current** | Payment for a *service* (insurance) in the ordinary course; no foreign asset of the nature restricted is created for the payer beyond a policy — treated as current | Generally permissible |

**Key distinction applied.** Items 2 and 5 *create/alter foreign assets* (a flat, shares) → **capital**. Items 1, 3, 4, 6 are *payments/remittances* that do not add to Arjun's stock of foreign assets/liabilities → **current**. Note item 3 is a *trap*: although the underlying *loan* was capital, the *interest* on it is expressly current under Sec 2(j).

**Conclusion.** Current: #1, #3, #4, #6. Capital: #2, #5. Capital account transactions and personal remittances by a resident individual are routed through the **Liberalised Remittance Scheme (USD 250,000 per FY)** and applicable Rules; current account transactions are freely permitted unless prohibited/restricted under the Current Account Transaction Rules.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The master key is the *balance-sheet test*: a **capital** account transaction changes the *stock* of foreign assets or liabilities (you now own a London flat or foreign shares); a **current** account transaction is a *flow* — a payment for trade, services, interest, or maintenance that does not enlarge your foreign asset/liability base. Section 2(j) is drafted as the *residual* — "anything that is not capital" — and then lists common flows to remove doubt. The classic exam traps are (a) loan *principal/creation* (capital) vs loan *interest* (current) — same loan, different limbs; and (b) treating every large remittance as capital, when a gift or education/maintenance remittance is a *current* transaction. Getting classification right matters because the *permission regime differs*: current = free (Sec 5) unless restricted; capital = only as permitted by RBI regulations/LRS.

*(Full-marks tip: give the Sec 2(e)/2(j) definitions FIRST, then a one-line "asset/liability created?" reason per item, and *flag the interest-vs-loan trap*. Answers that classify without the balance-sheet reasoning, or that miss item 3's trap, lose marks.)*

---

### Q94. Ch: FEMA, 1999 — Current Account Transaction Rules: Prohibited & Approval-based (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** A resident company/individual proposes the following remittances. For EACH, state under the *Foreign Exchange Management (Current Account Transactions) Rules, 2000* whether it is (a) **prohibited outright** (Schedule I), (b) **permitted only with Central Government approval** (Schedule II), (c) **permitted only with RBI approval beyond a limit** (Schedule III), or (d) **freely permitted**, and give the reasoning.

| # | Proposed remittance |
|---|---|
| 1 | Remittance out of lottery winnings |
| 2 | Payment of commission on exports towards equity investment in a JV/WOS abroad |
| 3 | USD 20,000 for a private visit abroad (holiday) |
| 4 | Remittance of freight of a vessel chartered by a PSU (Government undertaking) |
| 5 | Payment related to "call-back services" of telephones |
| 6 | Remittance of USD 3,00,000 by a resident individual for a close relative's maintenance abroad |

**Answer:**
**Governing framework.** Section 5 of FEMA permits current account transactions freely, *subject to* reasonable restrictions imposed by the Central Government in consultation with RBI. These are contained in the Current Account Transaction Rules, 2000:
- **Schedule I** — transactions *prohibited* absolutely.
- **Schedule II** — transactions requiring prior *Central Government (Ministry)* approval.
- **Schedule III** — transactions permitted, but requiring prior *RBI* approval where they exceed the specified monetary limits (individuals covered by the LRS ceiling).

**Classification.**

| # | Remittance | Category | Reasoning |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Lottery winnings | **(a) Prohibited — Sch I** | Remittance out of lottery winnings is expressly banned; the law discourages export of speculative/gambling gains |
| 2 | Commission on exports toward equity in JV/WOS abroad | **(a) Prohibited — Sch I** | Commission on exports towards equity investment in JV/WOS abroad (beyond the permitted %) is prohibited to prevent disguised capital export |
| 3 | USD 20,000 private visit | **(d) Freely permitted (within LRS)** | Private travel is a current account transaction; within the LRS/limit it needs no approval |
| 4 | Freight of vessel chartered by a PSU | **(b) Central Government approval — Sch II** | Chartering by a Government undertaking engages Sch II Ministry approval |
| 5 | Payment for "call-back services" of telephones | **(a) Prohibited — Sch I** | Payment related to call-back telephone services is expressly prohibited |
| 6 | USD 3,00,000 for close relative's maintenance | **(c) RBI approval beyond limit — Sch III** | Maintenance of a close relative is a current transaction but exceeds the LRS/specified ceiling (USD 250,000), so the excess needs prior RBI approval |

**Conclusion.** Prohibited (Sch I): #1, #2, #5. Central Government approval (Sch II): #4. RBI approval beyond limit (Sch III): #6. Freely permitted: #3.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The architecture to internalise is a *three-gate filter* on current account transactions: Schedule I is the "never" list (socially/economically undesirable outflows — gambling, disguised capital export, banned services); Schedule II is "government-clearance" (transactions with policy/PSU sensitivity); Schedule III is "quantitative control" (otherwise-fine transactions that become RBI-approval-worthy only *above a threshold*). Everything else flows freely under Section 5. The exam trap is to treat a transaction as prohibited when it is merely *limit-controlled* (item 6 is *not* banned — only the amount above the LRS ceiling needs RBI nod), or to think approval is needed for ordinary travel (item 3 is free within limits). Correctly placing a transaction in the right Schedule is what determines whether it needs *no approval*, *Government approval*, *RBI approval*, or is *forbidden*.

*(Full-marks tip: map each item to Schedule I/II/III or "free" and *justify with the policy reason*. The high-value discriminators are #2 (disguised capital export → prohibited) and #6 (limit-based RBI approval, not prohibition). Blanket-labelling everything "needs RBI approval" is the common failure.)*

---

### Q95. Ch: FEMA, 1999 — Residential Status under Sec 2(v) (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Determine the residential status under **Section 2(v) of FEMA, 1999** for each of the following for the financial year 2026-27, with reasoning:

| # | Person and facts |
|---|---|
| 1 | Mr. Ramesh, an Indian citizen, left India on 10 May 2026 to take up employment in Dubai (intends to stay indefinitely) |
| 2 | Ms. Lisa, a UK citizen, came to India on 1 June 2026 to start a business she intends to run for 3 years |
| 3 | Mr. Ken, a foreign national, came to India on 15 April 2026 only for a 4-month tourist trip |
| 4 | A company incorporated in India with its entire business carried on in Singapore |
| 5 | Mr. Iqbal, who was in India for 200 days in FY 2025-26, went abroad on 1 April 2026 for a medical treatment expected to last 2 months |

**Answer:**
**Governing definition — Sec 2(v), "person resident in India".** A person is resident in India if he/she resided in India for **more than 182 days during the *preceding* financial year**, **BUT** this is *subject to exceptions* — a person is **NOT** resident if he goes out of / stays outside India for the purpose of (a) taking up employment, (b) carrying on business/vocation, or (c) any other purpose indicating an intention to stay outside India for an *uncertain period*. Conversely, a person who *comes to / stays in* India is resident **only** if he comes for or intends employment, business/vocation, or a stay for an uncertain period; a person coming for a *definite short purpose* (tourism, medical, temporary) does *not* become resident merely by the day-count. **Note:** FEMA residence is *purpose-driven*, not purely day-count driven (unlike Income-tax).

**Also — Sec 2(u)/2(v):** any *person or body corporate registered or incorporated in India* is resident in India; an office/branch/agency in India owned/controlled by a non-resident is resident, and vice versa.

**Determination.**

| # | Status FY 2026-27 | Reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Non-resident** | Left India *for employment* abroad with intent to stay indefinitely → falls in the exception; day-count in prior year is overridden by the *purpose* (employment) test |
| 2 | **Resident** | Came to India to *carry on business* for an uncertain/extended period (3 years) → satisfies the "comes for business/uncertain period" limb; becomes resident from arrival despite being a UK citizen |
| 3 | **Non-resident** | Came only for a *4-month tourist trip* — a definite, temporary purpose, not employment/business/uncertain stay → does not become resident |
| 4 | **Resident** | A company *incorporated in India* is resident in India regardless of where its business is actually carried on — incorporation in India is decisive |
| 5 | **Resident** | Was in India > 182 days in the *preceding* FY (200 days), and going abroad for a *definite short purpose* (2-month medical treatment) does **not** fall in the "uncertain period/employment/business" exception → he *remains resident* |

**Conclusion.** Non-resident: #1, #3. Resident: #2, #4, #5.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** FEMA residence hinges on *purpose and intention*, using the 182-day prior-year test only as a *baseline* that the purpose-exceptions can override. The logic is that FEMA regulates the *economic centre of a person's activity*: someone who leaves for employment/business/indefinite stay has shifted that centre out of India (non-resident) *from the day he leaves*, even if he was here >182 days before; someone who comes to India to work/do business shifts it in (resident) *from arrival*, even as a foreigner. The two big traps: (i) applying the Income-tax day-count mechanically — FEMA lets a *definite short purpose* (tourism/medical) preserve the earlier status (so Ken stays non-resident and Iqbal stays resident); and (ii) forgetting that *incorporation in India* fixes a company as resident irrespective of where it trades. Citizenship is irrelevant throughout.

*(Full-marks tip: lead with "purpose overrides day-count" and apply the employment/business/uncertain-period test to each person. Marks are lost for using Income-tax rules, or for treating Iqbal's short medical trip as making him non-resident.)*

---

### Q96. Ch: FEMA, 1999 — Liberalised Remittance Scheme (LRS) (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Mr. Sanjay, a resident individual, has *already* remitted USD 2,00,000 during FY 2026-27 (USD 1,00,000 for his daughter's overseas tuition and USD 1,00,000 to buy foreign equity shares). He now wishes to: (i) remit a further USD 80,000 to purchase a small overseas property; and (ii) separately gift USD 40,000 to his brother who is a resident. Advise on the permissibility of the fresh transactions under the **Liberalised Remittance Scheme**, with reasoning.

**Answer:**
**Governing principle — LRS.** Under the Liberalised Remittance Scheme (framed under FEMA), a *resident individual* may remit **up to USD 2,50,000 per financial year** for *any permitted current or capital account transaction, or a combination* — including overseas education, travel, gifts to non-residents, maintenance of relatives abroad, purchase of foreign shares/property, etc. Key features: (a) the ceiling is *aggregate* across all eligible transactions in the FY; (b) it is *per individual* (family members can pool only for *their own* limits, and only for permitted purposes); (c) it does **not** cover transactions that are otherwise *prohibited* (e.g. remittances to FATF non-cooperative jurisdictions, margin trading, lottery, etc.); and (d) the scheme is available only to *resident individuals*, and a *gift within India to another resident is not a foreign-exchange remittance at all*.

**Application.**
- *Already used:* USD 2,00,000 (tuition USD 1,00,000 + foreign shares USD 1,00,000). Both are LRS-eligible, so USD 2,00,000 of the USD 2,50,000 ceiling is consumed. **Balance available = USD 50,000.**
- *(i) USD 80,000 overseas property:* Purchase of overseas property is an eligible LRS *capital* transaction, but USD 80,000 *exceeds* the remaining USD 50,000 limit. He may remit **only USD 50,000** this FY under LRS; the balance USD 30,000 must wait for the next FY or requires specific RBI approval. So the full USD 80,000 is **not permissible now**.
- *(ii) USD 40,000 gift to his brother, a resident, in India:* This is **not a cross-border remittance of foreign exchange at all** — a rupee gift between two residents in India is outside FEMA's LRS. If, however, the "gift" were a *remittance of foreign exchange abroad* to the brother, it would count against the LRS limit; but a domestic gift to a resident does **not** consume the LRS ceiling.

**Conclusion.** (i) Sanjay can remit only USD 50,000 (not USD 80,000) toward the property this FY; the excess must await the next year or RBI approval. (ii) A domestic gift to a resident brother is outside LRS and freely permitted.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The core concept is that LRS is a *single aggregate annual wallet* (USD 250,000) that covers *both* current and capital eligible transactions for a resident individual — you cannot get a fresh USD 250,000 for each *purpose*. So education, shares and property all draw from the *same* pool, and once USD 200,000 is spent only USD 50,000 remains. The trap in (i) is thinking a *new type* of transaction resets the limit — it does not. The trap in (ii) is treating a *domestic* rupee gift as an LRS item — LRS regulates *foreign-exchange remittances*, not resident-to-resident transfers within India, so it neither needs LRS room nor is restricted by it. Understanding *what draws down the wallet and what does not* is the whole point.

*(Full-marks tip: compute the *remaining* limit (250,000 − 200,000 = 50,000) explicitly, cap the property remittance at 50,000, and correctly exclude the domestic gift from LRS. Candidates who give a fresh 250,000 for the property, or count the resident-to-resident gift, lose marks.)*

---

### Q97. Ch: FEMA, 1999 — Export of Goods & Services: Realisation and Sec 8 (Marks: 8) [Case/Application]

**Question:** M/s Global Exports Ltd. exported garments worth USD 5,00,000 on 1 April 2026. As on the due date, only USD 3,00,000 has been realised. The company (i) wants to *write off* USD 50,000 as the buyer disputes quality; (ii) has *not* realised the remaining USD 1,50,000 within the prescribed period; and (iii) received an *advance* of USD 20,000 against a *future* export that it now cannot fulfil and wishes to refund. Examine the obligations and permissibility under **Section 7 and Section 8 of FEMA, 1999** and related regulations, with reasoning.

**Answer:**
**Governing principle.**
- **Sec 7 (Export of goods and services):** every exporter of goods/services must furnish to the RBI (or prescribed authority) a *declaration* with true and correct material particulars, including the *full export value* (or, if not ascertainable, the value the exporter *expects* to receive), and must comply with directions to ensure *realisation of export proceeds*.
- **Sec 8 (Realisation and repatriation of foreign exchange):** where any amount of foreign exchange is *due or has accrued* to a person resident in India, that person must take *all reasonable steps to realise and repatriate* it to India within the period and manner specified by RBI. This is a *positive statutory duty*, not optional.
- **Regulations:** export proceeds must be realised within the *prescribed period* (generally *nine months* from date of export, subject to RBI notifications); *write-off* of unrealised export bills is permitted only up to *specified percentage limits* / with AD bank or RBI approval; *advances* against exports carry a duty to complete the export within the stipulated period or refund/repatriate.

**Application.**
- **(i) Write-off of USD 50,000 (quality dispute):** Permissible only within the *self-write-off/ AD-bank limits* prescribed by RBI (a specified % of export proceeds), and subject to conditions (surrender of proportionate export incentives, documentary proof of the dispute). If within the eligible percentage, the AD bank may allow it; if beyond, prior RBI approval is required. It is *not* an unconditional right.
- **(ii) Unrealised USD 1,50,000 beyond the prescribed period:** The company is under a *continuing Sec 8 duty* to take all reasonable steps to realise and repatriate. Mere non-realisation is a *contravention* unless it has (a) diligently pursued the buyer, and (b) obtained *extension of time* from the AD bank/RBI. It must apply for extension or realise; failing which, it exposes itself to penal action under FEMA.
- **(iii) Advance of USD 20,000 for a failed export:** Advance received against exports must be used to make the shipment within the stipulated period. As the export cannot be fulfilled, the company must *refund/repatriate* the advance to the overseas buyer with AD-bank approval (and cannot simply retain it); retaining an advance without performing the export contravenes the export-advance regulations.

**Conclusion.** (i) Write-off allowed only within prescribed limits/approval; (ii) the USD 1,50,000 must be realised or time extended — inaction is a contravention of Sec 8; (iii) the USD 20,000 advance must be refunded to the buyer, not retained.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The animating idea behind Sections 7–8 is *conservation of the country's foreign exchange*: an export is not "complete" for FEMA until the *proceeds actually come home*. So the exporter carries an ongoing, affirmative duty to *chase and repatriate* — the law treats unrealised export value as national forex temporarily parked abroad. That is why (a) you cannot freely write off a receivable (it would let exporters quietly forgo forex the nation is owed), (b) non-realisation is presumptively a contravention unless time is formally extended, and (c) an advance for a cancelled export cannot be pocketed — it is either converted into an export or sent back. The trap is treating realisation as a commercial choice; under FEMA it is a *statutory obligation* backed by penalties.

*(Full-marks tip: cite Sec 7 (declaration) and Sec 8 (duty to realise & repatriate) separately, and stress that write-off and time-extension are *permission-based, limit-bound*, not automatic. Missing the "continuing duty / contravention" point on the unrealised sum costs marks.)*

---

### Q98. Ch: FEMA, 1999 — Contravention, Compounding & Penalty (Sec 13–15) (Marks: 6) [Case/Application]

**Question:** M/s Nova Ltd. contravened FEMA by acquiring foreign securities of USD 1,00,000 without the required approval. The amount involved is *quantifiable*. The company approaches you to (i) quantify the maximum penalty exposure, (ii) advise whether the contravention can be *compounded*, and (iii) explain what happens if the penalty is not paid. Address each with reference to **Sections 13, 14 and 15 of FEMA, 1999**.

**Answer:**
**Governing provisions.**
- **Sec 13 (Penalties):** on any contravention of FEMA/rules/regulations, the person is liable to a penalty **up to *thrice the sum involved* where the amount is quantifiable**, or **up to USD... (₹2 lakh) where not quantifiable**; and where the contravention is *continuing*, a *further penalty up to ₹5,000 per day* during which it continues. The Adjudicating Authority may also order *confiscation* of the currency/security/property involved.
- **Sec 15 (Compounding):** any contravention under Sec 13 **may be compounded** on an application by the person, by the prescribed authority (RBI / Directorate of Enforcement), *within 180 days* of receipt of the application. Once a contravention is compounded, **no further proceedings** shall be initiated for that contravention.
- **Sec 14 (Recovery / enforcement of penalty):** if the penalty is *not paid* within 90 days of the notice of demand, the defaulter is liable to *civil imprisonment* (and the penalty is recoverable), following the procedure for enforcement of the Adjudicating Authority's order.

**Application.**
- **(i) Maximum penalty:** The sum involved is USD 1,00,000 (quantifiable). Maximum penalty = **up to 3 × USD 1,00,000 = USD 3,00,000** (equivalent in rupees). If continuing, add up to ₹5,000/day for the continuing period. Confiscation of the improperly acquired securities may also be ordered.
- **(ii) Compounding:** Yes. As this is a contravention *under Sec 13* (and not, say, a Sec 3(a) hawala-type offence excluded from compounding), Nova Ltd. may apply for **compounding under Sec 15** to the RBI/Enforcement Directorate, which must dispose of it within 180 days. Compounding closes the matter with no further proceedings — the prudent course to limit exposure and reputational risk.
- **(iii) Non-payment:** If the penalty imposed is not paid within *ninety days* of the demand notice, the person is liable to **civil imprisonment** under Sec 14 (simple/until payment, per the amount), and the penalty is enforced/recovered accordingly.

**Conclusion.** Maximum penalty is up to thrice USD 1,00,000 (plus continuing penalty/confiscation); the contravention is *compoundable* under Sec 15 (advisable); non-payment invites civil imprisonment and recovery under Sec 14.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** FEMA is a *civil/regulatory* law (post-1999), so its enforcement philosophy is *monetary deterrence plus a settlement window*, not criminal prosecution for ordinary breaches. That is why the penalty is pegged to a *multiple of the sum involved* (3×) — the deterrent scales with the forex at stake — and why *compounding* exists: to let a genuine, non-serious contravener regularise voluntarily, pay a reduced sum, and close the file, conserving enforcement resources. The student should see the *incentive structure*: face up to 3× exposure and confiscation if you litigate, or compound early and cap the damage. Civil imprisonment under Sec 14 is the *backstop* that gives the monetary penalty teeth — it is not punishment for the contravention itself but for *non-payment* of the adjudicated penalty. The trap is assuming FEMA breaches are criminal; only the recovery-default stage engages imprisonment.

*(Full-marks tip: state the "thrice the sum, quantifiable" rule and the *180-day* compounding window and *90-day / civil imprisonment* recovery rule with the right section numbers. Confusing FEMA (civil, compoundable) with FERA/criminal is the classic error.)*

### Q99. Ch: Interpretation of Statutes — Beneficial (Welfare) Construction & Casus Omissus (Marks: 5) [Case/Application]

**Question:** A welfare statute grants gratuity to *"every employee"* on termination. The definition of "employee" in the Act omits any mention of a *teacher*. A teacher, on retirement, claims gratuity. The employer argues that since the legislature *omitted* teachers, the court cannot supply the omission. Applying the doctrines of *beneficial construction* and *casus omissus*, and the interplay between them, advise.

**Answer:**
**Governing principles.**
- **Beneficial (welfare) construction:** a *social-welfare / beneficial* statute must be construed *liberally* in favour of the class it is designed to benefit; where two views are possible, the one that *advances the object* and confers the benefit is preferred, and doubts are resolved in favour of the beneficiary.
- **Casus omissus ("a case omitted"):** a matter which *should have been, but was not, provided for* in a statute cannot ordinarily be supplied by the courts, because "the court cannot legislate" — it must not read words into a statute that Parliament did not enact. This rule is applied *cautiously* and *unwillingly*.

**The interplay.** The two doctrines pull in opposite directions and must be *balanced*: beneficial construction *stretches* the meaning of existing words to cover the beneficiary; casus omissus *forbids inventing* words to fill a true gap. The reconciling test: a court may *interpret existing words liberally* to include a claimant, but it may *not* create an entirely new provision. If "employee" is defined widely enough (e.g. "any person employed for wages to do any work") to *linguistically accommodate* a teacher, beneficial construction includes the teacher — there is no true casus omissus, only a doubtful word resolved in the beneficiary's favour. If, however, the Act *expressly and deliberately* excludes teachers, that is a genuine legislative choice the court cannot override — supplying it would be legislating.

**Application.** Assuming the definition of "employee" is a *general wage-earner definition* that does not expressly exclude teachers, the mere *silence* about teachers is not a deliberate exclusion. Being a welfare statute, "employee" is read liberally to include a teacher who otherwise fits the wage-employment description. This is *interpretation*, not filling a casus omissus. Advise: **the teacher is entitled to gratuity**, unless the statute *expressly* excludes teachers, in which case the omission is deliberate and the court cannot supply it.

**Conclusion.** Beneficial construction prevails over the employer's casus-omissus argument where the omission is mere silence; it yields only to an *express* legislative exclusion.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** The concept is the boundary between *interpreting* and *legislating*. Welfare law carries a thumb on the scale for the weaker party, so ambiguous words are read generously to fulfil the statute's protective purpose. But that thumb cannot rewrite the statute — casus omissus guards against judges inventing coverage Parliament chose to withhold. The decisive question is therefore *"is this silence or is this deliberate exclusion?"* Silence in a beneficial statute is filled by liberal reading of existing words (no true omission); an *express* exclusion is a policy choice courts must respect. The trap is to treat every omission as an invitation to either always include (over-reaching) or always exclude (defeating the welfare object); the correct path distinguishes the two.

*(Full-marks tip: the marks are in *reconciling* the two doctrines via the "silence vs express exclusion" test, not in defining them separately. State that beneficial construction stretches *existing words* but cannot *supply new ones*.)*

---

### Q100. Ch: FEMA, 1999 + Interpretation — Integrated: "Person Resident" & Non-obstante Construction (Marks: 10) [Case/Application]

**Question:** Section X of a FEMA regulation provides: *"A person resident in India may acquire immovable property outside India... provided that this shall not apply to property acquired by way of inheritance from a person resident outside India."* A separate provision states: *"**Notwithstanding anything contained in this regulation**, a person resident in India may hold, own, transfer or invest in foreign currency, security or immovable property situated outside India if such property was acquired when he was resident outside India."* 

Mr. Verma, who lived and worked in the USA for 12 years (and was then a person resident *outside* India), bought a house in New York in 2019. He returned permanently to India on 1 January 2026 and is now a person resident in India. In FY 2026-27 he (i) continues to *hold* the New York house, and (ii) *inherits* a London flat from his late father who was resident in the UK. The Department alleges both holdings contravene the LRS ceiling and the proviso to Section X. Applying FEMA and the rules of interpretation (proviso and non-obstante clause), examine the validity of Verma's holdings.

**Answer:**
**Governing principles.**
- **FEMA — Sec 6(4) (and cognate regulations):** a person resident in India may *hold, own, transfer or invest in* foreign currency, foreign security, or immovable property *situated outside India* **if** such currency/security/property was *acquired, held or owned by him when he was resident outside India* or *inherited from a person resident outside India*. The purpose is to protect *legitimately-acquired* foreign assets of returning residents.
- **Interpretation — proviso:** a proviso carves an exception out of, and is read with, its *own* main clause; it limits the main clause and does not travel beyond it.
- **Interpretation — non-obstante clause:** "Notwithstanding anything contained in this regulation" *overrides* the rest of the regulation to the extent of repugnancy — where it conflicts with the general rule (and the proviso to Section X), the non-obstante provision *prevails*.

**Application.**
- **(i) The New York house.** Verma acquired it in 2019 *while he was a person resident outside India*. The *non-obstante* provision squarely applies: "Notwithstanding anything in this regulation, a person resident in India may hold... immovable property outside India if acquired when he was resident outside India." Because of the *overriding* effect, this provision *prevails over* the general prohibition/LRS ceiling in Section X. The LRS ceiling is *irrelevant* — LRS governs *fresh remittances* to *acquire* assets, not the *continued holding* of assets lawfully acquired abroad while a non-resident. **Holding the New York house is valid.**
- **(ii) The London flat (inheritance).** The *proviso* to Section X expressly says the LRS-type restriction "*shall not apply to property acquired by way of inheritance from a person resident outside India*." Verma inherited from his father, who was resident in the UK (outside India). The proviso *carves this out* of the restriction. Additionally, Sec 6(4)/regulations permit holding property *inherited from a person resident outside India*. **The inheritance is valid** and outside the LRS ceiling.

**Interpretive resolution.** The Department's error is to apply the *general rule / LRS ceiling* while ignoring (a) the *proviso* (which exempts inheritance from a non-resident) and (b) the *non-obstante* clause (which overrides the general rule for assets acquired while non-resident). Read correctly: the proviso *subtracts inheritance inward* from Section X, and the non-obstante clause *overrides outward* for pre-return acquisitions. Both of Verma's holdings fall within these carve-outs.

**Conclusion.** Both holdings are **valid**. The New York house is protected by the *non-obstante* provision (acquired while resident outside India); the London flat is protected by the *proviso* (inheritance from a person resident outside India). Neither engages the LRS ceiling, which controls *fresh outward remittances*, not the *retention* of legitimately-acquired foreign assets.

**Why this way (the reasoning):** This integrates the FEMA policy of *not penalising returning residents* with two interpretive tools. The policy logic: FEMA restricts *new export of capital*, but a returning Indian should be free to *keep* what he lawfully built up abroad — so Sec 6(4) shields assets acquired while non-resident and inheritances from non-residents. The *interpretation* logic makes the answer airtight: the **proviso** operates *inward* on Section X, pulling inheritance out of the restriction; the **non-obstante clause** operates *outward*, defeating the general rule for pre-return acquisitions. The Department's mistake is a textbook *failure to read the statute as a whole* — applying the charging/general rule while ignoring an overriding clause and a proviso. The decisive concept the student must carry away: **LRS controls fresh remittances to acquire; it does not tax the mere holding of assets already lawfully acquired abroad** — and a non-obstante clause always wins over the provision it overrides.

*(Full-marks tip: the top marks require *both* the FEMA point (Sec 6(4): assets acquired while non-resident / inherited from a non-resident are protected) *and* the interpretation point (proviso subtracts inward, non-obstante overrides outward), plus the clean distinction that LRS governs *acquisition remittances*, not *retention*. Answers that apply the LRS ceiling to the holdings miss the entire question.)*
